1     Model and standing assumptions
We study the hyperbolic Anderson model [4, (1.1)]
       ∂ 2u                                                         ∂u
            (t, x) = ∆u(t, x) + Ẇ (t, x)u(t, x),    u(0, x) = 1,      (0, x) = 0,       (1.1)
       ∂t2                                                          ∂t
for (t, x) ∈ R+ × Rd , d ∈ {1, 2, 3}, interpreted in the Stratonovich sense (limit of classical
wave equations driven by smoothed noises Ẇϵ,δ ). The noise is centered Gaussian with
              Cov Ẇ (s, x), Ẇ (t, y) = |s − t|−α0 γ(x − y),
                   (︁                   )︁
                                                                   0 < α0 < 1,            (1.2)
where γ ≥ 0 is nonnegative definite with spectral measure µ.
Hypothesis 1.1 (Homogeneous covariance). Let d ∈ {1, 2, 3} and 0 < α0 < 1. The
spatial covariance is a nonnegative, even, positive-definite tempered Radon measure Γ
on Rd , with positive even tempered Radon spectral measure µ in the convention
                    ∫︂
               Γ = eiξ·x µ( dξ) in the sense of tempered distributions.

The symbol γ in (1.2) denotes this covariance as a function when one exists, and as a
distribution otherwise. For every Borel set A and c > 0, Γ(cA) = cd−α Γ(A), and
                                          α0 + α < 2,                                    (1.3)
with 0 < α < d, or α = d = 1. In particular, the last case includes spatial white noise
Γ = δ0 .

                                                5
Main result and dependencies. Theorem 40.6 identifies the original point-moment
limit for every covariance in Hypothesis 1.1. Its full variational domain and normal-
ization are displayed in (40.9). Theorem 41.4 and Corollary 41.5 give derived bounds;
Section 42 evaluates the white spatial energy and its resulting moment bounds. These
bounds need not be attained at the moment constant.
    The independent earlier Theorem 13.8 establishes finite upper growth at scale tκ
and precedes its uses proving variational finiteness. It uses the fixed-order construction
proved in Lemma 3.6, the Gaussian and complex phase estimates in Sections 11–12,
and the interval coefficient and inventory estimates in Section 13. It does not use the
alternative covariance interpolation arguments in Sections 6–10 as inputs. Its uniform
geometric tail is proved here from scaled finite-model moments; the older mixed-series
and crude estimates in Section 3 are not needed for that moment-limit passage. The
separate verification of the mild equation is proved in Proposition 3.9, using the corrected
vertexwise bound of Lemma 3.8. The alternative arguments are retained to record their
separate hypotheses and limitations. Section 5 also gives explicit constants for α ≤ 1;
the full-class theorem asserts a finite uniform constant, without giving a numerically
optimized value. The later proof constructs the actual operator and its retaining sources
in Sections 19–22, then proves the same-solution lower bound and positive-control scores
in Sections 25–26. The actual Gaussian upper argument culminates in Sections 37–38.
Finite Gram optimization and its physical application then match the upper and lower
values in Section 40. This dependency order does not use that final identification to
prove its own finiteness.

Notation and source-dependent statements. The target spatial degree is α,
whereas χ denotes the degree of an auxiliary static covariance in Sections 7–10. The
coefficient power is β = 4 − α − α0 ; the factorial power in Section 13 is ν = 3 − α.
The local exponent b = 2 − α in Section 5 is different from coordinate powers bj in
Section 9. In Section 6, δ = α − 1 is an exponent; when used for temporal noise
smoothing, δ is a cutoff width. The covariance measure Γ( dx) and the Euler Gamma
function Γ(q) are distinguished by their arguments. The local p = 2 − α0 in Section 13
is an inventory-decay exponent; it is not the moment order p in Conjecture 2.3. The
unconditional first-to-second moment comparison is Proposition 2.4. Any numerical
consequence involving the source’s L∗ explicitly assumes a valid first-moment lower
bound with that constant. No correction of its printed normalization is adopted. When
a radius is given for topological support, the closed ball is meant unless a strict open
inclusion is specified. In particular the spatial cutoffs have support in B 2R and the wave
Green measures have support in B t , including the sphere measure in dimension three.
Plateau and causal-cone arguments retain their strict margins.

Which source statements are inputs. The moment estimate uses the bounded-
operator Crouzeix–Palencia theorem in (12.1). The fixed-order construction is supplied
here by Lemmas 3.5–3.6, using only the source’s universal pairing inequality [4, The-
orem 3.1, (3.6), pp. 14–15] and the direct norm bounds in Lemma 3.4. Thus neither


                                             6
Theorem 4.1 nor Lemma 4.4 nor Appendix A.1 of the source is an analytic construc-
tion input. The finite-width Gaussian field and multiple Stratonovich integral use its
definitions and regularization conventions, with Green smoothing removed before noise
smoothing. The three construction lemmas and their changed consumers passed a fresh
scoped Opus5.5/HIGH review at source revision f9303ed. This is separate from the
earlier quantitative reviews and from the pending whole-paper cold read.
    Proposition 3.9 proves that the resulting series satisfies its onefold Stratonovich mild
equation, using the corrected vertexwise bound. It does not import the author’s Sec-
tion 5(ii) argument or (5.3)–(5.4). No uniqueness assertion is made. The uniform tail
passage for the target-scale moment estimate is proved in Lemma 13.7, independently
of the crude bound and the source’s series estimate. The mixed-width and order-limit
passages for the mild equation are supplied here as well.
    The first-moment formulas in Theorem 2.1 are quoted source statements, with the
printed-normalization qualification there; they are not premises of the full upper theo-
rem. Chen–Hu’s static coefficient estimates and Chen’s 2023 variational finiteness the-
orem are used only in the explicitly identified auxiliary sections. In particular, our uses
of the latter cite Theorem 1.1, not Corollary 1.2. Thus neither a disputed first-moment
constant nor an auxiliary static coefficient theorem enters the interval-field upper bound.

mitted to the Annals of Applied Probability”, SHA-256 5f7385ca...); see

and must not be used as the authority for it.

2.1       Stratonovich expansion and moments
The solution is the Stratonovich expansion [4, (2.13)–(2.16)]
                                                       ∞
                                                      ∑︂      (︁           )︁
                                     u(t, x) =              Sn gn (·, t, x) ,
                                                      n=0
                                                          n
                                                      (︂ ∏︂                                )︂                          (2.1)
    gn (s1 , . . . , sn , x1 , . . . , xn , t, x) =           G(sl+1 − sl , xl+1 − xl ) 1[0,t]n< (s1 , . . . , sn ),
                                                        l=1

with sn+1 = t, xn+1 = x, G the fundamental solution of the wave equation, and [0, t]n< =
{0 < s1 < · · · < sn < t}. Odd expected Stratonovich integrals vanish and even ones
are given by Wick’s formula [4, (2.19)–(2.21)], a sum over pair partitions D ∈ Πn of
{1, . . . , 2n} that includes self-pairings within one chain. The second moment of one
ordered term is the two-chain pairing integral, stated in [4, Theorem 4.1, (4.3)] and

proved here under Hypothesis 1.1 in Proposition 3.1:

                              (j,k)∈D
                                                                                                           (2.2)

with I1 = {1, . . . , n} and I2 = {n + 1, . . . , 2n}. Here p(l) is the preceding label in its own
chain; at the first vertex of either chain use the common root (sp(l) , xp(l) ) = (0, 0). Thus,
the second chain does not continue the first one. This spells out the source’s conventions
in Theorem 4.1.
   The full mixed-term expansion is justified in Proposition 3.1.

2.2     The universal two-chain bound
[4, Theorem 3.1, (3.6), p. 15] bounds exactly such two-chain pairing integrals with general
                                                                         (1)     (2)
nonnegative kernels Gl by products of the norms ∥Gl ∥(0) , ∥Gl ∥j,k , ∥Gl ∥j,k of [4, (3.3)–
(3.5)], over a partition {Q0 , Q1 , Q2 } of {1, . . . , 2n} with #Q0 = #Q1 and #Q2 even. As
stated, the theorem uses finite spatial spectral       measures µj,k with nonnegative covariance
functions and temporal kernels Γ(α0 )−1 Aj,k e−λ|r| λα0 −1 dλ for measurable Aj,k ⊂ R+ .
                                              ∫︁

The application to the full covariance therefore requires regularization; this is carried
out in Lemma 3.7. The theorem includes cross-pairings between the two chains. In the
source it is used only for Stratonovich integrability (Section 4) and, via Lemma A.1 with
Gl (t, x) = e−θt G(t, x), in Laplace variables.

2.3     Known moment bounds

  The following statement records the source as printed. Its variational constants
  require normalization clarification; see Remark 9.8. We retain the printed formulas
  without adopting a correction. The upper-bound proofs below do not use the first-
  moment upper constant.

Proposition 2.4 (Jensen lower bound). Under Hypothesis 1.1, Eu(t, x) ≥ 1 and
Eu2 (t, x) ≥ (Eu(t, x))2 . Consequently, for every real ℓ,
               lim inf t−κ log Eu(t, x) ≥ ℓ   =⇒     lim inf t−κ log Eu2 (t, x) ≥ 2ℓ.
                t→∞                                    t→∞
The specialization ℓ = L∗ is conditional on the intended first-moment lower bound with
that constant. It is not an unconditional adoption of the printed normalization in The-
orem 2.1.
                                                        ∑︁
Proof. For each fixed t, x, Lemma 3.8 below   ∑︁  gives    n ∥Xn (t, x)∥2 < ∞; its proof does
not use the present proposition. Thus,           n E|Xn (t, x)| < ∞, and the mean of the
canonical
∑︁         series is the sum of its means. By (2.1) and [4, (2.20)–(2.21)], Eu(t, x) =
   n≥0 ES 2n (g2n (·, t, x)) ≥ 1, since every term is a pairing integral of a nonnegative
integrand and S0 = 1. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Eu2 (t, x) ≥ (Eu(t, x))2 ,
so t−κ log Eu2 (t, x) ≥ 2 t−κ log Eu(t, x). Taking lower limits proves the implication for
every valid ℓ. Substituting L∗ requires its stated first-moment premise. This proves
Proposition 2.4.


3     Gate G0: cross terms and the loss in the norm
      bound
This section records a diagnosis of the proposed route, not a proof of Goal 43.1. Put
Xn (t, x) = Sn (gn (·, t, x)), with X0 = 1, and
                                                           (2k)!
                         β = 4 − α − α0 > 2,         Pk = #Πk =  .
                                                           2k k!
The count follows by pairing the smallest remaining label with one of 2k − 1 labels:
P0 = 1 and Pk = (2k − 1)Pk−1 , which gives the displayed formula by induction. All
constants below may depend on the fixed covariance and its parameters, but not on the
orders or on t.

                                                 9
3.1    The complete two-chain expansion
For m + n = 2k, label the two chains by I1 = {1, . . . , m} and I2 = {m + 1, . . . , m + n}.
A self-pairing has both endpoints in one chain; a cross-pairing has one endpoint in each
chain. Both are included in Πk . Let p(l) denote the preceding vertex in its own chain;
at each first vertex set (sp(l) , yp(l) ) = (0, 0). Thus, the second chain has its own root,
rather than the last vertex of the first chain. For D ∈ Πk , set
                         ∫︂                     ∫︂                  2 ∏︂
                                                                   ∏︂
          Jm,n,D (t) =                     ds                 dy              G(sl − sp(l) , yl − yp(l) )
                          [0,t]m       n
                               < ×[0,t]<         (Rd )m+n          ρ=1 l∈Iρ
                                                ∏︂                                                          (3.1)
                                                                    −α0
                                           ×             |sj − sh |       γ(yj − yh ).
                                               (j,h)∈D


An empty chain contributes the factor one. For d = 3, the spatial Green kernels are
measures; for spatial white noise the covariance is a delta measure. Integrals in (3.1)
use the generalized-kernel and regularization conventions of the source, not pointwise
values of these measures. Lemmas 3.5–3.6 construct them with the convention of [4,
Remark 2.3, p. 11, and the Green regularization on pp. 13–14].

Proposition 3.1 (Mixed terms). Under Hypothesis 1.1,
                                     ⎧ ∑︂
                                     ⎨     Jm,n,D (t), m + n = 2k,
              E[Xm (t, x)Xn (t, x)] = D∈Πk                                                                  (3.2)
                                       0,              m + n odd,
                                     ⎩

where the case m = n = 0 equals one. In particular,
                                                 ∞ ∑︂
                                                ∑︂ 2k ∑︂
                                2
                              Eu (t, x) =                          Jm,2k−m,D (t).                           (3.3)
                                                k=0 m=0 D∈Πk

This is a convergent series of nonnegative terms.

Proof. Lemma 3.6 below constructs each Xn and each individual two-chain diagram at
arbitrary vertexwise widths, including unequal and empty chains. Its proof uses the
positive spatial-measure limit and the imported universal Theorem 3.1; it uses neither
the present proposition nor any infinite solution series.
    At positive noise widths Wick’s formula gives zero at odd total order and the finite
pairing sum at even total order. The Green-width limit is taken first, as in the construc-
tion preceding Lemma 3.5. At fixed m, n, joint L2 convergence of the two factors gives
product convergence in L1 :

          ∥Ym Yn − Xm Xn ∥1 ≤ ∥Ym − Xm ∥2 ∥Yn ∥2 + ∥Xm ∥2 ∥Yn − Xn ∥2 −→ 0.

A zero-order factor stays equal to one. Each individual Wick diagram also has the finite
nonnegative limit in Lemma 3.6.

                                                         10
   To identify its initial-chain form, for terminal variables in the first chain set

                 s′j = t − sm+1−j ,      yj′ = x − ym+1−j ,          1 ≤ j ≤ m,

and use the corresponding length-n transformation on the second chain. Each trans-
formed chain has initial root (0, 0). For example, G(t − sm , x − ym ) becomes G(s′1 , y1′ );
every other factor becomes the corresponding successive increment. The differences in
each covariance change sign, absorbed by evenness. The vertex involution is

      ρ(j) = m + 1 − j (1 ≤ j ≤ m),             ρ(m + j) = m + n + 1 − j (1 ≤ j ≤ n).

It induces the pairing bijection D ↦→ {{ρ(a), ρ(b)} : {a, b} ∈ D}. The same transforma-
tion holds before removing noise smoothing. Passing to the finitely many diagram limits
proves (3.2) with precisely (3.1).
    Lemmas 3.7 and 3.8 independently prove fixed-t norm summability of the Xn , using
the fixed-order construction just cited and the universal pairing bound. Proposition 3.9
identifies their series as a Stratonovich mild
                                           ∑︁N solution. None2 of those proofs uses the
present mixed-term identity. Hence UN = n=0 Xn → u in L , and

                    |EUN2 − Eu2 | ≤ ∥UN − u∥2 (∥UN ∥2 + ∥u∥2 ) −→ 0.

The finite expansions of EUN2 increase, since every mixed expectation computed above
is nonnegative. Their limit is the full double sum, independent of its enumeration.
Regrouping by total order gives (3.3). This proves Proposition 3.1.

3.2    A small-order check and a source uniformity issue

                                                  15
    Monotone convergence in (3.8) yields finite limits LD (λ) for all λ ≥ 0. The limiting
integrand at zero is integrable, so dominated convergence shows continuity at zero.
Here is the finite-measure Laplace argument, including possible loss of mass. Push the
measures to [0, 1]2k by yl = e−rl . Uniformly bounded masses on this compact cube have
weak cluster limits. Their integer monomial moments are the prescribed LD values,    ∏︁ soc
polynomial density determines a unique cluster limit. For c > 0 the integral of l yl
is LD (c, . . . , c). As c ↓ 0, continuity at zero and monotone convergence show that the
cluster limit has no mass on the faces where any yl = 0. Closed neighborhoods of the
y-zero faces have limiting mass tending to zero; the portmanteau inequality bounds the
approximating tail masses by these same quantities. Pulling back therefore proves weak
convergence on [0, ∞)2k . Bounded continuous test functions are handled by cutting off a
neighborhood of those zero faces and then using the vanishing tail mass. Every sequence
of widths tending to zero has this same limit, so the convergence is joint and independent
of their relative rates.
    Sending just λl → ∞ in the limiting positive integral (3.8) gives zero by domination
by its integrable zero-parameter integrand. On the measure side this limit is νD {rl = 0}.
Thus every coordinate-zero face has zero mass.
    Heat and wave scaling give, for Borel sets,

                               ρϵ,D (cA) = c(4−α)k ρϵ/c2 ,D (A).

This follows from Gcr (c dz) = cGr ( dz), γb (cx) = c−α γb/c2 (x), and the 2k time Jacobians.
Passing to vague limits proves the stated homogeneity of ρD . If a compact part A of
{ℓi = T }, T > 0, had positive mass, its dilates cA at distinct c ∈ [1, 2] would be disjoint,
have a common positive lower mass bound, and lie in one compact set. This contradicts
local finiteness. Exhausting the face by compacts proves it has zero mass. For an
empty chain the positive-level face is empty. The finite-horizon region is bounded, and
its boundary is contained in these horizon faces and the coordinate-zero faces.   ∑︁
                                                                                        Weak
                                                                                     rl
convergence with a compact cutoff, and the bounded continuous multiplier e φ there,
proves (3.7). This proves Lemma 3.5.

joint L2 limit, denoted Xn (t, x); it is the multiple Stratonovich integral of gn . Green
smoothing in dimension three is removed before noise smoothing.

    We next bound removal of that temporal cutoff uniformly in all spatial widths.

    Apply the imported universal pairing inequality [4, Theorem 3.1, (3.6), pp. 14–15]
to the damped kernels e−s Gs . At first smooth the Green kernels in dimension three
and cap the temporal Laplace domains above. Each spatial edge measure is a finite

is a Laplace interval or its restriction. These are exactly the hypotheses of that theorem.
The finite-horizon exponential loss is at most eT1 +T2 . Lemma 3.4 bounds all unmarked

bound survives both passages. Its right side tends to zero as η ↓ 0.

The limit allows arbitrary relative rates of the two widths. In dimension three the Green-
width limit precedes the noise-width limit. No uniqueness assertion is made.

    For d = 3 we read the source’s generalized-integrand definition using its Green regu-
larization, with the random factor held fixed. The proof below establishes that limit for
the actual product uWϵ,δ .
Proof. Let P≤n be the closed Gaussian polynomial space of total degree at most n,
generated by all the coupled source fields. It includes all lower-degree contractions.
The fixed-width terminal integrals lie in this space, by their Bochner construction; their
fixed-order L2 limits Xn do too.
    We first record an elementary multiplication bound on these spaces. If H is any
centered linear Gaussian variable and F ∈ P≤n , then
                                         √
                             ∥HF ∥2 ≤ 2 n + 1 ∥H∥2 ∥F ∥2 .                         (3.18)

If H = 0 the assertion is immediate. Otherwise take H/∥H∥2 as the first coordinate of
an orthonormal Gaussian basis. On normalized  √ Hermite √    polynomials its creation and
annihilation operators have norms at most n + 1 and n on total degrees at most
n: the squared coefficient sums have respective weights a1 + 1 ≤ n + 1 and a1 ≤ n.
Their sum proves the bound for finite-coordinate polynomials. Approximation in the
degree-closed space proves it for F as well. Indeed, the products of an approximating
sequence are L2 Cauchy by this bound; subsequences converging almost surely identify
their limit with HF . This also proves the closedness of multiplication needed below.
The argument allows F to depend on H.
    Lemma 3.8 proves absolute L2 convergence of the defining series for u, locally uni-
formly in time in the displayed norm bounds. All fields are strongly measurable as
L2 (Ω) vectors. At positive widths, rescale the ordered times and Green increments to
fixed unit-time domains. The Gaussian evaluations are jointly measurable, and their
bounded moments and the finite Green masses give Bochner domination on bounded
time intervals. Parameter integration therefore gives joint strong measurability in (s, y).
Pointwise L2 limits along a fixed countable sequence of widths give this property for Xn ,
then for the series. For later Green limits we also need spatial continuity at each fixed
time. Positive-width terminal terms are L2 -continuous in space, by continuity of their
bounded smoothed covariance products and dominated integration against the finite
Green measures. Joint spatial stationarity of the coupled regularizations shows that

                      ∥Ynb (s, y) − Xn (s, y)∥2 = ∥Ynb (s, 0) − Xn (s, 0)∥2 .


                                                20
Thus their convergence is uniform in y for fixed s. Consequently Xn (s, ·), and by summa-
bility u(s, ·), have continuous L2 representatives.
    Fix an external width a = (ϵ, δ) > 0 and write Wa = Wϵ,δ , with variance Va =

                                           √ √︁

and the spatial Green mass is t−s. At fixed a, multiplication of the inner regularizations
by Wa preserves their L2 convergence, by the same bound on degree at most n−1. Their
norms have the uniform domination just used. Dominated Bochner integration therefore
gives

Here a is at the last, terminal vertex and b at the preceding n − 1 vertices. At positive

and the identity is exact. In particular, (3.15) passes to this inner limit:

    We spell out the dimension-three convention in these steps.                     The product
Wa (s, y)Xn−1 (s, y) is L2 -continuous in y. Apply (3.18) first to Wa (s, y)(Xn−1 (s, y) −
Xn−1 (s, y ′ )) and then to (Wa (s, y) − Wa (s, y ′ ))Xn−1 (s, y ′ ). The two factors tend to zero
in the required norms, by the continuity just proved and the smoothed covariance of Wa .
The bound (3.19) is uniform in y. Therefore convolution against pη ∗ Gt−s converges
in L2 to convolution against Gt−s : on a compact set approximate this continuous
vector function by a finite continuous partition of unity times fixed vector values, use
scalar weak convergence, and control the complement by tightness and the uniform
bound. The mass t − s and (3.19) permit dominated time integration. This proves the
Green-width limit at fixed external widths. The same argument applies before the inner
                    (b,a)
noise limit; all Yn       already use their Green limit at positive noise widths. Thus, (3.20)
does not interchange the source’s Green and noise limits.

                               lim In,a (t, x) = Xn (t, x) in L2 .                         (3.22)
                               a↓0

To see the role of joint convergence, fix ζ > 0 and choose r > 0 so that every vertex

Proof. The wave kernel scales as G(cs, c dy) = cG(s, dy), and the covariance has degree
−α. Changing all time and spatial variables by θ−1 in the norms yields

             N0 (θ) = θ−2 ,    N1 (θ) = N1 (1)θα+α0 −2 ,        N2 (θ)2 = N2 (1)2 θ−β .   (3.26)

a positive constant if needed. Theorem 3.1 and q0 + q2 /2 = k give K k θ−βk for every

are uniform at both stages, by the multiplier bounds in Lemma 3.7. This proves (3.23).

Remark 3.11 (G0 verdict and its scope). Since β > 2, we have 1 < ρ < 2. Thus,
Proposition 3.10 improves the crude quadratic time scale under homogeneity. It does not
itself settle Goal 43.1; Section 13 uses a different argument. Theorem 3.1 used through its
uniform pairing estimate is not viable for Goal 43.1: even with exact homogeneous norm
scaling, the resulting majorant has exponent ρ > κ. The order coefficient has factorial
denominator (k!)3−α−α0 , whereas the desired scale requires (k!)3−α ; the missing factor is
(k!)α0 , up to exponential factors in k. Counting the 2k + 1 chain-length splits does not
cause this loss, since it can be absorbed into an exponential factor. This conclusion does
not rule out a more refined application retaining temporal order or grouping pairings
before estimating them, and does not disprove the conjecture. In the formal boundary
limit α0 ↓ 0, both ρ and κ tend to (4 − α)/(3 − α). This necessary time-independent
calibration does not distinguish them and asserts no endpoint theorem or uniformity of
constants in α0 . At p = 1 use the empty-chain case with eθt in place of e2θt ; minimizing at

                                                  23
θ = βk/t changes only a constant to the power k, leaving the same factorial denominator
(k!)3−α−α0 . It therefore also loses the factorial power α0 relative to β/κ = 3 − α, the
power corresponding to the target series scale. This is a comparison of scales, not a
coefficient estimate asserted by the source’s Theorem 1.2. The loss is not specific to the
second moment. Both the crude and homogeneous upper bounds are consistent with the
Jensen lower bound, being at larger time scales. No identification with the Skorohod
second moment is used: its exclusion of self-pairings changes the combinatorics, already
visible in the (0, 2) and (2, 0) terms of Lemma 3.2.


4     Gate G1: finite-order calibrations in one space
      dimension
We now compute the total-order-two and total-order-four coefficients in Proposition 3.1,
first for spatial white noise and then for γ(x) = cα |x|−α with 0 < α < 1 and cα > 0.
Here d = 1 throughout. A total order is the sum of the two Stratonovich orders; it is
not an orthogonal Wiener-chaos order. Write

             A = {(1, 2), (3, 4)},   B = {(1, 3), (2, 4)},    C = {(1, 4), (2, 3)}.

These are the three pairings of four vertices. All finite integrals below retain the nor-
malization G(s, y) = 12 1{|y|<s} and covariance |s − r|−α0 γ(y − z).

4.1    Spatial white noise: all order-four diagrams
For a, b, c ≥ 0, define

                      F (a, b, c) = 4ab − (a + b − c)2+ + (|a − b| − c)2+ .             (4.1)

This is the area of {(x, y) : |x| < a, |y| < b, |x − y| < c}. In particular, F is nonnegative
and symmetric in its three arguments. To verify the formula, subtract from the rectangle
the two regions where |x − y| ≥ c; when c ≥ |a − b| these are two right triangles, and
when c < |a − b| the retained strip has area 4 min(a, b)c. The three formulas, including
c ≥ a + b, give (4.1). Symmetry also follows from swapping x, y and the determinant-one
change (x, y) ↦→ (x, x − y).
   In the following table, V is a spatial factor and q1 , q2 are nonnegative time gaps.
The contribution of a row is the integral of V (q1 q2 )−α0 over its domain, with ordinary
Lebesgue measure in its four listed variables. The domains are

                      (0, 4) : a, b, c, d > 0, a + b + c + d < t,
                      (1, 3) : 0 < r < t, a, b, c > 0, a + b + c < t,
                      (2, 2) : a, b, c, d > 0, a + b < t, c + d < t.

The letters a, b, c, d denote increments within the nonempty chains; for (1, 3), r is the time
of the single vertex. Values on zero-gap sets do not affect these nonnegative integrals.

Each entry scales as t6−2α0 times its unit-time value.

Proof. In each pairing identify its first pair’s spatial positions with x and the other pair’s
positions with y. The two delta covariances have unit mass, so spatial integration leaves
1/16 times the area allowed by the four Green indicators. For a single chain the position
lists for A, B, C are, respectively, (x, x, y, y), (x, y, x, y) and (x, y, y, x). Their areas are
4ac, 4a min(b, c, d) and 4a min(b, d).
    For (1, 3) take the single position to be x. The long chain has positions (x, y, y),
(y, x, y) or (y, y, x) for A, B, C. The first area is 4 min(r, a)b; the other two areas
are F (r, a, min(b, c)) and F (r, a, c). For (2, 2) the position lists of the two chains are
(x, x), (y, y) for A, (x, y), (x, y) for B, and (x, y), (y, x) for C. The areas are 4ac,
4 min(a, c) min(b, d) and F (a, c, min(b, d)). In every case reading the paired times gives
the two gaps in (4.2). This proves each row, with no unlisted pairing or multiplicity. The
distributional delta integrations can equivalently be obtained by convolution with the
source’s spatial approximate identity: for positive increments the remaining bounded
interval indicators have only boundary discontinuities, and those boundaries have two-
dimensional measure zero. The resulting areas converge to the displayed areas. They
are uniformly bounded at unit time: each smoothed delta has mass one, so its integral
over its paired coordinates in [−1, 1]2 is at most two. The four Green factors give a spa-
tial bound 22 /16 = 1/4. Integrability of the time weights follows from the disjoint-pair
bound used in Lemma 4.2 below.
    Exchanging chains gives the (4, 0) and (3, 1) sums without changing the covariance
factors. This proves (4.3). Finally, scaling all four time variables by t gives t4 from
their measure, t2 from the area and t−2α0 from the covariance factors. This proves
Proposition 4.1.

    Finiteness can be checked without a numerical argument. On the unit-time domain
all vertex positions lie in [−1, 1]. Enlarge the time domain to [0, 1]4 and the spatial
domain to [−1, 1]4 , and bound the Green indicators by one. The two time covariance
factors involve disjoint pairs and are integrable since α0 < 1. The same is true of the
two spatial factors since α < 1. Tonelli’s theorem now gives a finite bound. This proves
Proposition 4.3.
Remark 4.4 (Normalization and static Riesz check). The same integrable bound with
any fixed exponent ā ∈ (α0 , 1) permits α0 ↓ 0 at these fixed orders. From (4.7), the
static total-order-two coefficient is
                                          cα 23−α
                                                            .
                               (1 − α)(2 − α)(3 − α)(4 − α)

                                                     28
For example, the double integral of (s + r)2−α − |s − r|2−α on [0, 1]2 is (24−α − 4)/((3 −
α)(4 − α)), which verifies this value after adding twice the single-chain coefficient. If
one chooses the normalization cα = (1 − α)/2, this coefficient tends to 1/3 as α ↑ 1,
in agreement with the white-space value. More generally, at each fixed α0 ∈ (0, 1) the
two normalized coefficients in (4.7) tend to c0 /2 and c0 , respectively. For the second
coefficient, the bracket at t = ∫︁1 tends to (1 − v)2 , the prefactor tends to 1/2, and
                                   1
dominated convergence gives 12 0 v −α0 (1 − v)2 dv = c0 . The brackets are uniformly
bounded for α near one, so Cv −α0 is an integrable dominator. This is a finite-coefficient
normalization check; unnormalized |y|−α must not be identified with δ0 .

4.3    Calibration table and limits of the finite-order test
Let M2 and M4 denote only the total-order-two and total-order-four contributions. The
results above give

       covariance and time regime                  M2 (t)               M4 (t)
                                                       3−α0          6−2α0
              δ0 , 0 < α 0 < 1                   2c0 t              t      M4 (1)
                 δ0 , α0 = 0                        t3 /3             11t6 /540
                 −α
          cα |y| , 0 < α0 < 1                    tβ M2 (1)            t2β M4 (1)
                                                    3−α 4−α
                                                cα 2 t
             cα |y|−α ,   α0 = 0                                     t8−2α M4 (1)
                                       (1 − α)(2 − α)(3 − α)(4 − α)

Here c0 is defined in Lemma 3.2; the fractional-time white coefficient M4 (1) is the explicit
four-dimensional time integral in (4.2), and the Riesz coefficients are specified by (4.7)–
(4.8). No closed form for those remaining integrals is asserted.

Remark 4.5 (What this gate tests). The static exponent from the published Chen–Hu
theorem is (4 − α)/(3 − α); see [5, Theorem 1.2, (1.9), p. 2147]. Both κ and the G0
exponent ρ have this boundary limit. The finite coefficient limits above are justified
separately by dominated convergence; they do not exchange t → ∞ and α0 ↓ 0 and
do not verify an asymptotic constant. In particular, the disputed printed white-space
constant in the separate Chen–Hu source audit is not used here. The single-chain rows
agree with the first-moment expansion, and all mixed rows are nonnegative, consistent
with the Jensen comparison. Relative to the Skorohod expansion, the rows with self-
pairings are present here and cannot be dropped: already M2 = EX12 + 2EX2 . This is
a structural comparison only, not a Skorohod result. Finally, any fixed truncation is a
polynomial in tβ with nonnegative coefficients, so its logarithm divided by tκ tends to
zero. Orders two and four can falsify a normalization or an incorrect pairing rule, but
cannot determine an all-order factorial power or prove either principal goal.

5     G2 upper bounds for spatial exponents at most
      one

 Reviewed partial result: the upper bound at scale tκ is proved for 0 < α ≤ 1
 in dimensions one, two and three under the standing assumptions. The separate
 one-dimensional Riesz estimates retain their sharper constants and white endpoint.
 Degree-one covariance in dimensions two and three does not mean full spatial white
 noise. Section 13 treats the higher exponents by a separate argument. The intrinsic
 constant is identified later in Theorem 40.6.

We retain the two-chain expansion of Proposition 3.1, but estimate its spatial and tem-
poral factors in a different order. The spatial estimate keeps a power of the number of
pairs. After taking a common spatial maximum, we sum all temporal pairings before
using the ordered time domains.

has determinant one. In this section xl denotes a spatial position and yl its increment;
this local notation differs from the position variables yl in (3.1). The terminal evaluation
point of u(t, x) remains denoted by x. Let pϵ be the centered Brownian Gaussian density

     Replace the nonnegative truncated temporal factors by |si − sj |−α0 before taking
their pairing sum. The temporal symmetry, cube integral and split count in (5.7) do not
involve the spatial dimension. Thus, exactly the multiplication of counts in the proof of
Proposition 5.2 gives the first bound in (5.13) for the regularized moments.
     We spell out the limit in dimension three. Fix every noise-smoothing parameter
and first let η ↓ 0. At each time vector, the Gaussian moment formula is a finite
sum of bounded continuous products of the smoothed covariance functions, integrated
against products of the finite Green measures.
                                           ∏︁       These products of measures converge
weakly as η ↓ 0, with the same masses l hl . At fixed noise smoothing
                                                                 ∏︁          the Gaussian
covariance factors are uniformly bounded, so a constant times l hl dominates on each
bounded time simplex. Dominated convergence applies also to the covariance between
two approximations with different η values. The resulting common joint covariance limit
proves the L2 Cauchy property of each fixed-order random integral. This is the inner
limit used in the source’s definition on pp. 13–14. Products of two factors converge in L1
by Cauchy–Schwarz, so the moment inequality survives this limit. Next send the noise-
smoothing parameters to zero using the fixed-order L2 convergence of Lemma 3.6, and
again pass products in L1 . In dimensions one and two only the latter limit is required.
The bounds at both stages are uniform.
     We have proved the first coefficient bound for Ak (t). The factorial inequality and
                                                                            gen
nonnegative-series argument in Proposition 5.2 apply unchanged, with Ksp        in place of
Ksp and A0 = 1. This proves Proposition 5.5.

Remark 5.6 (Scope of the spectral estimates). If V = 0, spectral homogeneity gives
µ(BR ) = 0 for every R, hence µ = 0 and u = 1; the noise-free conclusion is immediate.
Otherwise, all angular and dimensional dependence is allowed in V . For 0 < α < 1, the
example γ(x) = c|x1 |−α in dimensions two or three has its spectral measure supported
on an axis; a full-dimensional density is not required. The absolute Fourier envelope
diverges at α = 1, where the separate oscillatory calculation in Lemma 5.4 is essential.
                                                        gen
The endpoint is not obtained by taking a limit in Ksp       for α < 1. For d = 1 and
γ = cδ δ0 , the source convention gives µ( dξ) = cδ dξ/(2π) and V = cδ /π. Hence,
                         gen
the endpoint constant Ksp    = πV = cδ agrees exactly with Proposition 5.2, with no
extra antipodal factor. In dimensions two and three the endpoint covers degree-one

                                                   36
homogeneous covariance, not full spatial white noise. No result for α > 1 follows from
these estimates.

5.4    Checks on the scale, constants and scope
                                                                         gen
Corollary 5.7 (First-moment calibration). Let K∗ be Ksp under (5.1), or Ksp  under
                                                      1/q
the hypotheses of Proposition 5.5. Put C2,∗ = q(2ZK∗ ) . Then
                                                    (︃          )︃1/q
                                 κ                       ZK∗                    C2,∗
            Eu(t, x) ≤ exp(C1,∗ t ),     C1,∗ = q                       =                .   (5.14)
                                                         23−α               2(4−α)/(3−α)


In either nonzero case 2C1,∗ < C2,∗ . Conditionally on a first-moment lower rate at least
L∗ , 2L∗ ≤ 2C1,∗ < C2,∗ , consistently with Proposition 2.4.
Proof. For one chain of length 2k, the increments sum to at most t. The spatial estimate
in Lemma 5.1, or in the proof of Proposition 5.5, therefore applies with t/2 in place of
t. The temporal pairing sum on one ordered simplex is Pk (Zt2−α0 )k /(2k)!. Using the
same regularization and limit order as in the respective upper-bound proof gives

                                 (ZK∗ /2b+1 )k tβk   (ZK∗ /23−α )k tβk
                   EX2k (t, x) ≤                   ≤                   .
                                     k! k bk              (k!)q

Every odd term has zero expectation. The L2 expansion permits taking expectations
of partial sums, and the resulting nonnegative even series is summed by the scalar
inequality in the proof of Proposition 5.2. This gives (5.14). If the first-moment lower
rate is at least L∗ , (5.14) implies L∗ ≤ C1,∗ . In the stated nonzero cases K∗ > 0, hence
C1,∗ > 0; and (4 − α)/(3 − α) > 1 gives 2C1,∗ < C2,∗ . This proves Corollary 5.7.
Remark 5.8 (Checks and relation to G0). The argument preserves the factor k −(2−α)k
from the spatial increment maximum. Replacing the spatial volume merely by a constant
times t(2−α)k would lose the factorial power needed in (5.5). This proof does not use the
common-Laplace-parameter norm majorant of Proposition 3.10, and it does not assert a
stronger factorial estimate for every individual diagram. Instead, the temporal ordering
is exploited after summing pairings in (5.7). Thus, the scoped G0 obstruction remains
valid. For unit white covariance, the bound on the total-order-two coefficient is 2Z,
whereas the exact value from Lemma 3.2 is 2c0 ; their ratio is 2(3 − α0 ) > 4. At the
static endpoint the exact order-four coefficient is 11/540, while the first bound in (5.5)
gives 1/2. These are checks of normalization, not evidence of sharp constants.
Remark 5.9 (Boundary and literature comparisons). As α0 ↓ 0, the time exponent
tends to (4 − α)/(3 − α), the Chen–Hu static exponent cited in Remark 4.5. This is
an exponent calibration, not an interchange of the static and large-time limits. With
the Riesz normalization cα = (1 − α)/2, the displayed Ksp tends to one as α ↑ 1,
matching unit white covariance. For comparison only, the Skorohod upper bound of
Balan–Conus [1, Theorem 2.1(b), (8), (10), p. 1492] has time exponent (2H + 2 −
a)/(3 − a). Setting H = 1 − α0 /2 and a = α yields the same κ. Their covariance

                                            37
normalization contains H(2H − 1) and they use a Skorohod mild solution. No constants
or solutions are identified, and their theorem is not an input to the present proof. The
current coverage of this section is 0 < α ≤ 1 in d = 1, 2, 3, including the one-dimensional
white case. It does not address 1 < α < 2 − α0 in dimensions two and three.

In dimension three the third coordinate integrates to one. Each factor converges when

axes. The singular sets have zero shifted Green measure in both dimensions. Fatou with

Even if both widths must be positive, take η = ϵ and use their sum in the Gaussian
identity above. Thus, no uniform M exists. This proves Proposition 6.5.

Remark 6.6 (Calibrations and scope). The printed first-moment statement and uncon-
ditional comparison are Theorem 2.1 and Proposition 2.4. Under the additional spatial
hypothesis, Cauchy–Schwarz and Proposition 6.2 give Eu(t, x) ≤ exp{qK 1/q tκ /2}, and
hence 2L∗ ≤ qK 1/q if the intended first-moment lower bound holds. No sharp first- or
second-moment constant is identified. As α0 ↓ 0, the exponent tends to (4 − α)/(3 − α),
consistently with the Chen–Hu static calibration in Remark 5.9; no interchange of limits
is claimed. For the Riesz cases here, the Skorohod comparison of Balan–Conus [1, The-
orem 2.1(b), p. 1492; p. 1490] applies with a = α < d, α < 2, and H = 1 − α0 /2 > 1/2.
Its exponent (2H + 2 − a)/(3 − a) is κ. This comparison is restricted to those Riesz
cases, with a different solution concept and covariance normalization; it is not an in-
put here, and no equality with the Balan–Chen–Wang second-moment asymptotics is
inferred. The obstructions above concern only the spatial supremum, not existence, mo-
ment finiteness, or failure of the target time scale. In particular, arbitrary homogeneous
covariances remain outside this conditional argument, even when α + α0 < 2.


7    Riesz bounds by interpolation with static noise

 Candidate argument, pending independent review. This section concerns isotropic

We use the static Stratonovich model of Chen–Hu as one input and the space-degree-
one estimate of Section 5 as the other. The static source allows nonnegative generalized
covariances, not only Riesz functions: see its standing assumptions (1.2)–(1.3), p. 2145,
and its explicit generalized-covariance convention (2.18), p. 2152, in [5]. In the latter
convention every covariance factor is heat regularized before its defining limit. A nonneg-
ative, even, positive-definite tempered Radon measure is such a generalized covariance.
In each application below we separately verify its homogeneity range and static Dalang
condition before using the source’s quantitative coefficient estimates (5.1) and (5.15).
Positive definiteness of a weighted endpoint is proved in Lemma 8.1, or is an explicit
assumption in Hypothesis 9.2; weighting a measure alone is not being used to assert
that property. Chen’s variational finiteness theorem supplies only finiteness of the static
constant, not a coefficient-rate theorem.

    We pass to fixed-order limits before using the static coefficient bound. In dimension
three fix ϵ, δ and let η ↓ 0. The target and static smoothed covariance factors are
bounded continuous functions of the spatial
                                         ∏︁     positions. Products of the finite Green
measures converge weakly with masses l hl . A constant times this product dominates
each time integrand at fixed noise smoothing, so dominated convergence applies on the
bounded time simplexes. The same argument with two Green widths gives a common
mixed covariance limit, and hence fixed-order L2 convergence. This identifies the static
limit with the measure convention of Chen–Hu [5, (2.22), pp. 2152–2153]. The target
uses the corresponding inner limit in Chen [4, pp. 13–14]. We need no limit identification
for Lϵ,η

fixed-order L2 convergence follows from Lemma 3.6. If Y, Y ′ , Z, Z ′ are the corresponding
approximations and limits, then

                  ∥Y Z − Y ′ Z ′ ∥1 ≤ ∥Y − Y ′ ∥2 ∥Z∥2 + ∥Y ′ ∥2 ∥Z − Z ′ ∥2 .

Therefore, every fixed split product converges in L1 . The finite sums over the 2k + 1
splits converge as well; a zero-order factor stays equal to one. This argument applies
directly in dimension two, where no Green smoothing is needed. Passing to these limits
in (7.5), using (7.6), gives

                                                  47
Remark 7.3 (Calibrations and remaining scope). The time exponent in (7.3) tends to
(4 − α)/(3 − α) as α0 ↓ 0, in agreement with the Chen–Hu static exponent. This does
not interchange the static and large-time limits or assert uniformity of the constants.
Cauchy–Schwarz gives Eu(t, x) ≤ exp(qC 1/q tκ /2). If the first-moment lower rate is at
least L∗ , this upper bound implies 2L∗ ≤ qC 1/q . Thus, the first-moment and Jensen
calibrations are retained, without identifying a sharp constant. The Skorohod com-
parison remains only the comparison recorded in Remark 6.6; its exponent agrees, but
its solution concept and covariance normalization differ. No Skorohod estimate is used
in this proof. The argument covers isotropic Riesz covariance throughout the stated
higher-degree range in dimensions two and three, including the previously uncovered
two-dimensional interval 3/2 ≤ α < 2 − α0 . It does not prove the full homogeneous-
covariance goal: arbitrary anisotropic profiles need not factor into admissible endpoint
covariances as above. The sharp second-moment constant, novelty and formal verifica-
tion remain open.


8    General homogeneous covariance in dimension
     two

 Candidate argument, pending independent review. This section gives complete end-
 point constructions and a complete planar transfer. It does not use the preceding
 candidate proposition as an accepted input. Earlier accepted-scope statements and
 review confidence remain unchanged.

Throughout this section d = 2. Write Γ for the spatial covariance as a nonnegative,
even, positive-definite tempered Radon measure. For a locally integrable covariance
function, this means Γ( dx) = γ(x) dx. The measure interpretation uses the Gaussian
approximation framework of Chen [4, pp. 2–3, 13] and Chen–Hu [5, pp. 2145–2147, 2151–
2153]. All products of covariance factors below are taken after Gaussian smoothing; no
product or fractional power of distributions is used. Homogeneity means

                             Γ(rA) = r2−α Γ(A),           r > 0,                    (8.1)

for Borel sets A. This is equivalent to the covariance homogeneity in Hypothesis 1.1. If
Γ = 0, the noise vanishes and u = 1. We first treat Γ ̸= 0 and 1 < α < 2.

tempered distribution, and 1 < χ < 2 = d. The source expressly includes generalized
covariances and uses their heat-regularized interpretation [5, p. 2152, (2.18)]. Thus the
coefficient estimate invoked below is a direct application within the source’s stated class,
not an extension from density kernels. From Chen–Hu [5, Theorem 3.8(i), p. 2165], every

and empty chains. Thus, every coefficient is finite and nonnegative.

condition. The source’s invocation on p. 2175 identifies 2χ/(4−χ) M with the square-root
variational quantity covered by Chen [2, Theorem 1.1, p. 2]. That theorem explicitly
states finiteness under Dalang’s condition and permits generalized nonnegative positive-
definite covariances. We import that finiteness, not a sharp value or its proof.

Corollary 8.4 (Full planar parameter range). In dimension two, under Hypoth-
esis 1.1 and the standing covariance positivity assumptions, the upper bound
Eu2 (t, x) ≤ exp(C2 tκ ) holds throughout 0 < α < 2 − α0 , with a finite constant
C2 depending on α0 and the spatial covariance. A nonnegative generalized covariance
is interpreted as the homogeneous measure and its Gaussian approximations described
above.

Proof. For 0 < α ≤ 1, apply Proposition 5.5. For 1 < α < 2 − α0 , apply Proposition 8.3.
If the covariance vanishes, u = 1 and C2 = 0 suffices. This proves Corollary 8.4.

Remark 8.5 (Planar calibrations and remaining goals). The exponent tends to (4 −
α)/(3 − α) as α0 ↓ 0, consistently with the static source, without an interchange of limits
or uniformity claim for the constants. For the nonzero higher-exponent case, Cauchy–
Schwarz gives Eu(t, x) ≤ exp(qC 1/q tκ /2). If the first-moment lower rate is at least L∗ ,
this implies 2L∗ ≤ qC 1/q . No sharp constant is identified. The existing comparisons with

                                                   54
Skorohod exponents, in their stated Riesz subclasses, concern different solution concepts
and are not inputs here. The planar endpoint construction is dimension-specific; no
general higher-exponent result in dimension three follows from it. Section 13 treats the
full homogeneous-covariance class by a separate argument. The sharp constant remains
open.


9    Three-dimensional bounds from covariance end-
     points

 Expanded candidate, pending independent review of the new assembly. The con-
 ditional moment transfer has not passed the required high-confidence gate. The
 plane-supported endpoints and finite-sum comparison were reviewed separately as
 deterministic tools. We now include their proofs, a new linear-image closure argu-
 ment, and the resulting moment applications. Earlier accepted-scope statements are
 unchanged; no general three-dimensional factorization is asserted.


We first construct the product endpoints. Zero coordinate exponents below mean con-
stant factors, not one-dimensional white noise.

   We now take d = 3, 0 < α0 < 1, and 1 < α < 2 − α0 . Let Γ be a nonnegative,
even, positive-definite tempered spatial covariance, with homogeneity exponent α. As a
positive distribution it is a Radon measure satisfying Γ(rA) = r3−α Γ(A); a covariance
function is identified with its density measure. Gaussian convolution is used for all
covariance products. Choose and fix

               α0 < ϑ < 2 − α,         b0 = α0 /ϑ,         χ = (α − ϑ)/(1 − ϑ).                  (9.3)

Then 0 < b0 < 1 and α < χ < 2, by the identities in the preceding proof. The zero
target covariance is trivial, since then u = 1.

Hypothesis 9.2 (Gaussian covariance comparison). There are nonnegative, even,
positive-definite tempered endpoint covariances Γlow and Γst on R3 with measure scaling

                    Γlow (rA) = r2 Γlow (A),        Γst (rA) = r3−χ Γst (A),

such that, for every ϵ > 0 and z ∈ R3 ,

                       (pϵ ∗ Γ)(z) ≤ (pϵ ∗ Γlow )(z)ϑ (pϵ ∗ Γst )(z)1−ϑ .                        (9.4)

    This is a condition on deterministic covariance measures and their Gaussian convo-
lutions. It assumes no moment estimate. We do not assert that every homogeneous
covariance in dimension three admits these endpoints.

Lemma 9.4 (Linear images and finite positive sums). Fix the parameters in (9.3).
The covariance comparison of Hypothesis 9.2 is preserved by simultaneously pushing the
target and its two endpoints forward under any invertible linear map T : R3 → R3 . It is
also preserved by finite sums with nonnegative weights, when all component comparisons
use the same α, ϑ, χ.

    Lemma 5.4 applies to the low endpoint with no angular-density assumption. Its
uniform closing bound is πVlow /2. Descending increment integration removes the only

     In the second and mixed moments of these integrals, the Gaussian moment factors
are bounded continuous functions of all spatial positions. For each pair of time vectors,
weak convergence of the products of Green measures, including mixed products with one

depending only on n, ϵ times the product of all time gaps dominates the resulting time
integrals. This is integrable on the two bounded time simplexes. Hence, as η ↓ 0,

Corollary 9.6 (Ordinary product and Riesz covariances in dimension three). Let d = 3,
0 < α0 < 1, and 1 < α < 2−α0 . The coefficient and all-time moment estimates in (9.6),
and the asymptotic estimate in (9.7), hold for either of the following spatial covariances,
with c > 0:

                                                         65
                ∏︁3        −aj
                                                          ∑︁
  1. γ(x) = c     j=1 |xj |    , where 0 ≤ aj < 1 and          j aj = α;

  2. γ(x) = c|x|−α .

In the first case the constant depends only on a1 , a2 , a3 , c, α0 ; in the second it depends
only on α, c, α0 . A zero coordinate exponent denotes a constant factor; the case aj = 1
and any interpretation of it as coordinate white noise are excluded.

Proof. In both cases choose the fixed midpoint ϑ = (α0 + 2 − α)/2 and define b0 , χ
by (9.3). Then α0 < ϑ < 2 − α, and all subsequent choices depend only on the data
in the statement. For the product covariance, Lemma 9.1 supplies b, v by its explicit
interval formula. Its same-amplitude endpoint densities γb , γv are admissible covariance
measures of exponents one and χ. Their common-width Gaussian inequality is exactly
Hypothesis 9.2.
    For the Riesz covariance take γ1 (x) = c|x|−1 and γχ (x) = c|x|−χ . Each degree
e ∈ {1, α, χ} is less than three, so the corresponding density is locally integrable and
tempered. Positive definiteness follows from the Gaussian mixture
                                           ∫︂ ∞
                               −e      c                     2
                           c|x| =               se/2−1 e−s|x| ds.
                                    Γ(e/2) 0

For a Schwartz autocorrelation, absolute integrability follows from e < 3 near zero
and rapid decay at infinity. Tonelli and Fubini therefore justify integrating the positive
Gaussian quadratic forms. The affine identity α = ϑ+(1−ϑ)χ gives γα = γ1ϑ γχ1−ϑ almost
everywhere, with the same amplitude c. Hölder on the Gaussian probability measure
proves the required common-width comparison for every ϵ, z. Thus, in both cases all
endpoint hypotheses are discharged without relying on an earlier candidate proposition.
Proposition 9.5 applies. This proves Corollary 9.6.

10     Physical and spectral energy for measure covari-
       ances
This section justifies the positive-measure energy used in (9.5). It does not assume
Hypothesis 9.2 or the conditional moment bound. The imported analytic input is the

                                            67
finiteness of the variation on the right of (1.5), stated in the closing sentence of Chen [2,
Theorem 1.1, p. 2].

 The energy-identification lemma passed a scoped independent review. The full con-
 ditional moment argument has not passed its required gate. This source-functional
 identification does not promote the separate conditional moment argument or resolve
 general covariance factorization.

                                      ∫︂         ∫︂

Evenness makes the Fourier sign convention in this real pairing immaterial. On the

                  ∫︂     ∫︂
         E (f h) = Ξ( dz) f (y + z)f (y)h(y + z)h(y) dy ≤ Q(f )1/2 Q(h)1/2 .

All these are absolutely convergent Schwartz pairings. In particular, E (f h) is the
quadratic energy of f h, not the quartic expression Q(f h).
   Let m( dy, dz) = dy Ξ( dz) and define

                       Af (y, z) = f (y + z)f (y),      Bf (ξ) = fˆ︁2 (ξ).

The measure m is positive and sigma-finite. The decomposition

                   Af − Ah = (f − h)(y + z)f (y) + h(y + z)(f − h)(y)

and the bound for P imply
                                          (︁             )︁

   Choose real Schwartz fn → g in W 1,2 . These estimates make both Afn and Bfn
Cauchy in their respective L2 spaces. Pass to a subsequence with fn → g Lebesgue-
almost everywhere. Translation invariance of Lebesgue measure and sigma-finite Fubini
give
             fn (y + z)fn (y) −→ g(y + z)g(y) for m-almost every (y, z).
Thus, the strong L2 (m) limit is Ag . The same Fubini argument shows that changing the
Borel representative of g does not change this limit.
   Also
                      ∥fn2 − g 2 ∥1 ≤ ∥fn − g∥2 (∥fn ∥2 + ∥g∥2 ) −→ 0.
Hence, fˆ︁n2 → gˆ︁2 uniformly on all frequencies. A subsequence of its strong L2 (µΞ ) limit
converges µΞ -almost everywhere, and must agree with this uniform limit. Consequently,
that strong limit is Bg . For every n, ∥Afn ∥2L2 (m) = Q(fn ) = ∥Bfn ∥2L2 (µΞ ) . Passing to the
strong limits proves (10.1), including finiteness. Passing the Schwartz quartic bound to
the same limit proves (10.2). This proves Lemma 10.1.
    In particular, for ∥g∥2 = 1 the energy is at most ∥∇g∥22 /2 + C0 . Therefore, the
supremum of QΞ (g)1/2 − ∥∇g∥22 is finite. Applied to Ξ = Γst , the profile-by-profile
identity justifies the actual measure-form functional in (9.5) without assigning a density
to the covariance or borrowing a disputed special-case constant.

11      A static phase estimate at first variance order

bound.

    The second term in Kϵ must be retained; neither its sign nor monotonicity of the
full phase energy in covariance is assumed. The estimates control both terms at this
one variance order. They do not control higher Gaussian contractions, products of
independent time blocks, overlapping noise levels or the temporal regularization limit.
Thus they do not yet establish Goal 43.1.


12      All static orders with arbitrary initial phase
We now seek all static Duhamel orders, retaining the full spatial covariance class of
Section 11. The temporal covariance is not included in this section.

 The phase estimates and finite-word cutoff lemma passed scoped correctness review.
 Static estimates alone do not identify the evolution for overlapping intervals; that
 step is treated in the next section.

    We use one external operator theorem. From Crouzeix–Palencia [6, author preprint
v1, (3), p. 1, and Theorem 6, pp. 5–6], if T is a bounded operator on a complex Hilbert
space and F is holomorphic on a neighborhood of W (T ), then
                       √
       ∥F (T )∥ ≤ (1 + 2) sup |F (w)|,          W (T ) = {⟨T u, u⟩ : ∥u∥ = 1}.   (12.1)
                               w∈W (T )

The theorem is imported, not proved here. All operators to which we apply it below are

For example, the last block has two root-comparison factors of norm at most two, with
Ch (L) between them. Only functions of L commute; no commutation with Aλ is used
in these ordered products. A block operator on H ⊕ H has norm at most the sum of
its four block norms, by the triangle inequality and the norm-one coordinate inclusions
and projections. Therefore, its norm is at most
                         (39 + 12λh)eλh ≤ 39(1 + λh)eλh ≤ 100e2λh .
The phase operator itself is the exponential of the bounded block generator with rows
(0, 1) and (−L, 0). It is entire in z; the intermediate square roots are used to estimate
it, not to impose a branch-dependent definition of that entire operator. This proves
Lemma 12.1.

Lemma 12.3 (Exact cutoffs for finite Gaussian words). Let gij , 1 ≤ i ≤ M , 1 ≤ j ≤ mi ,
be independent standard real Gaussian coordinates, grouped by color i, and let X be a
complex Hilbert space. On Gaussian polynomials with coefficients in X set
                                              mi
                                              ∑︂
                                      Ji =          Mgij ⊗ Kij ,
                                              j=1

where each Kij is bounded on X . Let F0 , . . . , Fn be bounded operators acting only on
X . Fix a word i1 , . . . , in , and let ri be the number of its occurrences of color i. If Ni ≥ ri
and P projects each color onto total Hermite degree at most Ni , then, for every v ∈ X ,
                   (I ⊗ Fn )(P Jin P )(I ⊗ Fn−1 ) · · · (P Ji1 P )(I ⊗ F0 )(1 ⊗ v)
                                                                                              (12.6)

                                                    76
The equality holds for finite sums and for Bochner integrals of such words whenever the
displayed vector integrals exist.
Proof. In normalized Hermite coordinates, multiplication by gij maps ha to
√︁                 √
   aij + 1 ha+eij + aij ha−eij ; the second term is zero if aij = 0. Thus it changes the total
degree of color i by one and leaves all other color degrees unchanged. The operators
I ⊗ Fl leave every Gaussian degree unchanged. After the first l insertions, the uncom-
pressed word therefore has color-i degree at most ri (l) = |{j ≤ l : ij = i}| ≤ ri ≤ Ni .
This is an induction starting from degree zero in every color. Consequently P is the
identity both before and after every insertion along that word. Induction on l now
proves (12.6). The lowering term has been retained: the assertion includes creation
followed by annihilation and all resulting self-contractions. Linearity and equality of
the vector integrands prove the last assertion. This is an equality of selected finite
words, not of full propagators at a fixed cutoff. This proves Lemma 12.3.

These spatial sandwiches are selfadjoint, so their row and column variances√agree. The
normalized Hermite creation-row norm from degree j to degree j + 1 is j + 1, and
annihilation is its adjoint, as proved in Lemma 12.2. Composition with the spatial
column and addition of the two parts prove (13.2). The same positive operator identities
hold with an identity on every other Hilbert factor. The calculation used only the radial
mass µ(B1 ), homogeneity and physical nonnegativity. Thus, Rα is the only covariance
quantity in these estimates. No angular bound or unsmoothed multiplication norm has
been absorbed into a constant. This proves Lemma 13.1.

Proposition 13.5 (Uniform interval coefficients). There is C = C(α, α0 , L, c) such that,
for real Schwartz phase v, every k ≥ 1 satisfies

Proof. Zero noise is trivial; omit any zero σi . Enlarge the inventory constant L to
max{1, L} if necessary. The hypotheses remain true and the final constant may de-
pend on L, so throughout this proof we may assume L ≥ 1. Replacing (σi , Wiϵ ) by
(|σi |, sgn(σi )Wiϵ ) leaves the potential unchanged and preserves the joint law of the inde-
pendent real Gaussian fields. Thus, we may assume σi > 0. We first work with finitely
many Gaussian coordinates of each field and a bounded spatial Fourier band. Denote
their common phase Hilbert space by Hρ = L2 (Ω; Xρ ). The initial data are projected to
the band. This contracts every Xρ norm; we keep the original ∥v∥ρ on the right of the
estimates. The projections converge strongly to the original data.
    Ordinary coefficients and their existence. Fix λ > 0 temporarily. Give interval i its
own amplitude zi and write

    Identification and summation. Keep positive smoothing, finite inventory and a fixed
coefficient. The finite-degree multiplication bound used in (13.21) is uniform in mode
and band cutoffs. On finite-coordinate Gaussian vectors the omitted annihilation tail is
zero and the creation tail tends to zero by its omitted pointwise variances and dominated
convergence. Density in each finite degree extends this strong convergence. Closedness
identifies the limit with actual multiplication by the measurable smoothed field. Fourier
projections converge strongly and commute with free evolution. Dominated time inte-
gration thus identifies every fixed Duhamel word. The selected coefficients of the local
Hermite truncation were already equal to those untruncated words. The full actual se-
ries is constructed separately using (13.21), choosing a sufficient degree cutoff anew at
each order. A fixed-degree truncated propagator is not identified with the full evolution.
The entire majorant gives convergence uniformly on compact amplitude sets. The coef-
ficients estimated above are therefore those of the actual finite-interval model, with no
zero-smoothing limit at this step.

coefficient bound contains no such number. Its constants come from δα/2 , the fixed
phase bound, the inventory lemma and L, c; covariance enters only through Rαk . The

once in each ti at zero. Odd total degree gives zero. At degree 2k the result is the sum
over unordered pair partitions, with one covariance product per pairing. Orientations
and orderings of the k pairs cancel the exponential’s 2k k! denominator. Integration
gives (13.25) with precisely the domains and coefficients in (13.26).
    Symmetrizing a chain measure does not change its stochastic integral: the product of
its n scalar field values is invariant under relabeling. Thus, averaging the n! pushforwards
of gnT,x with coefficient 1/n! gives the same Yn . There is no additional n! factor in (13.25),
or m!n! in a two-chain product. This identifies the ordinary ordered regularized integrals
and their symmetrized convention in (2.1).
    In dimension three one may first replace G by pη ∗ G. Those positive measures have
the same masses and converge weakly to G. Off the finitely many time cut points,
all finite Gaussian moments are bounded continuous functions of the spatial variables.
Dominated convergence for their finite Wick sums proves the same limit for the mixed
second moments with two Green widths. It gives L2 convergence to the Bochner integral
above, exactly the fixed-noise Green convention used in the source.
    If K ≥ 0, positivity of the Green measures makes each covariance product nonnega-
tive. Comparing them proves the stated finite-moment monotonicity, including self-pairs
and unequal chains. Finally,

particular, the moment of every finite partial-order sum is at most the full moment, even

real amplitude a > 0. This replaces Γ by a2 Γ and Rα by a2 Rα , without changing its

          E|uϵ,δ (a; T, x)|2 ≤ B(T ; a) := e(T + 2)d exp{C(a2 Rα )1/ν T κ }.        (13.31)

Here the full regularized series exists by Lemma 13.6; it is the ordinary Duhamel series
with its order-n term multiplied by an . Taking the finite partial-order cutoff to infinity
in (13.30) is justified at positive regularization by that lemma, not by a theorem about
the original unsmoothed solution.
    Lemma 3.6 supplies the fixed-order construction: each Ynϵ,δ converges in L2 to
Xn = Sn (gn ) as ϵ, δ ↓ 0, with arbitrary relative rates. The bounded-noise Green limit
and ordered-kernel convention needed to match these terms are already verified in
Lemma 13.6.
    Apply Lemma 13.7 with r = 2 and Br = B(T ; 2). It proves absolute L2 convergence
of the series defining u in (2.1) and convergence of the full regularized solution to that
series. Taking the limit in (13.31) at a = 1 proves the required bound. This series-
limit argument is independent of Proposition 3.1, Lemma 3.7, and the source’s Section 5
convergence proof. No first-moment asymptotic or printed normalization is used. The
fixed-order construction just used is proved here from the universal pairing bound and
the homogeneous norm estimates; neither the source’s Theorem 4.1 nor its Lemma 4.4
is imported. Proposition 3.9 separately proves that this same series satisfies the source’s

                                             96
Definition 2.1: its onefold Stratonovich integral equals u − 1. That construction uses the
corrected mixed-vertex crude bound for its product and order limits. This is a separate
dependency from the sharp-scale moment estimate just proved; the source’s Section 5(ii)
proof is not imported. The crude constants used for this identification may depend on
the fixed covariance; they do not enter (13.28). No uniqueness assertion is made.
    Cutoff dependence and the order of limits. The preceding argument uses the following
distinct statements.

 (i) At fixed total order 2k, the coefficient estimates are uniform in the Gaussian-
     mode and spatial-band cutoffs, in the finite interval family, and in positive spatial
     smoothing. The local Hermite cutoff Ni = 2ki preserves each selected coefficient
     exactly; it is an algebraic choice, not a degree limit. Lemma 13.4 and the inventory
     bound contain no smoothing-dependent variance.

 (ii) At fixed finite interval family and ϵ > 0, mode and band limits identify these
      coefficients with the actual Duhamel words. The entire-series majorant in Propo-
      sition 13.5 may depend on this family and ϵ, through vmax . It is used for identifi-
      cation, not as a uniform moment estimate. The uniform coefficient bound passes
      to these limits. Localization and positive covariance comparison are applied only
      after removing these cutoffs; positivity of a finite-mode covariance is not assumed.

(iii) For the resulting finite-depth interval model, the moment bound is uniform in
      J, ϵ. Fix positive source widths ϵ, δ and finite partial order N . The Green-width
      limit is taken first. The subsequent J → ∞ comparison in (13.30) is monotone
      convergence of finite positive Wick integrals. At these fixed noise widths, N → ∞
      is justified by Lemma 13.6, yielding (13.31). There is no infinite-depth Gaussian
      field or simultaneous mode-depth limit in this step.

(iv) At each fixed T, x, Γ and order n, Lemma 3.6 gives qualitative L2 convergence as
     ϵ, δ ↓ 0, with arbitrary relative rates. The bound B(T ; 2) gives tails uniform in
     these two widths. Lemma 13.7 first uses their limit at fixed partial order, then
     sends that order to infinity. The final moment bound comes from B(T ; 1), not the
     larger tail bound B(T ; 2).

 (v) All identifications above are for fixed T, x, Γ; no convergence rate uniform in T or
     over covariance shapes is claimed. They prove (13.28) for every fixed T ≥ 1 with
     the same parameter-only constant. Only after this estimate is proved is T → ∞
     used for the growth-rate conclusion.

The constants come from L = 78, c = 6α0 and the parameter-only constants of Proposi-
tion 13.5; covariance enters only through Rα . The volume ratio is |BT +2 |/|B1 | = (T +2)d .
This proves Theorem 13.8.

Remark 13.9 (Claim and calibration boundary). Theorem 13.8 asserts a nonsharp
upper growth rate. It does not identify the sharp constant or require a correction
of the printed first-moment normalization as an input. Its exponent tends to (4 −

                                             97
α)/(3 − α) as α0 ↓ 0. Cauchy–Schwarz and the unconditional Jensen inequality remain
compatible with the nonsharp upper constant; any numeric L∗ calibration retains the
source-normalization qualification stated earlier. The Skorohod exponent is compared,
not used to identify constants: both Gaussian creation and annihilation were retained
here. The intrinsic identification in Goal 43.2 is proved later in Theorem 40.6, using a
separate argument.

variational constant or its white-noise specialization is adopted. Strict positivity in that
formula follows by using a nonzero Gaussian competitor: its interaction and gradient
terms scale as Acα/2 and Bc2 , with A, B > 0, so their difference is positive for small c.
Finiteness and the moment law are the imported source result. The static kernel and
integral conventions are its (2.10)–(2.14), p. 2150; the fixed-order limits agree with its
Theorem 3.8(i), p. 2165. We supply the static series convergence needed below from our
regularized fractional-time upper bound, without using the source’s quantitative (4.3).

These raw-time integrals are finite. At fixed ϵ the spatial factors and Green masses are
bounded on the finite horizon; the disjoint pair endpoints give integrable powers on the
full time cube since a < 1. Diagonals are null there. On the disjoint union of the pairing
measures, the time multiplier at a is exactly the a/b power of its multiplier at b. Hölder’s
inequality therefore gives

                                mϵk (a) ≤ mϵk (0)1−a/b mϵk (b)a/b .

This is a raw-power identity; no exponent-dependent Laplace cutoff or Euler Gamma
normalization is being interpolated.
   We justify removal of the common spatial width. For each positive exponent, the
proof of Lemma 3.6 gives this raw-time limit as well. Indeed, writing Fϵ,η,D for the
diagram with common temporal cutoff η, (3.11) gives

                     Fϵ,η,D ≤ Fϵ,0,D ≤ Fϵ,η,D + ra (η),         ra (η) −→ 0,

uniformly in ϵ. First pass to the spatial limit at fixed η, then send η to zero. The
resulting limit is exactly the joint diagram value from that lemma. At zero exponent the
multiplier is one, so Lemma 3.5 gives the spatial limit directly; the positive-width Wick
calculation and the imported static fixed-order convergence identify it with the static
expectation. Thus, mϵk (a) → mk (a) at all three fixed exponents 0, a, b, proving (14.3)
and nonnegativity. No convergence uniform in a is claimed.

gives the factor t2(2k)−αk−ak = tβ(a)k and changes the spatial width to ϵ/t2 . Passing
                                            (a)
to the just established limits proves EX2k (t, x) = tβ(a)k mk (a). Odd expectations van-

Lemma 3.8 justifies the mean-series interchange. At zero the static norm series just
proved does so, with nonnegative even means. This proves (14.2). Since β(a) > 0, every
z ≥ 0 equals tβ(a) for some t ≥ 0, and the series is finite. This proves Lemma 14.1.

Remark 14.5 (Scope and calibrations). The static lower comparison holds at every
admissible positive temporal exponent; the strict gap from twice the actual first-mean
limsup is asserted only in the nonempty window (14.8). Its endpoint depends on the fixed
covariance and the chosen anchor b; no uniformity over covariance shapes is claimed. As
a ↓ 0, the comparison clock has exponent tending to one, the growth exponent tends
to κst , and the mean upper bound tends to L0 . The static second-moment constant is
exactly 2κst L0 .
    The gap concerns Eu, which is positive by its pairing series; no theorem for E|u| or
all moment orders is asserted. If an intended source first-mean lower rate L∗ (a) is valid,
then L∗ (a) ≤ M (a), so (14.9) also improves the corresponding Jensen rate 2L∗ (a) in
this window. This does not establish the original lower target 2κst L∗ (a), or either sharp
bound for arbitrary a. The later intrinsic formula in Theorem 40.6 does not identify
either printed source endpoint. The proof here uses the ordinary Stratonovich pairing
expansion, including self-contractions; no Skorohod result or sibling-project development
is made.

                        cp,k (a) ≤ cp,k (a1 )1−θ cp,k (a2 )θ ,         k ≥ 0.           (14.12)

Proof. Work first at a common positive spatial noise width ϵ and at unit horizon. For
each split m + n = 2k and pairing D, let ωϵ,m,n,D be the finite positive spatial Green–
Wick measure in the initial-chain convention of Proposition 3.1, with covariance γ2ϵ .
For p = 1 take the disjoint union over the single specified split (m, n) = (0, 2k) and all
pairings. For p = 2, take the disjoint union over every m = 0, . . . , 2k and all pairings.
In particular, the two empty-chain orientations are distinct terms for p = 2, whereas
p = 1 has just one. Wick’s formula has coefficient one for each pairing on each ordered
domain.
   With ωϵ,p,k denoting this disjoint-union measure, define its raw-time integral by
                                     ∫︂ ∏︂
                           ϵ
                          cp,k (a) =        |sj − sh |−a dωϵ,p,k .
                                          {j,h}∈D

The pairing label in the multiplier is that of its component. These integrals are finite:
the spatial covariances and Green masses are bounded on the unit horizons, and the
disjoint pair endpoints give integrable factors on the full time cube since a < 1. The
multiplier at a is exactly the product of the endpoint multipliers to powers 1 − θ, θ, off
null diagonals. Hölder’s inequality on the disjoint union gives

                               cϵp,k (a) ≤ cϵp,k (a1 )1−θ cϵp,k (a2 )θ .

   For each fixed positive endpoint, the all-chain bound (3.11) and the common
temporal-cutoff sandwich used in Lemma 14.1 identify the raw-time spatial limit
with the joint diagram value. This applies to every unequal or empty split, not only
to a mean diagram. At exponent zero, the multiplier is one and Lemma 3.5 gives
the same limit. The static diagonal and mixed-width Gaussian limits identify those
diagram values with the split expectations of the constructed static terms: products
converge in L1 by Cauchy–Schwarz, and the terminal-to-initial chain reversal is the

                                                 104
one in Proposition 3.1, applied before removing widths. For positive exponents this
identification is Proposition 3.1. The union is finite at fixed k; passing to the spatial
limit proves (14.12) and nonnegativity. No interpolation of exponent-dependent Laplace
prefixes is used.
    Every diagram has 2k time Jacobians, 2k Green scaling factors, k spatial covariance
factors and k temporal factors. Thus its homogeneous time degree is 4k − αk − ak =
β(a)k, including both empty-chain cases. For positive a, the full second-moment series
follows from Proposition 3.1; the mean series is (14.2). At zero, the static norm series
gives convergence of the finite partial means and squared moments. Each finite moment
expansion has nonnegative mixed terms, so its increasing limit may be regrouped by total
order 2k. The homogeneity just proved then gives (14.11). This proves Lemma 14.6.
   For p = 1, 2 define the actual normalized time growth rates

Proposition 14.7 (Log-convexity of the upper growth type). The functions Λp (a),
p = 1, 2, are positive and finite on 0 ≤ a < amax . For the endpoints and θ in Lemma 14.6,

                                Λp (a) ≤ Λp (a1 )1−θ Λp (a2 )θ .                     (14.14)

Proof. Put Cp = pκst L0 > 0. The imported static law gives Λp (0) = Λp (0) = Cp .

for p = 2. Cauchy–Schwarz gives finiteness for p = 1, whose mean is at least one by its
positive series. Hence all logarithms and rates used here are well defined, positive and
finite.

Proof. The lower estimate is Proposition 14.3. For the upper estimate, apply Proposi-
