Remark 14.9 (Order of limits and remaining sharp target). The static calibration now
concerns the constant, not only the time exponent. It is an iterated statement: first
take a time liminf or limsup at each fixed a, with its own normalization T −κ(a) , and
then send a ↓ 0. No uniformity over covariances or T , no simultaneous limit, and no
existence of the time limit at a fixed positive a follows. Only the upper rate is asserted
to be log-convex on the interior.
    Neither L∗ (a) nor L∗ (a) enters this argument. The constant L0 is the actual static
first-mean rate. Parameter continuity alone does not identify the conjectured curve. The
separate intrinsic second-moment value is proved in Theorem 40.6. No all-p, absolute-
first-moment, Skorohod, novelty or circulation result is claimed.

                                            110
Relative to the shared-clock value ρ = 1, the omitted cross-pair multiplier is exactly

correction rule at higher orders.
   The transforms K× use gap damping. For the auxiliary cutoff function
                                     ∫︂ T1 ∫︂ T2
                                                           min(s, t)
                      F (T1 , T2 ) =             |s − t|−a           ds dt,
                                       0     0                2
nonnegative Fubini adds 1/(θ1 θ2 ) to its double external-horizon transform. This function
is not an actual unequal-time solution correlation. Reversing chains ending at different
physical times instead shifts the cross covariance to |T1 − T2 − s + t|−a . Setting the two
damping parameters equal is also not the single Laplace transform of a diagonal-horizon
moment. For a self-pair at common gap damping, one external horizon instead adds
1/θ, recovering the known first-mean coefficient.
    The proposition rules out exactly the analytic covariance substitution in (15.4), with
product heat-chain measure, unrestricted operational-time rectangle and constant Brow-
nian correlation. Additional relative-order weights,other derived clock couplings,matrix
representations,modulus inequalities and their possible asymptotics are not covered. In
particular, the inequality at this first cross pair leaves open a shared-clock lower compar-
ison or asymptotic equivalence at higher orders. This finite clock calculation does not
identify the intrinsic value proved later in Theorem 40.6 with the source’s all-moment
conjecture. This is not a Skorohod result or a no-go for every two-clock method.


16     Covariance bounds for physically masked wave
       fields
We establish the covariance estimates needed for the operator construction. Throughout
this section, Hypothesis 1.1 holds, a = α0 , and Γ ̸= 0. For zero spatial covariance,
the spatial spectral measures and actual noise variances vanish; the purely temporal
estimates remain valid independently of Γ. We use the unitary Fourier transform
                                ∫︂
                     f (ξ) = cd
                     ˆ︁            e−iξ·x f (x) dx,  cd = (2π)−d/2 .
                                  Rd

The spectral convention in Hypothesis 1.1 means that, for every complex Schwartz test
φ,                 ∫︂               ∫︂ (︃∫︂              )︃
                           φ(x)Γ( dx) =               eiξ·x φ(x) dx µ( dξ).          (16.1)
                      Rd                  Rd     Rd
This is an identity of distribution pairings. We will not interchange two infinite measures
in the associated oscillatory expression.
    A positive tempered Radon measure has polynomial mass growth. Indeed, choose
a nonnegative compact smooth test that is at least one on the unit ball, and apply a
defining Schwartz seminorm bound for the distribution to its dilations. Its mass on a
ball of radius R ≥ 1 is bounded by a power of R. Summation over dyadic shells then

                                               111
gives integrability of (1 + |x|)−N for some N , and hence absolute integrability of every
Schwartz test. These facts apply to both Γ and µ. Smooth cutoffs converge in the
Schwartz topology, so these integrals agree with the tempered pairings.

Proof. Schwartz decay of kR and polynomial growth of µ show that ρR (η) is finite at every

For scalar Schwartz functions this follows by Fourier inversion and absolute integrability.

the full identity projection suffices; the zero space is immediate. The triangle inequality

by Schwartz decay and temperedness of νR . This proves Proposition 16.2.

L2 -dense family of compact smooth tests is consequently dense in Ha , proving separa-

complexification. Smooth density in L2 , together with the Schur bound on the absolute
bilinear form, extends the inclusion and the integral identity to L2 . This includes the
stated interval amplitudes. This proves Lemma 16.3.

    Proposition 16.2, applied with H = HaC , gives EW (ζR F )2 ≤ NR (F )2 . It annihilates
auxiliary-null tests. An auxiliary Cauchy sequence thus gives a Cauchy sequence of
actual noise evaluations in L2 (Ω), proving the contraction extension and its uniqueness.
Every finite linear combination of the limits is an L2 limit of centered Gaussian variables.
Their variances converge, so their characteristic functions prove that the limits remain
jointly centered Gaussian.
    For the finite family let A be the actual covariance       matrix and B the auxiliary
Gram matrix. Applying the norm inequality to i bi Fi gives bT Ab ≤ bT Bb for every
                                                     ∑︁
real vector b. Diagonalizing B − A constructs a centered Gaussian vector Z of that
covariance, independent of the actual evaluation vector X, on a product space. Then
X + Z has covariance B. Conditional Jensen gives E∥X∥p ≤ E∥X + Z∥p for every
norm and p ≥ 1. Finite Gaussian moments ensure finiteness of both sides. This proves
Proposition 16.4.
   The auxiliary completion depends on R and ζ. Its construction does not define
multiplication by ζR on the original noise quotient. Nor does the finite linear Gaussian
comparison assert a covariance comparison for nonlinear wave solutions. The auxiliary
and original test norms need not dominate one another. No spatial representative for
every auxiliary class, nonnegative physical inverse covariance for νR , or uniform density
bound in R is asserted.

Lemma 16.7 (Correlated trigonometric products). Let r, ρ ≥ 0. Let Pr be either cos(rt)
or sin(rt), and let Qρ be either cos(ρt) or sin(ρt). With the amplitude and constant above,

                       ∥1I APr Qρ ∥2a ≤ C0 (a)h2−a (M + hD)2 Qh (r, ρ),            (16.10)
                            Qh (r, ρ) := ⟨h(r − ρ)⟩a−1 + ⟨h(r + ρ)⟩a−1 .           (16.11)

Proof. Expanding the two trigonometric factors gives four complex exponential modes
with frequencies σr + τ ρ, σ, τ ∈ {−1, 1}, and coefficients of absolute value 1/4. In a
Hilbert space,          ⃦            ⃦2 (︄        )︄
                        ⃦∑︂          ⃦    ∑︂         ∑︂
                               cj vj ⃦ ≤     |cj |      |cj |∥vj ∥2 .
                        ⃦            ⃦
                        ⃦
                        ⃦            ⃦
                               j                   j          j

The first coefficient sum is one. Apply Lemma 16.6 to each mode. Two frequencies have
absolute value |r − ρ| and two have absolute value r + ρ. This gives one half of the
asserted right side, and hence the bound. Repeated or zero frequencies are permitted;
no orthogonality or independence of the modes is used. This proves Lemma 16.7.
Lemma 16.8 (Continuous sine factors). Define cr (t) = cos(rt) and

                                         sin(rt)
                          br (t) = ⟨r⟩             (r > 0),       b0 (t) = t.
                                            r
For Fr ∈ {cr , br } and Gρ ∈ {cρ , bρ },

       ∥1I Fr Gρ ∥2a ≤ C1 (a)(1 + T )4 h2−a Qh (r, ρ),C1 (a) = 4 · 31−a C0 (a). (16.12)
                                                               √
Proof. For r ≥ 1, br is a sine times a coefficient at most 2. For 0 ≤ r ≤ 1, the
fundamental theorem of calculus gives
                                           ∫︂ 1
                             br (t) = t⟨r⟩      cos(vrt) dv.
                                                       0

This includes r = 0. Hence, a product Fr Gρ is a scalar coefficient of absolute value
at most two times a normalized integral of tj Pr′ (t)Qρ′ (t), where j ∈ {0, 1, 2} and |r′ −
r|, |ρ′ − ρ| ≤ 1. The integral is over zero, one or two copies of [0, 1]; with no averages,

                                                   117
use a one-point probability space. Its parameter dependence is continuous in L2 (0, T )
by dominated convergence. Lemma 16.3 makes it continuous in Ha as well. Thus, the
averages are genuine Bochner integrals, and their Ha images agree with the pointwise
averages by continuity of the inclusion. Hilbert Jensen bounds the squared norm of an
average by the average of the squared norms.
    Apply Lemma 16.7 to each integrand with A(t) = tj . For j = 0, 1, 2, respectively,
M + hD is at most 1, T + 1 and T 2 + 2T . In every case (M + hD)2 ≤ (1 + T )4 . For real
x, e, the triangle inequality and its reverse imply

                               ⟨x⟩
                                     ≤ ⟨x + e⟩ ≤ (1 + |e|)⟨x⟩.
                             1 + |e|

The changes in h(r ± ρ) have magnitude at most two, since h ≤ 1. Because a − 1 < 0,
each altered bracket to the power a − 1 is at most 31−a times the original one. Apply
these comparisons, Hilbert Jensen and the squared scalar coefficient bound four to obtain
(16.12). This proves Lemma 16.8.
   These estimates concern covariance forms and temporal factors. Their actual two-
phase application is in Section 17; the subsequent operator, source and moment results
require their separate proofs. They do not follow from covariance domination alone.

17.3     Bessel’s inequality and the two variances
Lemma 17.4 (Hilbert-valued finite Bessel inequality). Let H be a separable complex
Hilbert space, let {uk } be a finite orthonormal family in L2 (Rd ; C2 ), and let g = (g1 , g2 ) ∈
L2 (Rd ; H 2 ). Then
                        ⃦∫︂                      ⃦2

                               uk,j (η)gj (η) dη ⃦ ≤       ∥gj (η)∥2H dη.                     (17.20)

                        ⃦
                        ⃦                        ⃦
                      k        j=1                          H      j=1


The same bound holds with uk,j in place of uk,j .

Proof. Expand g in a countable orthonormal basis of H. On each scalar coefficient apply
scalar Bessel to the orthonormal family {uk }, or to {uk } for the conjugated version.
Scalar finite Bessel follows by subtracting an orthogonal finite projection and expanding
its nonnegative squared norm. Sum over the H basis and use Parseval and nonnegative
Tonelli. The Bochner integrals exist by L2 Cauchy–Schwarz. A finite-dimensional H
uses its finite basis, and the zero space is immediate. No independence is involved. This
proves Lemma 17.4.

17.4     Same-noise mean-square columns
Write Erreal for the real phase space in dimension d.

Proposition 17.6 (Column and adjoint-column maps). On the same probability space
there are deterministic bounded real linear maps
                                          real
                           mR,I , nR,I : Es−γ  −→ L2 (Ω; Esreal ),

with squared bounds CT h2−a Bα (h), such that, for every fixed real Schwartz pair z, w,

                  ⟨mR,I z, w⟩E = ℓR (I; z, w) = ⟨z, nR,I w⟩E      in L2 (Ω).      (17.25)

Fixed-vector values and finite families of values are centered Gaussian.

Proof. Choose a countable real Schwartz orthonormal basis {ek } of Es . Such a ba-
sis follows by Gram–Schmidt from a countable dense Schwartz family, discarding zero
residuals. Schwartz density follows by smooth Fourier cutoff approximation in the
weighted L2 norms: approximate by compact smooth Fourier coordinates, replace g(ξ)
by (g(ξ) + g(−ξ))/2, and apply the inverse Bessel/Fourier maps. The resulting phases
are real Schwartz, and Gram–Schmidt preserves that class. The same density holds in
Es−γ .
    For fixed real Schwartz z, define
                         ∑︂                              ∑︂
                mN (z) =     ℓR (I; z, ek )ek , nN (z) =    ℓR (I; ek , z)ek .
                        k≤N                                 k≤N

Both are finite Gaussian sums on the original W . By Proposition 17.5, their coefficient
expected squares have finite sum. Orthogonality gives the squared tail norms as the
corresponding sums of expected squares. Thus, both sequences converge in L2 (Ω; Es ) to


                                            125
m(z), n(z) with the asserted bounds, without independence of the coefficients. Finite-
sum measurability and separable Es give strong measurability of the limits. The bounded
real linear maps extend uniquely from Schwartz phases to Es−γ .
    If wN is the Es projection of a Schwartz w onto the first N basis vectors, then
⟨mN (z), w⟩E = ℓR (I; z, wN ). The singleton form of (17.22), after normalizing w − wN in
Es , gives
               E|ℓR (I; z, w − wN )|2 ≤ CT h2−a Bα (h)∥z∥2Es−γ ∥w − wN ∥2Es .
The zero residual is immediate. The right side tends to zero, giving the first identity
in (17.25). For the second, project z instead and use the singleton input bound (17.21).
A fixed countable Schwartz orthonormal basis and Parseval show basis independence of
the constructed limits.
    Continuous linear functionals of the limits, and finite linear combinations of such
functionals, are L2 limits of centered Gaussian variables with convergent variances. Their
characteristic functions therefore converge to those of centered Gaussians. The same
reasoning after the bounded input extension proves joint Gaussianity for arbitrary fixed
inputs and finite families of the two maps. This proves Proposition 17.6.
   These are maps into L2 (Ω; Es ) for fixed intervals. No common uncountable almost-
sure version, random bounded operator, Hilbert–Schmidt realization, time path, source
convergence or new solution-moment bound is inferred by this section alone. Both
variance directions are retained in Section 19.


18     Finite Gaussian moments                              and      whole-space
       Sobolev compactness
We prove the finite Gaussian and compact-cutoff estimates needed to construct the
wave driver. All traces and Schatten norms in this section are unnormalized. The spatial
states remain on Rd , d ∈ {1, 2, 3}. Here p is an auxiliary Schatten and Gaussian-moment
index. The auxiliary periodic space below is used only to factor a compactly supported
multiplication operator. No infinite Gaussian series or time path is constructed in this
section.

18.1     Finite Gaussian matrices with both variance powers
Lemma 18.1 (Scalar power estimates). For 0 < θ < 1 and u, v ≥ 0,
                                uθ v 1−θ ≤ θu + (1 − θ)v.                          (18.1)
Consequently, if L > 0 and 0 ≤ q ≤ L are integers, then for real x, y,
                                            q L L−q L
                            |x|q |y|L−q ≤     |x| +   |y| .                        (18.2)
                                            L       L
For nonnegative finite sequences, the usual Hölder inequality holds with exponents m and
m/(m − 1), m > 1. If Z ≥ 0 has finite expectation F and 0 < r < 1, then EZ r ≤ F r .

                                             126
Proof. If v > 0, divide (18.1) by v and put t = u/v. The function θt+1−θ −tθ decreases
to zero at one and then increases, by its derivative θ(1 − tθ−1 ). Continuity covers t = 0;
if v = 0 the inequality is immediate. For 0 < q < L, apply this with u = |x|L , v = |y|L
and θ = q/L. The endpoint cases q = 0, L are identities, with the ordinary integer
convention a0 = 1.
    To prove finite Hölder, divide the two sequences by their respective ℓm and ℓm/(m−1)
norms. If either norm vanishes, the result is immediate. Apply (18.1) with θ = 1/m
to the corresponding normalized powers and sum; the sum of products is at most one.
Undo the normalization. Finally, F = 0 gives Z = 0 almost surely. If F > 0, apply
(18.1) to u = Z/F , v = 1 and θ = r, and integrate. The right side has expectation one.
This proves Lemma 18.1.
Lemma 18.2 (Two Hermitian trace bounds). Let B, Y be finite Hermitian matrices,
m ≥ 2 an integer, and L = 2m − 2. For integers 0 ≤ q ≤ L,

The two weights sum to one, proving (18.3). No commutation of B and Y is used.
   In the same basis put di = D∑︁  ii ≥ 0. If θj are the nonnegative eigenvalues of D, a

Remark 18.4 (Both variance directions are necessary). Let n = 16∑︁and Ak = eTk be the

Here Vout = n and Vin = In . Keeping only the input variance in (18.6) would give fourth
power 9 Tr In2 = 144, which is false. Transposing the family refutes the corresponding
output-only bound. The actual average and maximum bounds have fourth powers 1224
and 2304, respectively. This finite example asserts nothing about a specialized wave
estimate.

full-Lebesgue Schur argument proves smooth compact multiplication bounded on every
whole-space H r .
    For Schwartz u, the periodic Fourier series of ψu converges smoothly on the cube.

χ recovers χu with no alias term. Both sides are bounded from H σ+γ (Rd ) to H σ (Rd ),
using the just-proved maps, the inclusion and smooth multiplication. Density proves
(18.9) on that full domain. This proves Lemma 18.5.

Replacing the inclusion by Qn Iγ gives finite-rank Kn converging in norm to Kχ,σ,γ . This
proves compactness: finite-dimensional images have finite nets of each positive radius,
and norm approximation transfers those nets to the limiting image of the unit ball.
   Put
            M = ∥Eχ : H σ (TL ) → H σ (Rd )∥∥Pψ : H σ+γ (Rd ) → H σ+γ (TL )∥.

    No uniform support-radius constant or endpoint necessity is asserted in Proposi-

18.3       Balanced adjoints and localized variance envelopes
For E = H 1/2 (Rd ) × H −1/2 (Rd ) use the physical unitary coordinates
                              J0 (u, v) = (Λ1/2 u, Λ−1/2 v),           H = L2 (Rd ; C2 ).
The frequency map J in Section 17 is (F ⊕ F)J0 . Let Dχ multiply both phase entries
and define
                                 Pχ = J0 Dχ J0−1 ,    Dγ = diag(Λ−γ , Λ−γ ),                    (18.13)
                                Kout = Pχ Dγ ,     Kin = Pχ∗ Dγ .                               (18.14)

                                                        131
Proposition 18.7 (Both compact envelopes). The operator Pχ is bounded. For the
unitary swap U (v, w) = (w, v),

                              Pχ∗ = U Pχ U,         Kin = U Kout U.

Both factors are compact and have equal Sp norms for p ≥ 1, pγ > d. For p ≥ 2, pγ > d,
the positive compact operators

                 Bout = Pχ Dγ2 Pχ∗ = Kout Kout
                                           ∗
                                               ,         Bin = Pχ∗ Dγ2 Pχ = Kin Kin
                                                                                 ∗


satisfy
                                     p/2           p/2
                                Tr Bout = Tr Bin = ∥Kout ∥pSp .                       (18.15)

Proof. Put Aσ = Λσ χΛ−σ . The full-Lebesgue Schur argument in Lemma 18.5 shows
boundedness for every real σ. On Schwartz functions, self-adjointness of real multipli-
cation and of the Bessel powers gives A∗σ = A−σ . Density and boundedness extend this
to the actual Hilbert adjoints. No evenness of χ is needed. Thus,

               Pχ = diag(A1/2 , A−1/2 ),       Pχ∗ = diag(A−1/2 , A1/2 ) = U Pχ U.

The swap commutes with Dγ , giving the claimed factor identity. The blocks of Kout
are Kχ,1/2,γ and Kχ,−1/2,γ . Proposition 18.6 gives compactness and Sp membership. The
singular values of their direct sum form the multiset union, so

                          ∥Kout ∥pSp = ∥Kχ,1/2,γ ∥pSp + ∥Kχ,−1/2,γ ∥pSp .

Unitary conjugation preserves them. Finally, the positive eigenvalues of KK ∗ and K√
                                                                                   ∗
                                                                                     K
agree with
         √ multiplicity: on an eigenspace with eigenvalue λ > 0, the maps K/ λ
and K ∗ / λ are inverse isometries. Zero eigenvalues contribute nothing to the trace.
Summing these scalar powers proves (18.15). This proves Proposition 18.7.
   We use the usual positive square root of a bounded positive operator and the spectral
decomposition of compact positive operators. The needed order argument is recorded
explicitly.
Lemma 18.8 (Compact positive order and trace). If A, B are bounded self-adjoint
operators with 0 ≤ A ≤ B and B compact positive, then A is compact and its ordered
eigenvalues satisfy λj (A) ≤ λj (B), including zero padding. Consequently, for t ≥ 1 with
finite Tr B t ,
                                       Tr At ≤ Tr B t .                           (18.16)
Proof. Let Qn be finite spectral projections of B whose tails tend to zero in norm. Then

          ∥(1 − Qn )A1/2 ∥2 = ∥(1 − Qn )A(1 − Qn )∥ ≤ ∥(1 − Qn )B(1 − Qn )∥ −→ 0.

Thus, A1/2 is a norm limit of finite-rank operators and is compact, as is A. For compact
positive operators, the max-min formula for λj follows in an eigenbasis: the span of the
first j eigenvectors gives the lower bound for the supremum over j-dimensional subspaces

                                               132
of their minimum Rayleigh quotient, while every such subspace meets the orthogonal
complement of the first j−1 eigenvectors, giving the upper bound. Compare the Rayleigh
quotients of A and B to obtain the eigenvalue inequality. Sum the ordered scalar powers
to prove (18.16). No inequality At ≤ B t is asserted. This proves Lemma 18.8.
Proposition 18.9 (Localized finite variance bounds). Suppose finitely many bounded
operators Zℓ on H satisfy
                                 ∑︂                        ∑︂
 Pχ Zℓ = Zℓ = Zℓ Pχ ,    Vout :=    Zℓ Zℓ∗ ≤ CDγ2 , Vin :=    Zℓ∗ Zℓ ≤ CDγ2 , (18.17)
                                        ℓ                              ℓ

with C ≥ 0. If p ≥ 2 and pγ > d, every Zℓ is compact in Sp , the two variances are
compact in Sp/2 , and

                                            Vout ≤ CBout ,    Vin ≤ CBin ,         (18.18)
                           p/2             p/2
                      Tr Vout ,       Tr Vin ≤ C p/2 ∥Kout ∥pSp .                  (18.19)

Proof. Left localization gives Vout = Pχ Vout Pχ∗ . Apply positive congruence to the output
bound in (18.17) to get Vout ≤ CPχ Dγ2 Pχ∗ = CBout . Right localization similarly gives
Vin = Pχ∗ Vin Pχ ≤ CBin . Proposition 18.7 and Lemma 18.8 prove compactness and the
two trace bounds with t = p/2. If C = 0, both variances and every Zℓ vanish, so all
assertions are immediate.
    For each ℓ, Zℓ Zℓ∗ ≤ Vout is compact by the same lemma. Its finite spectral projections
Qn give ∥(1−Qn )Zℓ ∥2 = ∥(1−Qn )Zℓ Zℓ∗ (1−Qn )∥ → 0. Hence, Zℓ is compact. Its singular
values squared are the nonzero eigenvalues of Zℓ Zℓ∗ . Comparing with Vout and summing
                                     p/2
proves Zℓ ∈ Sp with ∥Zℓ ∥pSp ≤ Tr Vout . This proves Proposition 18.9.
   The two localization identities are hypotheses here; an actual noise coefficient family
has not yet been shown to satisfy them. In particular, Pχ is not assumed self-adjoint or
a projection.
Corollary 18.10 (Finite Gaussian operators). Under (18.17), let gℓ be∑︁independent real
standard Gaussians. For even p ≥ 2 with pγ > d, the finite sum X = ℓ gℓ Zℓ satisfies
                                              √
                        (E∥X∥pSp )1/p ≤ p − 1 C∥Kout ∥Sp .
                                       √︁
                                                                               (18.20)

Its extended norm is measurable, with finite expectation as displayed.
Proof. Choose finite-rank orthogonal projections Πn from a∑︁   countable orthonormal basis
of H, with Πn → I strongly. Put Zℓ,n = Πn Zℓ Πn and Xn = ℓ gℓ Zℓ,n , viewed as matrices
on ran Πn . Their output variance satisfies
                             ∑︂
                                Πn Zℓ Πn Zℓ∗ Πn ≤ Πn Vout Πn ,
                                  ℓ

since the difference is ℓ Πn Zℓ (1 − Πn )Zℓ∗ Πn ≥ 0. The input inequality is identical with
                      ∑︁
adjoints interchanged. Compact max-min also gives λj (Πn V Πn ) ≤ λj (V ) for positive

                                                  133
compact V , since Rayleigh subspaces inside ran Πn form a subset of all subspaces. Thus,
both variance trace powers of these matrices are bounded by C p/2 ∥Kout ∥pSp .
   Proposition 18.3 applies to these finite complex matrices, the same real independent
Gaussians and the even p. Taking the average of the two already bounded trace powers
gives
                          E∥Xn ∥pSp ≤ (p − 1)p/2 C p/2 ∥Kout ∥pSp .              (18.21)
No factor two is added by this average.
   For each Gaussian coordinate vector, X is a finite sum of compact operators. Strong
convergence of Πn implies Πn XΠn → X in operator norm: approximate X by a finite-
rank F , use uniform strong convergence on the finite-dimensional ranges of F and F ∗ ,
and control the remainder using ∥Πn ∥ ≤ 1. Singular values are norm-Lipschitz. Indeed,
their formula
                            sj (A) = inf      sup      ∥Av∥
                                        dim L<j v∈L⊥ , ∥v∥=1

follows from compact singular vectors and gives sj (A) ≤ sj (B) + ∥A − B∥, and the
reverse bound by interchanging A, B. Hence, sj (Xn ) → sj (X) for each j. Fatou for the
nonnegative singular-value sum gives ∥X∥pSp ≤ lim inf n ∥Xn ∥pSp , allowing extended infin-
ity. These variables are measurable: the finite Gaussian coordinate map into operators is
norm-continuous, each singular value is continuous, and the norm power is their nonneg-
ative measurable sum. Fatou in probability and (18.21) prove the bound and almost-sure
finiteness. No Sp -Bochner convergence is assumed. This proves Corollary 18.10.

18.4     A nonzero witness and the need for a compact cutoff

    Thus, the choice χ = 1 cannot be substituted into the compact-cutoff argument. No
necessity at the Schatten endpoint pγ = d is asserted. The results concern finite matrices
and conditional finite operator sums. Actual coefficient localization, compact variance
tails, an infinite Gaussian series and time regularity are proved in Section 19; source
and Young identification are proved in Sections 20–22. They are not consequences of
the finite comparison alone.


19     The actual Gaussian operator driver
We now construct the first interaction driver on the same probability space as the original
noise. Retain Hypothesis 1.1, set a = α0 , s = 1/2 and Er = H r (Rd ) × H r−1 (Rd ), and
initially use real phases E = Es . Let HW be the real completion of Cc∞ ((0, ∞) × Rd )
modulo its covariance-null tests. The given isonormal process on it is W . If Γ = 0, the
driver and all raw forms are zero, so the conclusions below hold directly. Assume Γ ̸= 0
in the construction.
    Choose and fix R, T > 0 and a real even nonnegative ζ ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ) equal to one on
B1 and zero off B2 . The physical mask is ζR (x) = ζ(x/R). The free group S(t) and
forms ℓR are those of Section 17; both phase entries and the continuous zero-frequency
sine quotient are retained. Fix parameters
                    (︃        )︃                        (︃                 )︃

       0 < γ < min       ,       ,      < θ < β < min 1 − ,                   .      (19.1)

The strict intervals are nonempty. The C θ norm is the supremum norm plus the θ-Hölder
seminorm.

{fj } of HW with real smooth compact test representatives, independent standard real

19.1     A test basis and literal covariance coefficients
Lemma 19.2 (Basis in the prescribed quotient). The nonzero space HW is separable
and infinite-dimensional. It has a real orthonormal basis with compact smooth represen-
tatives, whose evaluations by W are independent standard real Gaussians.
Proof. For tests supported in [0, L] × K, with K compact, the physical covariance and
the temporal Schur bound give the absolute estimate
                                    2L1−a
       |⟨F, G⟩HW | ≤ |K|Γ(K − K)          ∥∥F (t)∥∞ ∥L2 (0,L) ∥∥G(t)∥∞ ∥L2 (0,L) .    (19.5)
                                    1−a
Only spatial differences in K − K contribute. On each rational compact box inside
positive time, rational step approximations followed by compact smooth mollifiers at ra-
tional scales give a countable uniformly dense test collection, supported in a fixed slightly
larger box. The estimate proves noise-norm approximation. The union over increasing
boxes is dense in the test completion. This proves separability without assuming the
covariance form is injective.
    Choose L ≥ 1 with 0 < Γ(BL ) < ∞ and nonnegative compact smooth q ≥ 1 on
B2L+1 . Then
                         ∫︂        ∫︂
                  Qsp := Γ( dy) q(x)q(x − y) dx ≥ |BL+1 |Γ(BL ) > 0,

and the compact-support estimate makes it finite. For every real compact smooth

                                  ∥τ ⊗ q∥2HW = Qsp ∥τ ∥2a .
Here the temporal norm is computed on any finite interval containing the support of τ .
It is strictly positive for signed nonzero τ . Indeed, zero-extend this deterministic test
and use the positive Laplace factorization from Lemma 16.3 to write

Signed exchanges are absolutely integrable on the test support, by the temporal Schur
estimate. If the norm vanished, some λ > 0 would have Eλ = 0. Its inner integral v(r) is
continuous and satisfies v ′ = λv − τ . Vanishing almost everywhere makes it identically
zero and forces τ = 0. Thus, infinitely many linearly independent compact time bumps
inject into HW after multiplication by q.
    Apply Gram–Schmidt to a countable dense real test collection, discarding zero resid-
uals in the given null quotient. Infinitude gives an infinite basis; each representative
is a finite linear combination of compact smooth tests. The isonormal identity gives
Egj gk = δjk . Joint Gaussian characteristic functions then factor, giving independence
and unit variances. No physical null direction is removed beyond the prescribed quotient,
and no HW -valued white Gaussian vector is used. This proves Lemma 19.2.

Lemma 19.3 (Actual covariance coefficients). For each basis representative put
                                 ∫︂            ∫︂
                                            −a
            Hj,R (t, x) = ζR (x)    |t − u|       Γ( dy)fj (u, x − y) du.      (19.6)

These functions are real smooth on finite time-space compacts, supported spatially in
B2R , with bounded derivatives there. For smooth compact real positive-time F , and also
for the admitted wave tests of (17.13),
                                         ∫︂
                       E[WC (ζR F )gj ] = Hj,R (t, x)F (t, x) dt dx.              (19.7)

The coefficient on t ≥ 0 depends only on the HW class of fj .

Proof. Extend ∫︁ fj −a
                    smoothly
                       ∫︁      by zero in time and substitute v = t − u. The unmasked
expression is R |v|       Γ( dy)fj (t−v, x−y) dv. On each compact (t, x) set, the integrand
involves only bounded v and a common compact set of y. Local Radon mass and
local integrability of |v|−a allow every mixed derivative to move onto the smooth test.
Dominated integration proves smoothness and the derivative bounds. Multiplication by
ζR gives the stated support. Zero extension is only of a deterministic test; no negative-
time noise is introduced.
    The physical covariance formula and absolute Fubini give (19.7) first for real compact
tests. The compact difference set in Γ and integrable temporal singularity justify the ex-
change. For wave tests use the common-support raw approximations of Proposition 17.3,
converging in L2 time in the spatial supremum norm after masking. The left side con-
verges by noise Cauchy–Schwarz and the right side by the coefficient’s boundedness on
that support. Real and imaginary parts give the complex version. If two representatives
give the same class, their Gaussian difference is zero. Testing the covariance identity
against every compact smooth F makes the difference of their masked coefficients zero as
a distribution for t > 0. Smoothness, then continuity at zero, proves the representative
assertion on t ≥ 0. This proves Lemma 19.3.




                                           137
19.2     Gain, adjoints and actual localization
Lemma 19.4 (Deterministic coefficient operators). For I ⊂ [0, T ], the statewise integral
                                ∫︂
                     Aj (I)z = S(−t)B(Hj,R (t))S(t)z dt                            (19.8)
                                        I

satisfies, for every real r,
                               ∥Aj (I)∥B(Er ,Er+1 ) ≤ Cj,r,R,T |I|.                 (19.9)
Its Es adjoint has the gain E2s−r−1 → E2s−r , so both operators act from Es−γ to E.
Choose real compact smooth χ = 1 on B2R+T +1 . On every Er ,
                                Dχ Aj (I) = Aj (I) = Aj (I)Dχ .                    (19.10)
For each fixed Schwartz pair,
                               E[gj ℓR (I; z, w)] = ⟨Aj (I)z, w⟩E .                (19.11)
Proof. The full massless wave is strongly continuous and bounded on every Er on finite
time intervals. Its normalized off-diagonal Fourier entries have sizes at most CT and
one, respectively; continuity and domination prove the strong assertion. Multiplication
by Hj,R (t) is bounded on every H r , continuously in t, by weighted Fourier convolution
and its compact smooth derivative bounds. Hence, B(Hj,R (t)) : Er → Er+1 is bounded:
its second entry is a multiplier of the displacement in H r . The integrand in (19.8) is
strongly continuous with values in Er+1 for each input in Er , and uniformly bounded in
that operator norm. Statewise Bochner integration proves (19.9), including the Lipschitz
operator-norm primitive Aj (0, t). Norm continuity of S(t) is not needed.
    Under the Es pairing, the dual of Er is isometrically E2s−r , directly by the two
Bessel weights. Dualizing the gain-one bound therefore gives the asserted adjoint gain.
It agrees on Schwartz phases with the actual bounded Es adjoint. Since γ < 1, gain one
at every index implies the two stated maps from Es−γ to E.
    For smooth data the free-wave energy on a shrinking ball has nonpositive boundary
flux: v∂n u − (v 2 + |∇u|2 )/2 ≤ 0. Vanishing initial displacement and velocity give zero
velocity and gradient in the cone; integration of velocity from the zero displacement gives
zero displacement there as well. Time reversal gives the backward assertion. Thus, both
entries of S(±t) enlarge supports by at most |t|. Density and testing against compact
smooth functions extend this support assertion to Sobolev phases. In particular, for
input (1 − Dχ )z, approximate z by Schwartz phases and retain the factor 1 − Dχ ; the
approximants vanish on B2R+T +1 . Their free displacement vanishes on B2R throughout
[0, T ], and the same is true in the distributional limit. They contribute nothing to the
integral. Its output is supported in B2R+T , by the support of Hj,R and backward finite
propagation. Since χ = 1 on a neighborhood of this ball, both identities in (19.10)
follow. This uses both entries, not a scalar restart or a positivity theorem.
    Finally apply the physical Sobolev pairing (17.15) and (19.7) to the state integral.
They give (19.11). In (19.6), the mask is after convolution; replacing it by a mask inside
Γ ∗ fj would change this identity. This proves Lemma 19.4.

    Complexification preserves these statements and constants. The Hilbert norms split
into real and imaginary squared norms; real operators extend isometrically. A real or-
thonormal basis complexifies to a complex orthonormal basis, preserving singular values
and multiplicities. The complex column maps have the same bounds and sesquilinear
row identities. This proves Lemma 19.5.
Lemma 19.6 (Compact domination of positive tails). If 0 ≤ TN ≤ B, with positive
compact B ∈ Sm , m ≥ 1, and TN → 0 weakly, then

                              ∥TN ∥ −→ 0,          Tr TNm −→ 0.                    (19.16)

Proof. Choose a finite spectral projection Q of B with ∥(1 − Q)B(1 − Q)∥ < ϵ. Positive
quadratic Cauchy–Schwarz gives, for unit x,

        ⟨TN x, x⟩ ≤ 2⟨TN Qx, Qx⟩ + 2⟨TN (1 − Q)x, (1 − Q)x⟩ ≤ 2∥QTN Q∥ + 2ϵ.

Weak convergence makes the finite-dimensional matrix QTN Q converge in norm to zero.
Letting ϵ ↓ 0 proves norm convergence. Lemma 18.8 gives λj (TN ) ≤ λj (B), while each
λj (TN ) → 0 by norm convergence. Dominated summation of λj (B)m proves the trace
limit. No order comparison of the operator powers is used. This proves Lemma 19.6.

19.4     Actual interval limits on the given probability space
Lemma 19.8 (Fixed-interval operator construction). For each fixed short interval I,
XN (I) converges in every finite Lp (Ω; B(E)) to a compact-valued centered Gaussian op-
erator XR (I) on the original probability space. For every admissible even q ≥ 2, qγ > d,
                                √︁
    (E∥XR (I)∥qSq )1/q ≤ q − 1 CT h1−a/2 Bα (h)1/2 ∥Kχ ∥Sq ,
                        √︁
                                                                 Kχ = Pχ Dγ .     (19.18)

Its fixed Schwartz rows equal the actual raw forms in L2 .
Proof. The actual finite families aj , j ≤ N , now satisfy every hypothesis of Propo-
sition 18.9 and Corollary 18.10: both localizations, both weighted variances, and the
independent real gj have been proved. Thus, their coefficients are compact, and the
finite sums satisfy the bound in (19.18), uniformly in N . The fixed cutoff factor may
depend on R, T .
    Apply Lemma 19.6 to (19.14), whose fixed-interval envelopes belong to Sq/2 . Both
tail trace powers tend to zero. For M > N , the two variances of M
                                                                       ∑︁
                                                                         j=N +1 gj Aj (I) are
bounded by the corresponding tails. Lemma 19.7 and eigenvalue max-min show that
its expected Sq norm to the qth power tends to zero, uniformly in M > N . Since the
operator norm is at most the Sq norm, this gives Lq (B(E)) Cauchy convergence. This
Cauchy estimate uses the vanishing tail traces.
    All finite sums lie in the separable closed real span of the countably many determin-
istic Aj (I) in B(E). For an elementary realization of the limit, choose a subsequence
with summable Lq norms of successive differences. Their L1 norms are summable, so
the pointwise operator-norm differences sum absolutely almost surely. Completeness
gives a limit in that span; Fatou and the Cauchy property give convergence of the whole
sequence in Lq . This proves strong measurability on the same original space, with no
new noise. Different admissible even indices give the same limit by uniqueness of con-
vergence in probability. Choosing a larger even index yields convergence for every finite
operator-norm moment p ≥ 1.
    An almost surely norm-convergent subsequence makes the limit compact. Singular-
value Fatou gives (19.18). Every continuous linear functional is an L2 limit of centered
Gaussians, as are finite combinations, so the limit is jointly centered Gaussian. The
fixed matrix rows of XN (I) are the orthogonal projections of the literal raw forms onto
the complete noise basis. They converge to those forms in L2 . Operator-norm L2
convergence identifies their other limit with ⟨XR (I)z, w⟩E .

                                            141
   For a long interval, partition it into finitely many intervals of length at most one.
Their indicators add to the long indicator except at finitely many endpoints, which have
zero raw norm by the common-support temporal estimate. Raw completion and linearity
give exact additivity of the forms. Strong-integral additivity gives it for every XN and
hence for the operator limits. No long-h version of the short-interval bound is used.
This proves Lemma 19.8.

19.5     Time-path convergence and Gaussian norm bounds
Lemma 19.9 (Actual Hölder path limit). The finite paths XN (t) = XN ((0, t)) converge
in Lp (Ω; C θ ([0, T ]; B(E))) for every finite p ≥ 1, in a separable closed path span on the
same probability space. The limit starts at zero and has compact values for all times on
                                                                               √
one path event. For sufficiently large even q0 , its C θ norm has Lq bound C q, uniformly
over even q ≥ q0 and the basis.
Proof. Choose θ < η < β and an even q0 ≥ 2 with q0 γ > d and β − η − 1/q0 > 0. For
q ≥ q0 , the singular-value sequence gives ∥Kχ ∥Sq ≤ ∥Kχ ∥Sq0 : if the latter is nonzero,
normalize by it, so every singular value is at most one, and sum its qth powers. The zero
case is immediate. Apply the finite Gaussian operator estimate with the time-refined
actual variances from Proposition 17.5. This gives, uniformly in N and even q ≥ q0 ,
                                               √
                ∥XN (t) − XN (u)∥Lq (B(E)) ≤ C q |t − u|β ,    |t − u| ≤ 1.       (19.19)

The constant includes the fixed Sq0 cutoff norm and does not depend on q. Longer
increments are finite sums of unit increments, with constants depending on T .
    Let Dn (F ) be the maximum of adjacent increments on the grid kT 2−n , 0 ≤ k ≤ 2n .
At fine levels T 2−n ≤ 1, the elementary bound for a finite maximum yields
                                                 √
                            ∥Dn (XN )∥Lq ≤ 2n/q C q (T 2−n )β .

No independence of these increments is used. The finitely many coarse levels are con-
trolled by unit partitions. Minkowski and a geometric sum therefore give
                                ⃦               ⃦
                                ⃦∑︂             ⃦       √
                            sup ⃦   2nη Dn (XN )⃦ ≤ Cη q,                     (19.20)
                                ⃦               ⃦
                             N ⃦                ⃦
                                  n≥0              Lq

since the fine-level ratio is at most 2−(β−η−1/q0 ) < 1.
    For a continuous Banach-valued F starting at zero, this weighted dyadic sum controls
its C η norm. Approximate two times by dyadic points at a scale comparable to their
separation. The two grid points differ by at most two adjacent increments, and each
endpoint’s finer binary corrections are bounded by the tail sum of Dn . Multiplying
by the separation to the power −η bounds that total by the weighted sum, up to a
constant depending on T, η. Continuity identifies the endpoint limits and also controls
the supremum norm from F (0) = 0. The finite paths are Lipschitz by (19.9). Thus,
(19.20) gives uniform Lq C η bounds, and the triangle inequality gives the same bound
for their differences.

                                            142
   On a fixed finite time grid, Lemma 19.8 gives Lq convergence of every point value.
For continuous F ,
                       ∥F ∥∞ ≤ max ∥F (tk )∥ + C(mesh)η ∥F ∥C η .
                                   k

Apply this to XN − XM , send N, M → ∞ at a fixed grid, and then send the mesh to
zero. The uniform C η bound proves sup-norm Lq Cauchy convergence. The deterministic
interpolation inequality
                                                      θ/η
                             ∥F ∥C θ ≤ C∥F ∥1−θ/η
                                            ∞     ∥F ∥C η                   (19.21)
follows by bounding each increment by both 2∥F ∥∞ and its C η bound, and splitting at
their balancing scale. Its supremum term is controlled too. Scalar Hölder in probability
proves the Lq C θ Cauchy property. For any requested finite p, choose an even q ≥
max(p, q0 ) and use Lyapunov.
    The full Hölder/operator space need not be separable. Nevertheless, every finite
path lies in the span of the countably many deterministic Lipschitz paths Aj (0, ·).
Their C θ -closed real span is a separable Banach space. In it the same elementary
Cauchy/subsequence construction as in Lemma 19.8 gives a strongly measurable limit
MR on the original space. Different p limits agree by uniqueness of convergence in
probability in the path norm. An almost surely C θ -convergent subsequence converges
uniformly in operator norm. Finite paths have compact values on short intervals, and on
long ones by finite additivity. Therefore, the limit has compact values at every time on
that single path set. It starts at zero. Passing the uniform bounds from (19.20) through
                                              √
the path convergence proves the asserted C q bound. This proves Lemma 19.9.
Proof of Proposition 19.1. Lemmas 19.2–19.9 construct the stated finite sums and their
path limit, with no new probability space. Evaluation at a fixed time agrees with the
interval limit by uniqueness in probability. Its raw row identity is (19.3), by Lemma 19.8,
including the long-interval interpretation. The same additivity identifies each fixed in-
crement with XR (I), proving its stated Schatten moment bound.
    The finite paths are Gaussian linear combinations of deterministic paths. Every
continuous linear functional on their closed path span converges in L2 to a centered
Gaussian; finite linear combinations do as well. Thus, MR is a centered Gaussian path.
The four real pair identities give its complexified sesquilinear row identity.
    The construction used only compact smooth representatives, orthonormality and
completeness, so it applies to any such noise basis. Compare two constructions using
a fixed countable dense Schwartz phase collection and rational times. Both rows equal
the same original raw forms. A countable null-set union, boundedness of the operators,
phase density and path continuity give equality at all times on one probability-one set.
This proves indistinguishability without an assumed common event for uncountably
many raw evaluations.
    For p ≥ 1, choose even q ≥ max(p, q0 ) with q ≤ p + q0 + 2 ≤ (q0 + 3)p. Lemma 19.9
and Lyapunov give the first bound in (19.4). Write its resulting constant as C1 and
let Y = ∥MR ∥C θ . If C1 = 0, then Y =√ 0 almost surely and the exponential bound
is immediate. Otherwise, EY 2m ≤ (C1 2m)2m . Since m! ≥ (m/e)m , as follows by
integrating log x from one to m, the mth term in the exponential series is at most

                                           143
(2ecC12 )m . For 0 < c < 1/(2eC12 ), positive Tonelli and the geometric series prove the
second bound. This proves Proposition 19.1.
    All constants here may depend on R, T and the fixed admissible parameters. No
uniform-in-R path-norm estimate or C θ Sq -valued path is asserted. This constructs the
local masked first driver on the original noise. Source-cutoff convergence, Young evolu-
tion, canonical initial data and solution-moment asymptotics remain separate parts of
the argument.


20     Convergence of the canonical Gaussian source
       drivers
We match the heat normalization, Laplace prefix and joint source law in the author
version [4, (1.20), (1.21), (2.9), (2.11), pp. 5–6, 8–9]. Given-noise retention, the Radon
implementation and the operator-path conclusions are proved here. No first-moment
variational theorem is an input.
    Retain Hypothesis 1.1, the fixed R, T, ζR , E and parameters of (19.1), and the original
noise space HW . Write Bθ = C θ ([0, T ]; B(E)). Fix one raw test∑︁ basis fj and its actual
coefficient paths Aj (t) from Proposition 19.1. Thus, MN = j≤N W (fj )Aj → MR in
every finite Lp (Bθ ). Zero covariance gives zero noises and drivers throughout; assume
Γ ̸= 0 in the construction.

20.1     Gaussian transfer in the full path norm
Lemma 20.1 (Finite Gaussian linear images). Let v1 , . . . , vN lie in a real Banach space
and let ξ be a centered Gaussian vector with covariance bounded by CI, C ≥ 0. For
independent standard real gj and p ≥ 1,
                     (︄ ⃦         ⃦p )︄1/p    (︄ ⃦         ⃦p )︄1/p
                        ⃦∑︂       ⃦         √    ⃦∑︂       ⃦
                            ξj vj ⃦        ≤ C E⃦    gj vj ⃦        .                (20.1)
                        ⃦         ⃦              ⃦         ⃦
                       E⃦
                        ⃦         ⃦              ⃦         ⃦
                           j                           j


Proof. The function φ(x) = ∥ j xj vj ∥p is continuous and convex, even when the lin-
                                ∑︁

ear image gives only a seminorm on RN . Its Gaussian expectations are finite by the
finite-sum bound. On an auxiliary space take an independent centered Gaussian Z of
covariance CI −
              √Cov ξ. Conditional Jensen gives Eφ(ξ) ≤ Eφ(ξ + Z). The latter vector
has the law of C g, proving the claim. The case C = 0 is immediate. This auxiliary
comparison changes neither the actual coupling of ξ nor any nonlinear wave solution.
This proves Lemma 20.1.

Proposition 20.2 (Actual Gaussian transfer and stability). Let Y be a centered Gaus-
sian linear noise on smooth compact positive-time tests, on a common coupled space,
with
                               Var Y (F ) ≤ C∥F ∥2HW .                         (20.2)

It extends to HW . The finite paths MY,N =       j≤N Y (fj )Aj converge in every finite
Lp (Bθ ) to an actual centered Gaussian path MY with compact values at all times on one
path event, and
                                             √
                        ∥MY − MY,N ∥Lp Bθ ≤ C∥MR − MN ∥Lp Bθ ,                    (20.3)
                                             √
                                ∥MY ∥Lp Bθ ≤ C∥MR ∥Lp Bθ .                        (20.4)

For a fixed real Schwartz pair and time,

                             ⟨MY (t)z, w⟩E = Y (hR,t;z,w )    in L2 ,                      (20.5)

where hR,t;z,w is the literal raw test class ζR 1(0,t) vz Gw with the actual adjoint from (17.12).
The path is basis-independent up to indistinguishability, is linear in jointly Gaussian
noise families, and complexifies sesquilinearly.
    If (W, Yn ) are jointly centered Gaussian, Yn and Dn = W − Yn have uniform bounds
of the form (20.2), and Var Dn (F ) → 0 on every smooth compact test, then MYn → MR
in every finite Lp (Bθ ).

Proof. The variance bound annihilates raw-null tests and extends Y as a bounded linear
map into its actual L2 space. Completion preserves Gaussian finite-dimensional laws.
For each finite raw orthonormal family, the covariance of its Y evaluations is bounded
by CI, by applying the variance bound to every real linear combination. Independence
of Y (fj ) is not assumed.
    Apply Lemma 20.1 with vj = Aj (·) in Bθ , also to finite tails. The standard com-
parison vector has the law of the raw W (fj ). The already-proved raw path convergence
makes MY,N Cauchy in every Lp . It lies in the same separable closed span of the count-
ably many deterministic paths. The elementary Cauchy/subsequence construction of
Lemma 19.9 gives a strongly measurable limit on the actual space of Y . Different p
limits agree by convergence in probability. Passing to limits in the finite inequalities
gives both displayed bounds. An almost surely uniformly convergent subsequence gives
compact values for all times. Functional L2 limits of Gaussian linear combinations prove
that the path is Gaussian.
    For real Schwartz z, w, the actual coefficient identity is

                  ⟨Aj (t)z, w⟩E = E[W (hR,t;z,w )W (fj )] = ⟨hR,t;z,w , fj ⟩HW .

Therefore, the finite transferred row is Y (PN h), with PN the raw HW orthogonal pro-
jection. Since PN h → h, the noise bound gives Y (PN h) → Y (h) in L2 . Operator-path
L2 convergence proves (20.5). Long intervals use the same raw completion and endpoint
nullity as in Lemma 19.8. No filter is commuted through a physical mask or wave group.
Four real pair identities give the complex assertion. Countably many dense Schwartz
phases and rational times, followed by phase density and path continuity, prove basis
indistinguishability.
    For jointly Gaussian Y, Z, their real linear combinations have a raw variance bound
by the L2 triangle inequality. The finite sums are linear in the noise, hence so are the

                                               145
path limits. In the stability assertion Dn is centered Gaussian by the joint hypothesis.
Its uniform bound gives
                                              √︁
                     ∥MDn − MDn ,N ∥Lp Bθ ≤ CD ∥MR − MN ∥Lp Bθ .
For fixed N , the ∑︁
                   covariance matrix of (Dn (fj ))j≤N is positive and bounded by sN (n)I,
where sN (n) =
√︁                   j≤N Var Dn (fj ) → 0. The finite comparison gives ∥MDn ,N ∥Lp Bθ ≤
   sN (n)∥MN ∥Lp Bθ . Choose N large for the uniform tail, then n large for the finite head.
Linearity identifies MDn = MR − MYn . The same argument proves a multiparameter
limit whenever each fixed test error tends to zero, with no relation between parameter
rates. This proves Proposition 20.2.

20.2     The spatial covariance space and a retaining extension
Lemma 20.3 (Original spatial space and heat). Let HΓ be the real test-null completion
with              ∫︂        ∫︂                         ∫︂

It is separable and includes Schwartz tests. With
                                               2
                      pε (x) = (2πε)−d/2 e−|x| /(2ε) ,   Cε f = pε ∗ f,              (20.6)
heat is a positive self-adjoint contraction on HΓ , I − Cε is a contraction, and Cε → I
strongly.
Proof. The spectral normalization is the spatial computation in Proposition 16.4; its
integral is real on real tests by evenness of µ. The local bound |BΓ (f, g)| ≤ |K|Γ(K −
K)∥f ∥∞ ∥g∥∞ on a common compact support gives separability by countable smooth
approximation. Expanding smooth cutoffs of a Schwartz test converge in Schwartz
seminorms, and its covariance correlation pairing converges against tempered Γ. Thus,
Schwartz tests enter the same completion. The Fourier multiplier of Cε is mε (ξ) =
      2
e−ε|ξ| /2 . On dense Schwartz tests, the displayed norm gives positivity, self-adjointness
and both contraction bounds. Dominated convergence gives strong convergence there;
uniform boundedness extends it to the completion. This uses no physical mask operator
on a noise quotient. This proves Lemma 20.3.
   Set νa ( dλ) = λa−1 dλ/ΓE (a) and
                          G = L2 ((0, ∞)λ × Rr , νa ( dλ) dr; HΓ ).
This is a real separable Hilbert space: countable simple functions on rational rectangles
with a countable dense spatial collection suffice.
Lemma 20.4 (Feature isometry retaining the given noise). On finite smooth temporal-
spatial tensors define
                                  √ ∫︂
                       ΦF (λ, r) = 2λ     e−λ(t−r) F (t, ·) dt.              (20.7)
                                               t>0
                                               t≥r

It extends to an isometry Φ : HW → G with closed range, and the formula represents
smooth compact tests almost everywhere. On a product extension retaining the original
W , there is an isonormal I on G with

                       I(Φh) = W (h)         in L2    for each h ∈ HW .              (20.8)

Proof. The identity
                           ∫︂ min(t,u)
                                         2λe−λ(t+u−2r) dr = e−λ|t−u|
                             −∞

and the raw Laplace formula give ∥ΦF ∥2G = ∥F ∥2HW . All signed exchanges for finite ten-
sors are absolutely integrable: compact temporal factors give integrals of |τ (t)σ(u)||t −
u|−a , finite for a < 1, times fixed finite spatial inner products. No oscillatory infinite-
measure Fubini is used. Smooth time partitions on one compact support approximate
a general smooth spacetime test uniformly by finite tensors. The local covariance
bound (19.5) proves their density in HW . Hence, the isometry extends with closed
range.
    For a general smooth compact F , those approximants also converge uniformly in
spatial HΓ norm in time, by local Radon mass. For fixed λ, r, their integrals in (20.7)
converge in HΓ . Meanwhile the isometry gives convergence in G, with an almost every-
where convergent subsequence. Its two limits agree, proving the stated formula. The
coordinate r is auxiliary; no value of raw negative-time noise has been assigned.
    Let P project orthogonally onto ΦHW . On a product extension take an independent
isonormal J on (ΦHW )⊥ , built from independent real standard Gaussians on a finite or
countable basis. Define

                            I(g) = W (Φ−1 P g) + J((I − P )g).

Orthogonality and independence make it isonormal on G and prove (20.8) with the actual
original random variable, not only its law. For the countable fixed raw basis, these
equalities hold on one common probability-one set. Thus, the raw finite paths and their
Lp limits are precisely pullbacks of the already constructed raw driver. Cutoff fields may
use J; they are not asserted to be functions of W alone. This proves Lemma 20.4.

                 Cov(Yε,η (t, x), Yε′ ,η′ (s, y)) = Kmax(η,η′ ) (t − s)Γε+ε′ (x − y).   (20.11)

                          Var Yρ (F ), Var Dρ (F ) ≤ ∥F ∥2HW ,                (20.12)
and Var Dρ (F ) → 0 for every F ∈ HW as ε, η ↓ 0 at arbitrary relative rates. Conse-

                 MRρ := MYρ −→ MR          in Lp (Bθ ),        MDρ = MR − MRρ .         (20.13)

The source and residual have uniform local Gaussian driver moments and square-

    The point feature is continuous in G norm, directly from this continuous covariance.
Its norm is bounded on compact parameter sets. Hence, for a smooth compact spacetime
F , its Bochner integral against vρ;t,x exists. Integrate the heat translates in x, then the
time feature, to obtain exactly Qρ ΦF . Applying the bounded map I : G → L2 proves
the pointfield-test identity on the same space. Common-support indicator tests follow by
raw completion and L1 approximation; bounded point-feature norms and the domination
below give the same L2 limit.
    Orthogonality of the two λ ranges gives, for g ∈ G,

                 ∥(I − Qρ )g∥2G = ∥(I − Pη )g∥2G + ∥Pη (I − Cε )g∥2G ≤ ∥g∥2G .

Also ∥Qρ g∥G ≤ ∥g∥G . Together with the isometry these prove both variance bounds.
Disjoint λ ranges are independent; the full heat residual need not be independent of the
source.
    The L2 tail integral gives Pη → I strongly. Fiberwise heat converges strongly on
simple G functions by Lemma 20.3, then on all of G by the contraction bound. The
estimate
                  ∥(I − Qρ )g∥G ≤ ∥(I − Cε )g∥G + ∥(I − Pη )g∥G −→ 0
holds at arbitrary relative rates. Thus, the residual variance tends to zero for every
raw F . Proposition 20.2 gives (20.13) without commuting a filter through ζR or a wave
group.
                                                                                √
    The same comparison gives ∥MRρ ∥Lp Bθ , ∥MDρ ∥Lp Bθ ≤ ∥MR ∥Lp Bθ . The raw p es-
timate and its even-moment exponential-series argument give uniform Gaussian driver
bounds. They are statements about Lp equivalence classes on the retaining extension,
not a simultaneous path event for all uncountably many cutoffs. This proves Proposi-
tion 20.5.

20.4     Regularized pointfields and the ordinary first response
Lemma 20.6 (Regularity for each fixed cutoff). For each fixed ρ > 0, the pointfield has
a version continuous in time and smooth in space, with every spatial derivative jointly
continuous on compact time-space rectangles.

Proof. For each spatial multiindex b, differentiate (20.15) in x as a G vector. All deriva-
tives exist because their spatial squared norms are the finite heat moments
                                    ∫︂
                                                  2
                                       ξ 2b e−ε|ξ| µ( dξ),

and the next moments dominate difference quotients. Set Zb (t, x) = I(∂xb vρ;t,x ). At fixed
x, use 1 − e−λh ≤ λh in the finite λ integral; at fixed t, use |eiξ·x − eiξ·y | ≤ |ξ||x − y| and
the next heat moment. The squared triangle inequality gives

                      E|Zb (t, x) − Zb (s, y)|2 ≤ Cρ,b (|t − s| + |x − y|2 ).           (20.16)

This computation uses (20.11), including its spatial width, and applies to singular spec-
tral measures without a density assumption.
    Here is a direct continuity construction. In m = d + 1 parameter dimensions on a
                                                                                      √
compact rectangle, even Gaussian qth increment moments have root bounded by C q
times the parameter distance to the power 1/2 at small distances. The dyadic grid
has at most Cm 2nm adjacent edges at level n. Their maximum has Lq norm at most
  √
C q 2−n(1/2−m/q) , with constants for this rectangle and fixed ρ, b. Choose q large and
0 < h0 < 1/2 − m/q. The sum of these maxima weighted by 2nh0 has finite Lq norm.
Binary refinement, using a fixed number of adjacent edges per coordinate at each scale,
gives a continuous extension from dyadic points. At each fixed parameter the extension
agrees with the given Gaussian variable in L2 , by (20.16); it is a modification. Use

                                               149
countably many nested compact rectangles and all multiindices. The versions agree on
overlaps on countable dense sets, hence everywhere there by continuity, so they patch.
   They are actual derivatives. The G-valued fundamental theorem gives, along a coor-
dinate segment,
                                                  ∫︂ h
                  b                   b
                 ∂x v(t, x + hei ) − ∂x v(t, x) =      ∂xb+ei v(t, x + uei ) du.
                                                   0

Apply bounded linear I and L2 integration. The continuous versions give the same
ordinary integral by their Riemann sums, which converge both pathwise and in L2 .
Enforce these identities first on countably many rational t, x, h and multiindices, then
use joint continuity to extend them to every segment in the compact rectangles. The
ordinary fundamental theorem yields ∂i Zb = Zb+ei . Thus, all spatial derivatives exist
and are jointly continuous with time. The versions are for this fixed ρ, not a common
path event over uncountably many source parameters. This proves Lemma 20.6.

Proposition 20.7 (Actual ordinary source response). For each fixed ρ > 0, on its
regularity event, put bρ (t, x) = ζR (x)Yρ (t, x). The statewise integral
                                       ∫︂ t
                             Lρ (t)z =      S(−u)B(bρ (u))S(u)z du        (20.17)
                                       0

defines an operator-norm Lipschitz path on E, and Lρ = MRρ for all t ∈ [0, T ] on one
probability-one set. In particular, it has the strongly measurable path version supplied by
the Gaussian construction.

Proof. The coefficient is compact smooth in space and continuous in time in every spatial
derivative norm. Multiplication estimates and strong continuity of the full free group
show that the statewise integral exists on every Er . Its operator increments Er → Er+1
are bounded by a finite sample constant times |t − s|, exactly by the statewise gain
argument of Lemma 19.4. This proves Lipschitz continuity in B(E), without requiring
norm continuity or an operator-norm derivative of S(t).
    At each fixed u, the Sobolev duality calculation uses only spatial smoothness of
bρ (u, ·). From S(−u)(0, f ) and the two Bessel weights, for real Schwartz phases,
                                           ∫︂
              ⟨S(−u)B(bρ (u))S(u)z, w⟩E = bρ (u, x)[S(u)z]1 (x)Gw (u, x) dx.

Thus, no prior hypothesis of smoothness in time is invoked for this instantaneous identity.
For complex pairs conjugate the second factor. Integrating gives the ordinary source-
field integral against the literal raw wave test hR,t;z,w . For smooth tests, the point-feature
integral has already been identified with Yρ (F ). The continuous pointfield version has
the same integral: compact Riemann sums converge pathwise and in L2 to the ordinary
and feature integrals, respectively. Approximate the indicator wave test in the raw
completion with common compact spatial support and L2 -time spatial supremum error
tending to zero. Its L1 error also tends to zero. The point-feature norms are bounded

                                             150
on that support, so their integrals converge in G; the raw variance domination gives the
same L2 limit on the test side. Consequently,

                   ⟨Lρ (t)z, w⟩E = Yρ (hR,t;z,w ) = ⟨MRρ (t)z, w⟩E       in L2 .

Compare a countable dense Schwartz phase collection and rational times. Boundedness
and the path continuity of both sides extend this equality to all states and all times on
one probability-one set. The equality is on the actual coupled space, not only in law.
The set is for fixed ρ; the Lp limit in (20.13) needs no simultaneous version for every
source parameter. This proves Proposition 20.7.
    The source laws above match the pinned author definitions. The author’s page 9
redefinition keeps the law; the retaining extension was proved in Lemma 20.4. The
derivative estimate here was derived from the correct joint covariance, not from the
differing spatial index in the author’s page 8 increment display. No source correction is
adopted. All limits are at fixed finite R, T . The nonlinear Young flow, canonical initial
datum (1, 0) and its solution-moment transfer remain separate parts of the argument.


21     Fixed masks and displacement moments
We retain Hypothesis 1.1, data (1, 0), and all its original dimensions and covariance
amplitudes. Fix ρ = (ϵ, η) > 0 and the actual source Yρ of Proposition 20.5. For each
fixed ρ use the continuous-time, smooth-space version in Lemma 20.6. Source fields can
use the retaining auxiliary extension; their law and the given raw noise remain those
already constructed. Let m ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ) be real with 0 ≤ m ≤ 1. It multiplies the physical
source after regularization.
             m
    Write Xρ,n  (t, x) for the ordinary ordered displacement integral with coefficient mYρ ,
and Xρ,n for its unmasked version; both order-zero terms are one. We first construct
these integrals. The positive Green measures Gd (r, dz) have mass r and support in Br ,
including the sphere measure in d = 3. These are the source kernels [4, (2.1), (2.3)–(2.5),
p. 7]; the ordered convention and Gaussian pairing formula are [4, (2.13)–(2.16), p. 10;
(2.19), p. 12]. We use all pair partitions, including self-pairings.

are Bochner L2 integrals. For all n, k ≥ 0, fixed t, x, y and positive ρ,

   The actual covariance is

      Cρ (s, z; r, w) = Kη (s − r)Γ2ϵ (z − w),         0 ≤ Cρ ≤ cρ := Kη (0)Γ2ϵ (0) < ∞.

Positivity uses the nonnegative Radon measure Γ and positive heat and Laplace kernels.
The upper bound also follows by Gaussian Cauchy–Schwarz. Hölder’s inequality and
scalar Gaussian moments bound the absolute expectation of any fixed number of source
factors, uniformly over their vertices. Applied to 2n factors this bounds the L2 norm
of each n-fold product. The field is measurable and continuous, so integration against
the finite chain measure defines a Bochner L2 integral and permits stochastic Fubini
between two chains.
    These are the canonical regularized terminal integrals at common noise widths. Here
is the Green-width identification needed in dimension three. At fixed positive ρ, replace
every Green measure by its convolution with a positive heat approximate identity. At
fixed times the measures converge weakly, preserving masses. Every Wick covariance
product is bounded by a power of cρ and is spatially continuous by Lemma 20.6. Thus
its spatial integrals converge. The chain mass integrands from (21.3) give an integrable
time majorant. Square and cross moments between smoothed and unsmoothed integrals
converge to the same diagram sum; therefore, the random integrals converge in L2 . This
is precisely the Green-before-noise limit in Lemma 3.6. It also applies in dimensions one
and two if Green smoothing is used. No samplewise source bound on heat-kernel tails
is needed. Consequently, Lemma 3.8 applies to the actual unmasked integrals.
    If n + k is odd, Gaussian centering gives zero. If n + k = 2l, each pair partition D
contributes the nonnegative finite integral
                           ∫︂ ∏︂
                                   Cρ (si , zi ; sj , zj ) d(νnt,x ⊗ νkt,y ).
                             {i,j}∈D


The masked integrand has the additional factor 2l
                                                    ∏︁

pears exactly once, irrespective of its chain or partner. Summing gives (21.1), including
unequal and empty chains and every self-pairing. At n = k = 0 both sides are one.
Taking k = n, y = x and then applying the unmasked bound gives (21.2). This is
a statement about expected displacement diagrams; no samplewise solution order or
Gaussian covariance order is used. This proves Lemma 21.1.

The spatial integral is an actual Bochner L2 integral.

Proof. Put QT = n≥0 qn (T ) < ∞, using (3.16). Lemma 21.1 proves absolute L2 con-
                   ∑︁
vergence uniformly in space and widths. To identify the sums pathwise, fix ρ, a compact
observation set and a finite horizon T . On the source regularity event the coefficient is
bounded by a finite sample-dependent M on the compact backward-cone enlargement.
Finite propagation and (21.3) bound each Picard term there by M n T 2n /(2n)!. This
series converges uniformly. Each term is continuous by the unit-time Green represen-
tation and dominated integration. Summing the Volterra recursion gives the ordinary
mild equation with initial displacement one and velocity zero. Iterating the difference
equation between two continuous solutions bounds its value on the observation set by
BM n T 2n /(2n)!, where B bounds their difference on the larger compact cone. Letting
n → ∞ proves uniqueness.
    Ordinary Fubini and the absolute product integrability in the preceding lemma iden-
tify each Bochner term with the pathwise chain integral at a fixed terminal point, si-
multaneously over countably many orders. An almost surely convergent subsequence
of the L2 partial sums then identifies its limit with the continuous Picard version at
that point. These versions define the solution for all terminal points at this fixed ρ; no
common event over uncountably many widths is needed.
    For either masked or unmasked terms, Cauchy–Schwarz gives
                            ∑︂                  (m)
                                     (m)
                                 E|Xρ,n  (t, x)Xρ,k (t, y)| ≤ Q2t .
                            n,k≥0


Products of partial sums converge in L1 . Thus the preceding lemma passes termwise
to (21.4). The unmasked source law is spatially stationary, and its finite Picard integrals
commute with translations. Their L2 limits inherit stationarity, so E[uρ (t, x)uρ (t, y)] ≤
E|uρ (t, 0)|2 . Stationarity is not required of the masked solution.
    Each finite Bochner term is L2 -continuous in x by source increment continuity on
compact cones and dominated Gaussian product moments. Uniform qn tails pass this
property to the full sum. Its separable L2 image and bound Qt give the Bochner average.
Absolute Fubini is justified by ∥ϕ∥21 Q2t . Integrating the two-point inequality against
ϕ(x)ϕ(y) proves (21.5). This proves Proposition 21.2.

                               lim E|uρ (t, 0)|2 = E|u(t, 0)|2 ,                    (21.6)
                               ϵ,η↓0


                                             153
where u is the original canonical Stratonovich solution. If the masked averages in (21.5)
converge in L2 to Zm (t, ϕ), either at arbitrary rates or along a specified sequence with
both widths tending to zero, then

                               E|Zm (t, ϕ)|2 ≤ ∥ϕ∥21 E|u(t, 0)|2 .                       (21.7)

Proof. At every fixed pair of orders the actual common-width source law is the vertexwise
law in Lemma 3.6. At terminal (t, 0) reverse each chain by s′j = t−sn+1−j , zj′ = −zn+1−j ,
and likewise for the other chain. Even covariances preserve the reversed differences.
Relabeling vertices sends each pairing {i, j} to {r(i), r(j)}, giving the exact initial-chain
convention in Proposition 3.1, also for an empty chain. The preceding Green-width
identification removes Green smoothing first. Lemma 3.6 then gives the canonical limit
of each diagram at arbitrary relative noise-width rates. Odd mixed moments stay zero.
Hence, at fixed n, k,

                       E[Xρ,n (t, 0)Xρ,k (t, 0)] −→ E[Xn (t, 0)Xk (t, 0)].

All absolute mixed moments, before and after the limit, are bounded by qn (t)qk (t). Given
δ > 0, choose a finite square of orders with the double majorant outside it less than δ.
On the square the finitely many limits hold jointly; off it both tails are bounded by δ.
Proposition 3.1 identifies the canonical sum, proving (21.6). This argument uses the
single-width diagram laws, without assigning a new pathwise realization of the entire
canonical solution family.
    Finally, L2 convergence of the masked averages implies convergence of their second
moments by
                       |E|B|2 − E|A|2 | ≤ ∥B − A∥2 (∥B∥2 + ∥A∥2 ).
Take the same source limit in (21.5) and use (21.6). Existence of the masked limit
remains an explicit premise here; the next section constructs it on a phase containing
the constant data. This proves Proposition 21.3.


22      Young evolution with constant initial data
We use the fixed R, T, E, γ, θ, β of (19.1), and choose the allowed even mask m = ζR
with 0 ≤ m ≤ 1. All constants in this section can depend on these fixed data, the model
and the auxiliary physical cutoffs. There is no uniform estimate as R or T grows.

22.1     The distributional phase and its free evolution
Define the real Hilbert space

        F = E ⊕ R2 ,       ∥(e, c1 , c2 )∥2F = ∥e∥2E + c21 + c22 ,   e0 = (0, 1, 0).     (22.1)

Its distributional interpretation is (e, c1 , c2 ) ↦→ (e1 + c1 , e2 + c2 ). Let i : E → F denote
e ↦→ (e, 0, 0) and let πC : F → R2 be the constant-coordinate projection.

                                                154
Lemma 22.1 (Constants and finite propagation). The distributional interpretation of
F is continuous and injective. The space is complete and separable, and iE is closed
and proper. For every real ψ ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ),
                                Lψ (e, c1 , c2 ) = ψe + (c1 ψ, c2 ψ)                      (22.2)
is bounded from F to E. The actual free wave group on F is
                            SF (t)(e, c1 , c2 ) = (S(t)e, c1 + tc2 , c2 ).                (22.3)
It is strongly continuous and bounded on finite horizons and has finite propagation for
the total distributional data, including local cancellation between e and the constants.
Proof. A nonzero constant has Fourier transform a nonzero multiple of δ0 . Every H σ
Fourier transform is locally L2 near zero, since its weight is bounded above and below
there. A locally L2 function f cannot represent δ0 : testing against φ(ξ/ε), with φ(0) = 1
and φ compactly supported in B1 , bounds its integral by
                                   ∥f ∥L2 (Bε ) εd/2 ∥φ∥2 −→ 0,
whereas δ0 gives one. Thus constants lie in neither H 1/2 nor H −1/2 ; the interpretation is
injective. Sobolev duality and the two test integrals prove continuity into distributions,
uniformly on bounded families of compact tests. Completeness, separability and the
closed proper inclusion follow from the Hilbert direct sum.
    Multiplication by ψ is bounded on both Sobolev entries. Indeed, the Peetre inequal-
ities give ⟨ξ⟩σ ⟨η⟩−σ ≤ 2|σ|/2 ⟨ξ − η⟩|σ| . In the unitary Fourier convention its weighted ker-
nel is therefore bounded by convolution with the L1 function 2|σ|/2 cd |ψ(q)|⟨q⟩
                                                                             ˆ︁       |σ|
                                                                                          , where
           −d/2         1     2
cd = (2π)       . The L ∗ L bound applies at σ = ±1/2; the two constant columns are
also in E. This proves boundedness of (22.2).
    The finite-dimensional block in (22.3) and the strong, finite-horizon bound for S(t)
from Lemma 19.4 prove the group assertions. To verify its physical meaning, for t > 0
consider the matrix of compactly supported distributions
                                      (︃                  )︃
                                         ∂t Gd (t) Gd (t)
                                                             .
                                         ∆Gd (t) ∂t Gd (t)

The source Green convention gives G     ˆ︁d (t)(ξ) = cd sin(t|ξ|)/|ξ|; hence its convolution
multiplier is the sine quotient, and the differentiated multipliers are cos(t|ξ|) and
−|ξ| sin(t|ξ|). The matrix agrees with S(t) on Schwartz phases. Convolution by a
compact distribution is continuous on tempered distributions: its transpose convolves
a Schwartz test with the reflected compact distribution, continuously in all Schwartz
seminorms. Schwartz approximation in E extends the identity to E. The masses of
the four entries are 1, t, 0, 1, so constants give exactly (22.3). Time reversal handles
t < 0. Each entry is supported in B|t| , also for the three-dimensional sphere kernel.
If the total initial distribution vanishes on an enlarged region, each translated kernel
tests zero there, so the evolved total distribution vanishes on the smaller cone. This
argument permits cancellation between its Sobolev and constant parts. This proves
Lemma 22.1.

Proof. Lemma 19.4 gives both localizations for every deterministic Aj (0, t). The raw

for all t on one event. Use a separate event for each fixed ρ.
    For two choices ψ, ψ ′ , (Lψ −Lψ′ )z ∈ E and Dχ (Lψ −Lψ′ )z = 0. The right localization
