22.3     The exact linear Young equation
We use the Banach Young estimate [7, Sections 1.4–1.5, 4.1, (4.3), pp. 13, 61–62]. For
D, V with exponent θ > 1/2 and bounded bilinear multiplication, left composition gives
               ⃦∫︂ t                          ⃦
               ⃦                              ⃦
               ⃦     dD(r)V (r) − D(s, t)V (s)⃦ ≤ Cθ [D]θ [V ]θ |t − s|2θ .     (22.7)
               ⃦                              ⃦
                    s

The Banach spaces here are B(F ), and multiplication is bounded with constant one. We
derive the linear solution and stability estimates below rather than invoke a nonlinear
bounded-vector-field theorem.
Proposition 22.4 (Linear Young evolution). For every D ∈ C θ ([0, T ]; B(F )) with
D(0) = 0 there is a unique C θ solution, in the terminal variable, of
                                           ∫︂ t
                          UD (t, s) = I +       dD(r)UD (r, s).             (22.8)
                                                  s

It has the composition law, invertible values and joint operator-norm continuity on 0 ≤
s ≤ t ≤ T . The map D ↦→ UD is locally Lipschitz into the uniform time-triangle norm,
and
                     sup ∥UD (t, s)∥ ≤ CT exp CT (1 + ∥D∥C θ )1/θ .
                                               (︁                 )︁
                                                                                  (22.9)
                        s≤t≤T

Proof. On an interval∫︁ [a, a + ℓ] use the complete scaled norm Nℓ (V ) = sup ∥V ∥ + ℓθ [V ]θ
                        t
and put ID (V )(t) = a dD V . Applying (22.7) to every subinterval gives

                        sup ∥ID (V )∥ ≤ [D]θ ℓθ sup ∥V ∥ + Cθ [D]θ ℓ2θ [V ]θ ,
                            [ID (V )]θ ≤ [D]θ sup ∥V ∥ + Cθ [D]θ ℓθ [V ]θ .
Thus, with cθ = 2 max(1, Cθ ),
                                  Nℓ (ID (V )) ≤ cθ [D]θ ℓθ Nℓ (V ).                 (22.10)
Take K ≥ 1, [D]θ ≤ K, and choose ℓ with cθ Kℓθ ≤ 1/4. The map V ↦→ Va + ID (V ) is
a contraction on the complete affine space of paths starting at Va . Its solution satisfies
Nℓ (V ) ≤ (4/3)∥Va ∥ ≤ 2∥Va ∥.

                                                 157
   To compare D, D′ , require both seminorms at most K and write δ = ∥D − D′ ∥C θ ,

1/4 yields
                          Nℓ (W ) ≤ 2∥Wa ∥ + 2cθ ℓθ δNℓ (V ′ ).               (22.11)
Cover [s, T ] by at most nK,T such pieces. If the second initial norm is B, its norm on
piece j, indexed from zero, is at most 2j+1 B. With Ej the difference at its left endpoint,
induction gives

                Ej+1 ≤ 2Ej + 2j+2 cθ ℓθ δB,         Ej ≤ 2j E0 + j2j+1 cθ ℓθ δB.

Equation (22.11) gives the same bound inside each piece; a shorter final piece only
reduces the coefficient. For evolutions, E0 = 0 and B = 1. The number of pieces is
uniform in s, t, proving local Lipschitz dependence in the full triangle norm.
                                                           1/θ
    Now choose K = 1 + ∥D∥C θ . At most CT (1 + ∥D∥C θ ) pieces suffice. Each multiplies
the norm by at most two, giving (22.9). Uniqueness patches the solutions; right multi-
plication by the preceding evolution gives the composition law. On a small piece (22.10)
gives ∥UD −I∥∞ ≤ 1/3, so a Neumann series proves local invertibility, then products give
global invertibility. Finally, UD (t, s) = UD (t, 0)UD (s, 0)−1 proves joint norm continuity,
since UD (·, 0) is continuous and inversion is continuous on invertible operators. This
proves Proposition 22.4.

Proposition 22.5 (Actual Gaussian Young and physical flows). Apply Proposition 22.4
to A, Aρ . The resulting flows are strongly measurable in uniform triangle norm, have
every finite norm moment and satisfy
                                                  (︃               )︃
                                                p
                  UAρ −→ UA in every finite L        sup ∥ · ∥B(F ) .          (22.12)
                                                         0≤s≤t≤T

They preserve the interaction constant coordinates:

                       πC UA (t, s) = πC ,     (UA (t, s) − I)F ⊂ iE,                (22.13)

and likewise for the source. The physical flow is

                             PA (t, s) = SF (t)UA (t, s)SF (−s).                     (22.14)

It has composition and strong continuity on states, physical constant block (c1 , c2 ) ↦→
(c1 + (t − s)c2 , c2 ), all finite moments and the analogous uniform Lp source convergence.
Its difference from the free flow has range in iE.

Proof. The original paths lie in separable closed path spans by their Gaussian construc-
tion. Bounded composition in (22.5) and the continuous Young map preserve strong
measurability. Applying πC to Young Riemann sums gives the first identity in (22.13).
Each increment applied to any state lies in iE; closedness of this subspace passes the
property through sums and limits, giving the second identity.


                                              158
    On a ball of driver norm K, the local Lipschitz estimate and driver convergence
give convergence in probability of the flows. Uniform Gaussian driver tails make the
complement of this ball arbitrarily small, uniformly in ρ. Hence convergence holds
in probability without restriction. Set q = 1/θ < 2. For every finite p and c > 0,
pC(1 + x)q ≤ cx2 + Cp,c . Combine this inequality, (22.9) and the uniform square-
exponential driver moment to obtain uniform flow moments of every order. They imply
uniform integrability of the pth powers of flow differences, and therefore (22.12).
    The free group is strongly continuous and bounded on the horizon. Formula (22.14)
gives composition, statewise continuity and the asserted constant block. The map
V (t, s) ↦→ SF (t)V (t, s)SF (−s) is bounded linear from the continuous triangle-valued
operator space into the Banach space of bounded triangle-valued operator families, with
norm at most CT2 . It transfers strong measurability, moments and convergence. This
does not assert operator-norm continuity of the physical free group. Finally,

                   PA (t, s) − SF (t − s) = SF (t)(UA (t, s) − I)SF (−s)

has range in iE, since SF (t)iE = iE. This proves Proposition 22.5.

23     Compact mean families and the full-phase spec-
       tral gap
Let I = [0, 1], let Kx ⊂ Rd be compact, and let σ ↦→ Vσ be continuous from I into
Cc∞ (Kx ), real and nonnegative. The index σ is a family parameter, distinct from physical
propagation time t. Assume that one real w ∈ H 1 (Rd ) with ∥w∥2 = 1 satisfies
                              ∫︂
                     ∥∇w∥2 − Vσ w2 ≤ −ε for all σ ∈ I,
                          2
                                                                ε > 0.              (23.1)

Put M = supI ∥Vσ ∥∞ and Hσ = −∆ − Vσ . The phases E, F , inclusion i, data e0 and
observable Oϕ are exactly those of Section 22. Proposition 22.8, with deterministic
coefficient h = Vσ and noise amplitude zero, gives the actual ordinary physical flow
Pσ (t) on F . Its E restriction is PσE (t). Uniqueness of the Duhamel solution makes
finite-horizon constructions agree on overlaps; integer horizons and autonomy give the
same semigroup for every t ≥ 0.
    We import the following statements from the second edition of [8]: Theorem 10.2,
p. 258, gives the self-adjoint H 2 operator, lower bound, essential spectrum [0, ∞) and

                                                 161
compact smooth core for real compact smooth potentials in the dimensions considered
here. Theorem 10.13, p. 273, gives a simple positive ground state if the spectral bottom is
an eigenvalue. Theorem 4.12 and Corollary 4.13, pp. 140–141, give max–min and form
monotonicity, with the essential threshold used after discrete eigenvalues end and with
the quadratic-form domain allowed. Uniformity, fractional norms and all applications
to F are proved below.

The variational bound gives qσ (pτ ) ≤ E1 (σ) + 2dV , whereas the complementary spectral
bound gives qσ (pτ ) ≥ E1 (σ) + ∆∗ ∥r∥22 . Hence,

                     ∥pτ − pσ ∥22 = 2(1 − a) ≤ 2(1 − a2 ) ≤ 4dV /∆∗ .              (23.5)

The eigenfunction equation is

                              (1 − ∆)pσ = (1 − λ2σ + Vσ )pσ .

Its right side is continuous in L2 and bounded there by 1 + 2M . The inverse of 1 − ∆
is an isometry from L2 to the Bessel H 2 space, proving the asserted H 2 continuity and
bound. This proves Proposition 23.1.

                     1/2
graph levels (Aσ u, Aσ z) give continuous solutions in H 2 × H 1 . These graph norms
are uniformly equivalent to the Sobolev norms: the H 1 case was proved above, and
Aσ − B = M − Vσ is bounded by M while Aσ , B ≥ 1. Spectral cutoffs give ut = z in
H 1 and zt = −Hσ u in L2 , with continuous derivatives there. The free Fourier group on

                                                  164
H 1 ×L2 has, after its Bessel isometry, the same bounded weighted entries and is strongly
continuous by dominated Fourier convergence. Its generator is (z, ∆u) on H 2 × H 1 , as
the Fourier multipliers show. For compact smooth initial data, differentiating the free
interaction picture at these graph levels gives Duhamel with force (0, Vσ u). The identity
also holds in E. Multiplication by Vσ maps H 1/2 into L2 and then H −1/2 with bound M ,
so Picard uniqueness identifies the spectral solution with the ordinary E flow on these
data. Density within E and finite-horizon boundedness extend equality to all E. No
density of E in F is used. Finally, the ground branches give (23.8); their pairings are
continuous because pσ ∈ H 2 . Continuity and uniform bounds of the displayed vectors
and duals follow from Proposition 23.1. This proves Proposition 23.3.

24     Exact scaling of the canonical solution
Lemma 24.1 (Canonical law scaling). Under Hypothesis 1.1, put a = α0 , κ = (4 − α −
a)/(3 − α), and, for T > 0,
                          δ = T −κ ,     rT = T δ = T (a−1)/(3−α) .
The law of u(T t, rT x) is the canonical expansion solution Vδ (t, x) with data (1, 0) of
                                                   √
                                 δ 2 ∂t2 Vδ = ∆Vδ + δ Ẇ Vδ ,                         (24.1)
where Ẇ has the original raw covariance. In particular,
                         T −κ log E|u(T, 0)|2 = δ log E|Vδ (1, 0)|2 .                (24.2)
The assertion is equality in law; it assumes no pathwise uniqueness for a full rough
solution.
Proof. At positive widths, time and space dilation of the Gaussian source gives covari-
ance amplitude T −a rT−α . This follows distributionally from the homogeneous Radon
covariance, including spatial white noise. Its source cutoffs transform by Kη (T s) =
T −a Kη/T (s) and ϵ ↦→ ϵ/rT2 ; the joint maximum-time and summed-space width laws are
preserved at every vertex. The actual Green measure has the scaling
                             Gd (T τ, rT dz) = rT Gd (τ /δ, dz).
It follows from its degree 1 − d distributional scaling, also for the sphere measure in
                                                                                   −α/2
dimension three. Each terminal Duhamel vertex thus contributes T rT T −a/2 rT . Sub-
stitution of the displayed positive real powers gives
                               2−α/2
                                      √                  1−α/2
                       T −a/2 rT     = δ,      T 1−a/2 rT      = δ −1/2 .
                                                                                          √
The Green kernel for the forcing in (24.1) is δ −1 Gd ((t − s)/δ); after multiplication by δ
it gives exactly that vertex factor. Therefore every finite list of fixed-order coefficients
has the same joint law under the dilation, including all self-pairings, unequal orders and
empty chains. At fixed T, rT , remove Green smoothing first in dimension three, with its
width divided by rT2 on the scaled side. Lemma 3.6 and the absolute L2 summability
in Lemma 3.8 pass the identities to the canonical series and its second moment. No
large-time and source limit are interchanged. This proves the law assertion and (24.2).
This proves Lemma 24.1.

Lemma 25.1 (Actual coupled Gaussian block inputs). Each Tδ,m is an isometry of
the raw test completion, respecting covariance-null tests. All Wδ,m and Z are on the
same given noise space, without an independence assertion. Let K(F ) have the operator


                                               170
norm and let B0 = hθ0 ([0, B]; K(F )) be the C θ closure of zero-initial polygonal compact-

(X, Z), X = (Xm )N m=0 , is jointly centered Gaussian and
                                         √
                         b := E[XZ] = δ H,          H = (Hm )N m=0 .                 (25.4)

                                            √
                           ∥Xm ∥Lp B0 ≤ CR,B p gδ ,       p ≥ 1.                     (25.5)
Proof. Substituting s = sm + δu, t = sm + δr in the raw covariance gives the factor
δ a−2 δ 2 δ −a = 1. Thus the maps extend isometrically through the null quotient. Direct
test substitution gives Wδ,m (f ) = Wfast (f (· − mB, ·)). These are literal common-space
identities.
    Finite-rank rational matrices in a countable real Hilbert basis are dense in K(F ):
for compact T , Pn T Pn → T in norm by compactness of T, T ∗ , and finite entries admit
rational approximation. Polygonal paths with rational breakpoints and such matrices
give separability of B0 . To approximate breakpoints, use uniform convergence, uniform
Lipschitz bounds and [f ]θ ≤ (2∥f ∥∞ )1−θ Lip(f )θ . The same inequality puts every zero-
initial Lipschitz compact-valued path in B0 by polygonal approximation. The finite raw
coefficient paths iAj Lψ have these properties. Their actual Gaussian sums converge in
Lp C θ and hence in the closed space B0 . Their laws and the parent Gaussian norm bound
give (25.5). Every finite joint coefficient list with Z is Gaussian on the same W . Scalar
continuous functionals pass through the L2 limits, preserving Gaussianity by convergence
of variances and characteristic functions. Thus (X,√Z) is the asserted Banach Gaussian
variable. Cauchy–Schwarz gives E∥XZ∥ ≤ ∥X∥2 v < ∞, so its Bochner covariance
exists.

                                              ∫︂
                                          a/2

extends to the literal driver matrix tests on E: their time indicator is approximated by
smooth bounded tests on one compact support, converging in the raw norm by dominated
convergence against |u−r|−a Γ; their mean pairings converge as well. The √   actual Sobolev
adjoint is the one in (19.11). Hence the path covariance is gδ δ a/2 Hm = δ Hm on real
Schwartz E pairs. For constant inputs the two columns are the same Lψ inputs (ψ, 0)
and (0, ψ), and total finite propagation identifies their deterministic response. The span
of E and both constant coordinates is dense in F ; E alone is not. Boundedness,
                                                                            √       rational
times and path continuity prove (25.4) on all F . In particular H = b/ δ ∈ Bδ . This

Proposition 25.2 (Change of law on the whole norm-Borel space). For every nonneg-
ative norm-Borel j on all of Bδ ,
                                  [︂   √                 ]︂
                         Ej(X) = E e−Z/ δ−v/(2δ) j(X + H) .                 (25.7)

The equality is valid also as an extended nonnegative integral.

                                            171
                                        2
Proof. For real s, the density e−sZ−s v/2 defines an equivalent probability measure Qs
by completing the square in the N (0, v) density. For any finite list ℓ of continuous real
coordinates on Bδ , the Gaussian vector (ℓ(X), Z) is equal in law to (T G, z ·G) for a finite
standard Gaussian vector G. A positive covariance square root gives this representation
even when singular; no inverse is used. Here T z = ℓ(b) and |z|2 = v. Substitution
G′ = G + sz in its density gives EQs f (ℓ(X + sb)) = Ef (ℓ(X)) for every bounded Borel
f.
    The matrix entries of each block in a real Hilbert basis at rational times, together
with 0, B, generate the norm-Borel sigma-field. To see the operator-norm point, for
a countable dense set D of unit vectors obtained from normalized rational finite basis
combinations, ∥T ∥ = supu,w∈D |⟨T u, w⟩|. These pairings are finite linear combinations of
matrix entries. Supremum and Hölder norms of continuous paths are suprema over ratio-
nal times and pairs. Thus distance to every fixed element of Bδ is coordinate-measurable.
Open balls are measurable by rational closed-ball approximation, and separability makes
every open set a countable union of balls from dense centers. Conversely the coordinates
are continuous. The cylinder sets form a generating pi-system; uniqueness of probability
measures extends the finite-coordinate equality to all norm-Borel sets. Simple functions
and monotone convergence give the identity for nonnegative j. Finally take s = δ −1/2
and use sb = H from (25.4). The map and its deterministic translate are defined on the
entire input space; there is no translated exceptional-domain assumption. This proves
Proposition 25.2.

Lemma 25.5 (The actual raw restart and source products). Fix δ, B, N and let L = δ −1 ,
Lext = (N + 1)B ≥ L. On this global fast horizon define Aglob = gδ AR (Wfast ). Its actual
physical flow obeys
                                           0
                     Pglob (mB + u, mB) = Dm (u),         0 ≤ u ≤ B.                   (25.11)

                                            174
The analogous identity holds with the deterministic mean Vδt added globally and local
drivers Xm + Hm . Their finite block products, including the last remainder τ , are actual
source limits in L2 , at this fixed δ, N .
Proof. Take one retaining slow source Yϵ,η on the extension of the given W constructed
in Section 20. Define the actual fast and local noises by

                      Yfast (f ) = Yϵ,η (Tδ,0 f ),         Ym (f ) = Yϵ,η (Tδ,m f ).

The pullback isometry and (20.12) give source and residual variance bounds by ∥f ∥2HW .
The residual tends to zero on each transformed smooth compact test at fixed δ, m. Joint
Gaussianity is retained. Proposition 20.2 therefore gives the actual Lp C θ first-driver
limits on [0, Lext ] and on [0, B], including both constant columns.
    Their pointfields are Yfast (u, x) = δ a/2 Yϵ,η (δu, x) and Ym (u, x) = Yfast (mB + u, x).
Indeed, these follow by change of variables in their test integrals. The temporal law
is Kη/δ because δ a Kη (δr) = Kη/δ (r); the spatial heat law and all joint width rules are
unchanged. They are continuous in time and smooth in space. Their ordinary primitives
equal their Gaussian-transfer drivers: their actual matrix entries are Ym (hR ), by the
indicator approximation used in (25.6) and the point-test integral identity. Total finite
propagation extends this identity to both constant columns. This does not assume a
separately chosen retaining extension automatically commutes with time dilation.
    Changing the ordinary source integration variable and composing the free groups
gives the exact identity on F

               SF (mB) Asrc                    src                        src

No physical mask is commuted through a wave group. Restriction, difference and con-

limits gives the same identity for Aglob , Xm . For the finitely many m, a convergent
subsequence or rational times and path continuity gives simultaneous identities in u.
Conjugation norms need only be finite at this fixed δ.
    Conjugate the global interaction Young equation on [mB, mB + u] by SF (mB).

driven by Xm . Uniqueness identifies it with UXm , proving (25.11). The ordinary de-
terministic primitive for Vδt has the identical cocycle with local Hm . Composition now
identifies the full product, with terminal τ .
    For completeness, source convergence of these finite products requires no growing-
product stability assertion. At fixed δ, N let C ≥ 1 be the maximum norm of their
source and raw factors, and let esrc be the maximum factor difference. The parent Young
convergence gives every finite moment of C uniformly in source widths and esrc → 0 in
every finite Lp . If d(n) is the difference of the first n ordered products, then

                         d(n + 1) ≤ Cd(n) + C n esrc ,               d(0) = 0.

Extend both finite sequences by identities so the recurrence holds for all natural n. The
finite error-accumulation estimate gives d(N + 1) ≤ (N + 1)C N +1 esrc . Hölder, using C

26.1     Global bounds and the unmasked spectral bottom
Lemma 26.1 (Gaussian envelope for the original covariance). For the normalized heat
                               2
kernel pϵ (x) = (2πϵ)−d/2 e−|x| /(2ϵ) ,
                   ∫︂
                                 2
    (Γ ∗ pϵ )(x) = eiξ·x e−ϵ|ξ| /2 µ( dξ), 0 ≤ (Γ ∗ pϵ )(x) ≤ (Γ ∗ pϵ )(0) < ∞. (26.2)

The value at zero is strictly positive.
Proof. A positive tempered Radon measure has polynomial ball growth. Indeed, evalu-
ate its tempered bound on a nonnegative smooth cutoff equal to one on B1 , dilated by
R ≥ 1. Its finitely many Schwartz seminorms are bounded by CRN , so positivity gives
a ball-mass bound CRN . Summing dyadic annuli shows that (1 + |x|)−L is integrable
for L > N . Apply this separately to Γ and µ. Gaussian weights are therefore integrable
against both measures. The classical convolution on the left is finite and smooth locally
in x: on compact x sets its translated Gaussian derivatives are bounded by any fixed
sufficiently fast polynomial weight in the integration variable.
    Pair the convolution with a Schwartz test φ. Absolute Fubini is justified by these
                                    ∫︁ of Γ with pϵ ∗ φ. Applying
weights. The resulting pairing is that
                                                           2
                                                                   the spectral convention
in Hypothesis 1.1 and the identity pϵ (y)e−iξ·y dy = e−ϵ|ξ| /2 gives exactly the right side
of (26.2) as a distribution. The heat kernel is even, so either Fourier sign gives that
same Gaussian multiplier. Its spectral integral is absolutely finite and continuous in
x by dominated convergence. Thus equality of the two continuous distributions gives
equality at every x, without an extra normalization factor. The physical convolution is
nonnegative. Evenness of µ makes the spectral integral real, and its absolute value is
bounded by its total Gaussian mass, the value at zero. Finally Γ ̸= 0 and pϵ > 0 give
strict positivity there. This proves Lemma 26.1.
Lemma 26.2 (Global regularity of the original mean). Every mixed time and space
derivative of hs is globally bounded in space, uniformly on compact time intervals. In
particular, for I = [0, 1] there are finite constants M, CΨ such that
                0 ≤ hs (x) ≤ M,       ∥hs − ht ∥∞ ≤ CΨ |s − t|,      s, t ∈ I.      (26.3)

Proof. Extend Ψ by zero in time; its compact support inside (0, 1) makes this a smooth
extension. For any mixed derivative DΨ, a fixed ϵ > 0 and compactness of its common
spatial support give |DΨ(t, x)| ≤ CD pϵ (x) for all t, x. Here the heat kernel has a positive
minimum on that support. Lemma 26.1 consequently bounds Γ ∗ |DΨ(t, ·)| uniformly in
t, x. Ordinary local differentiation against the Radon measure gives the corresponding
derivatives of the convolution. After the change r = s − t,
                                   ∫︂

                                           R

For s in a compact interval the relevant r lie in a fixed bounded interval. The preceding
global bound times |r|−a is integrable since a < 1. Every derivative thus falls on the
smooth test and passes through the integral by dominated convergence, with the claimed
global spatial bounds. Nonnegativity follows from positive kernels. The bounded time
derivative proves the Lipschitz estimate. No directional decay of the mean has been
inferred. This proves Lemma 26.2.

Proof. The Fourier Laplacian A = −∆ is self-adjoint on H 2 , being multiplication by |ξ|2
with its maximal domain. Multiplication Bs = −hs is bounded self-adjoint with norm
at most M . Thus Hs = A + Bs is densely defined, symmetric and closed on H 2 ; the two
graph norms are equivalent under a bounded perturbation. For real b > M ,

                              Hs − ib = (I + Bs (A − ib)−1 )(A − ib).

The first factor has a Neumann inverse because ∥Bs (A − ib)−1 ∥ ≤ M/b < 1, and the
same holds with +ib. To check self-adjointness directly, for u ∈ D(Hs∗ ) solve (Hs −ib)v =
(Hs∗ − ib)u with v ∈ D(Hs ). Then u − v ∈ ker(Hs∗ − ib) = Ran(Hs + ib)⊥ = {0}. Hence
D(Hs∗ ) = D(Hs ).
    Set cshift = 1 + M and Js = Hs + cshift ≥ 1. Integration by parts on H 2 gives

           ∥Js1/2 f ∥22 = qs (f ) + cshift ∥f ∥22 ,        ∥f ∥2H 1 ≤ ∥Js1/2 f ∥22 ≤ cshift ∥f ∥2H 1 .

Fourier cutoffs give its density in H 1 . Completion in the equivalent norms identifies

                                                         179
    1/2
D(Js ) and H 1 as actual L2 subspaces, since both norms dominate L2 . This proves the
stated form domain.
    The spectral lower bound gives one direction of the Rayleigh identity. For the reverse,
nonzero spectral projections in [Es , Es + ϵ] give unit H 2 vectors with Rayleigh values at
most Es + ϵ. The bottom is finite because the operator is bounded below and has finite
Rayleigh tests. Let ϵ ↓ 0. Real tests give the same infimum: qs (u + iv) = qs (u) + qs (v),
so the quotient is the norm-weighted average of the nonzero real quotients. This proves
the Rayleigh identity with −Es on the left. The following negative floor justifies writing
that value as λ2Ψ,s in (26.4).
    Proposition 23.7 supplies, for this original Ψ, one unit compactly supported w and a
finite plateau mask with a common negative margin ϵ0 > 0. Its trial has support in B2L ;
choose its plateau radius R0 ≥ 2L. The unmasked form        √ on this same w is no larger.

Consequently −M ≤ Es ≤ −ϵ0 < 0, and ϵ0 ≤ λΨ,s ≤ M . Comparing identical unit

26.3     Full-class dilation and finite positive scores
Lemma 26.5 (Radon and Rayleigh dilation). For r > 0 the map
                                (Dr Ψ)(s, x) = rd+2−α Ψ(s, rx)                           (26.6)
is a bijection of Cpos , with inverse D1/r . With the same original covariance,
 hDr Ψ,s (x) = r2 hΨ,s (rx),     vDr Ψ = r4−α vΨ ,      λDr Ψ,s = rλΨ,s ,   IDr Ψ = rIΨ . (26.7)
Proof. Positive dilation preserves the full smooth positive nonzero class; multiplication
of its amplitude powers gives the stated inverse. Homogeneity of the physical Radon
measure gives, for every nonnegative measurable G,
                          ∫︂                      ∫︂
                                              α−d
                             G(ry)Γ( dy) = r         G(z)Γ( dz).

Indeed, its left pushforward measure on a Borel set A is Γ(A/r) = rα−d Γ(A). Pos-
itive Tonelli applies this identity inside the temporal convolution, giving hDr Ψ,s (x) =
r2 hΨ,s (rx). Now use the Lebesgue substitution z = rx in the raw pairing:
                               vDr Ψ = rd+2−α r2 r−d vΨ = r4−α vΨ .
This distinguishes the Radon pushforward from the spatial volume factor; it includes
singular covariances and white noise.
     The map Ur f (x) = rd/2 f (rx) is a unitary bijection on real L2 , preserving
                                                                            ∫︁     H 1 , H 2 as
sets
   ∫︁ under its inverse. Weak derivatives give ∥∇Ur f ∥22 = r2 ∥∇f ∥22 and hDr Ψ,s (Ur f )2 =
r2 hΨ,s f 2 . The real Rayleigh bijection proves the squared rate identity; the rates are
positive, so their square roots and integrals give (26.7). On H 2 this is also HDr Ψ,s Ur =
r2 Ur HΨ,s . Time has not been rescaled. For compact masks the corresponding identity
uses mR (rx) = mR/r (x); a fixed mask radius is not invariant. This proves Lemma 26.5.

Proposition 26.6 (Positive finite intrinsic lower scores). For c > 0, the score set {cIΨ −
vΨ /2 : Ψ ∈ Cpos } is nonempty and bounded above. Its finite real supremum Scpos is positive
and satisfies
                          Scpos = c(4−α)/(3−α) S1pos , Q2 ≥ S2pos ,                  (26.8)
where Q2 is the original point liminf. The label-one value is an auxiliary variational
score; no actual first-moment or source-constant identification is asserted.

                                               181
Proof. Write k = 3 − α > 0, q = 4 − α = k + 1 and m = q/k > 1. Fix one Ψ0
from the nonempty class and put I0 = IΨ0 > 0, v0 = vΨ0 > 0. Positivity of the latter
is Lemma 23.6. For r0 = (I0 /v0 )1/k > 0, which need not be less than one, the exact
real-power identity gives

                    IDr0 Ψ0 − vDr0 Ψ0 /2 = r0 I0 − r0q v0 /2 = r0 I0 /2 > 0.

This positive score belongs to an actual smooth test.
   For c > 0 put rc = c1/k and Kc = cm > 0. Lemma 26.5 and the exact scalar powers
give, for every Ψ,
                          cIDrc Ψ − vDrc Ψ /2 = Kc (IΨ − vΨ /2).             (26.9)
Since Drc is a bijection of the entire class, the whole c-score set is Kc times the whole
label-one score set.
   We prove boundedness before taking finite-real suprema. The independent existing
Theorem 13.8, for the same canonical solution, gives
                                                 κ
                 E|u(T, 0)|2 ≤ e(T + 2)d eCup T ,        T ≥ 1,     Cup < ∞.

Its prefactor is negligible: choose ϵ = κ/2 > 0, use log T ≤ (T ϵ − 1)/ϵ and log(T + 2) ≤
log T + log 3 for T ≥ 1, and divide by T κ . Thus the original point limsup is at most
Cup . One fixed test/mask lower bound gives a finite lower bound for Q2 , so Q2 is
finite. By (26.5) every label-two score is at most this same Q2 ≤ Cup , uniformly in
Ψ. Equation (26.9) at c = 2 consequently bounds all label-one scores by Cup /K2 . The
nonempty set has a finite real supremum S1pos > 0 by the positive test above.
    For general c, set equality and Kc > 0 show that Kc S1pos is the supremum: it is
an upper bound, and every smaller number is exceeded by Kc times a label-one score
within the corresponding positive distance of its supremum. This proves finiteness,
positivity and the first identity in (26.8). Taking the numerical supremum of the label-
two lower bounds proves the second. Although 2m > 2, the resulting S2pos > 2S1pos is
only an auxiliary-score comparison. It is not a Jensen improvement without an actual
first-moment identification. This proves Proposition 26.6.
   For Γ = 0, the mean, raw variance and Rayleigh rate are zero. Set all these scores to
zero; the free canonical solution u = 1 has the corresponding zero moment rate. Strict
positivity above is restricted to nonzero covariance. Matching an upper variational rate
and identifying an exact original-point limit remain later steps.

28      Physical spatial pairings and the full occupation
        class
Retain a nonzero original covariance from Hypothesis 1.1 with its actual amplitude. Set
a = α0 , k = 3 − α > 1, q = 4 − α = k + 1 and D = q − 2a > 0. The scalar D is fixed. All
profiles are real. The original positive temporal exponent and threshold imply α < 2;
this is the strict inequality used in the scalar powers and concavity. The finite positive
score S1pos is that of Proposition 26.6.

Lemma 28.1 (The physical two-point integral). The positive measure ν( dx, dy) =
dx Γ( dy) is sigma-finite. For nonnegative functions define
                                      ∫︂
                           BΓ (f, h) = f (x)h(x − y)ν( dx, dy)            (28.1)

initially as an extended nonnegative integral, using finite Borel representatives of
Lebesgue classes. Its value is representative independent. Lebesgue-a.e. convergence
transfers to ν-a.e. convergence at both positions x and x − y.

Proof. Local finiteness of Γ gives sigma-finiteness on exhausting balls, and Lebesgue
measure is sigma-finite. If N is Borel and Lebesgue null, Tonelli applied first in x gives
ν{x ∈ N } = 0 and ν{x − y ∈ N } = 0, because every fixed translate of N is null.
An arbitrary Lebesgue null set has a Borel null superset. Thus arbitrary versions are
measurable in the ν completion with the same value, and the same observation applies to
the exceptional sets of a.e. convergence. Signed pairings will be used only after absolute
convergence. This proves Lemma 28.1.
    Put Asp (g) = BΓ (g 2 , g 2 ) and b(g) = ∥∇g∥22 on H 1 ; initially Asp is used only for smooth
compact profiles before the extension below. For smooth compact z, write Uz = Γ ∗ z in
its usual pointwise sense.

space is separable through its closed isometric image f ↦→ (f, ∇f ) in L2 (Rd )d+1 .

continuity. Thus Asp and the square pairings are continuous. PSD for smooth compact

two-profile Cauchy–Schwarz gives (28.6). This completes the physical pairing argument
beyond Fatou.

nonnegative integral, not an H 1 claim for 1E . Finiteness was already proved. This
proves Proposition 28.4.

the actual nonnegative physical integral.
    Set F (s) = b(gs )α/4 . Equations (28.5) and (28.6) give BΓ (gs2 , gt2 ) ≤ Rsp F (s)F (t).

        map
Retain the nonzero original covariance in Hypothesis 1.1, its amplitude, a = α0 , and
the actual real phases E = H 1/2 × H −1/2 and F = E ⊕ R2 . Write Λ = (1 − ∆)1/2 .
The form BΓ and constant Rsp are those of Section 28. Let Hsp be the real covariance-
null completion of Cc∞ (Rd ) with inner product BΓ , and Ht the analogous completion of
Cc∞ (I), I = (0, 1), with time kernel |s − t|−a . Let H = HI be the completion of smooth
tests supported inside I × Rd . It embeds isometrically as a closed subspace of the given
raw HW . We retain the given W restricted to it.
    Fix R > 0 and real even ηR ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ) with 0 ≤ ηR ≤ 1, equal to one on the radius-R
ball and supported in the closed radius-2R ball. The physical mask is mR = ηR2 . Every
driver below takes its mask argument equal to mR : at this fixed radius the base profile
ζ(y) = mR (Ry) meets the earlier hypotheses. The root ηR factors state tests; it does
not multiply a raw noise class. All compactness conclusions here fix R.

Proposition 29.2 (Compact spatial mean map). The smooth-test map
                                     Tsp q = iBF (mR (Γ ∗ q))
extends to a compact linear map Tsp : Hsp → K(F ), where K(F ) has the operator norm.
For z = (z1 , z2 , c1 , c2 ) and w = (w1 , w2 , d1 , d2 ) set w′ = Λ−1 w2 . Its exact row identity is
         ⟨Tsp qz, w⟩F = ⟨q, k(z, w)⟩Hsp ,       k(z, w) = (ηR z1 + c1 ηR )(ηR w′ ).           (29.4)
Proof. Fix s with α/4 < s < 1/2; this interval is nonempty because the original threshold
implies α < 2. For smooth compact q, Γ ∗ q is locally smooth by differentiation under
the compactly restricted Radon integral. The masked potential is smooth compact. The
velocity pairing in E has weight Λ−1 ; hence physical Fubini gives (29.4) on smooth states.
Evenness of Γ changes BΓ (k, q) to BΓ (q, k). For general states the localized products
are L1 , and Γ ∗ q is bounded on their compact support. L2 state approximation passes
the physical integral to the limit, while Lemma 29.1 passes the Hsp pairing. This proves
the actual row.
    Multiplication by ηR maps bounded H 1/2 sets to precompact H s sets by Proposi-
tion 18.6, with gain 1/2 − s > 0. This applies to z1 and w′ . The c1 ηR family is finite
dimensional and compact. The closures of both factor families for unit z, w are compact,
so their products have compact closure L in Hsp by the fractional product bound. The
row identity gives ∥Tsp q∥ ≤ supk∈L ∥k∥∥q∥. Null covariance classes therefore map to
zero.
    For smooth q, multiplication by its potential is compact H 1/2 → H −1/2 by the same
whole-space cutoff result with gain one. Its extra c1 column is finite rank, and the
instantaneous c2 column is zero. Thus the smooth images lie in K(F ), and so does the
bounded extension by operator-norm closure.
    Choose a complete smooth-test orthonormal basis of Hsp by countable compact-
support approximation and Gram–Schmidt, omitting null classes. Let Qn project onto
its first n terms. The row identity yields
                         ∥Tsp (I − Qn )∥ ≤ sup ∥(I − Qn )k∥Hsp −→ 0.
                                             k∈L

Strong projection convergence is uniform on compact L by a finite-net argument and
∥I − Qn ∥ ≤ 1. Each Tsp Qn has finite-dimensional range; their operator-norm limit is
a compact map. This is compactness of the map, in addition to compact values. This
proves Proposition 29.2.

Lemma 29.3 (Supported raw tensor space). Canonically H = Ht ⊗ Hsp as real Hilbert
spaces. It is separable and admits a complete orthonormal basis ej with smooth test

an orthogonal projection on HW by the same finite formula.

Proof. On separated smooth tests, absolute Fubini makes the raw inner product exactly
the tensor product after both null quotients. Finite sums are dense: approximate a
smooth compact spacetime test uniformly by smooth time partitions times its spatial
slices, on one common compact support inside I × Rd . A uniform error ϵ on spatial
compact A has squared raw norm at most
                                     ∫︂ ∫︂

This also gives countable smooth-test density on exhausting compact sets, both here and

embedding into HW has closed image by completeness; its finite orthonormal projection
formula remains orthogonal there. It is not multiplication by a time indicator and gives
no independence of disjoint time intervals. This proves Lemma 29.3.

Proposition 29.4 (The compact temporal mean, without trace class). Let K on L2 (I)
have kernel |s − t|−a . It is compact, self-adjoint, strictly positive and injective. The map
ι : f ↦→ K 1/2 f extends isometrically from Ht onto L2 (I), and the actual temporal mean

                                 Ct = K 1/2 ι : Ht −→ Lr (I)                           (29.5)

is bounded and compact for every 2 < r < 2/a. The displayed formula first has its L2 (I)
meaning; the same completed map is then shown to take values compactly in Lr (I). The
operator K is not trace class and Ct : Ht → L2 (I) is not Hilbert–Schmidt.

Proof. Schur bounds K by its finite row and column integrals. The Gamma representa-
tion of |s − t|−a and zero extension of f ∈ L2 (I) give strict positivity directly: the kernel
e−λ|v| has quadratic form
                         ∫︂
                                 2λ
                               2      2
                                        |fˆ︁(ξ)|2 dξ > 0 (f ̸= 0, λ > 0)
                           R λ   +  ξ
in the unitary Fourier convention. Absolute Fubini is justified by Schur applied to
|f (s)f (t)|. Integrating with positive weight λa−1 /ΓE (a) proves positivity and injectivity.
Symmetry gives self-adjointness. The deterministic truncated kernel Kϵ = K1{|s−t|≥ϵ}
is square integrable, so finite separated-kernel approximations make it compact. The
Schur norm of K − Kϵ is at most 2ϵ1−a /(1 − a), proving compactness of K. These
truncations are not used as Gaussian covariances. Its positive square root is compact
by the compact spectral theorem.

    The actual physical mean identity on separated tests follows from (29.4). For a

fixed h are bounded by ∥(I − PJ )h∥. Compactness gives a norm-convergent image
subsequence with weak limit zero, a contradiction. This proves Proposition 29.5.

Let X be any nonzero real Banach space, with its induced operator norm on B(X ). Fix

Lemma 30.1 (Refined growth and uniform inverse control). With dimension-free con-

                sup ∥UD (t)∥ ≤ exp{cθ,B (d + dν )}, d = ∥D∥θ .             (30.1)

If cθ dB θ ≤ 1/4, whole-interval contraction gives sup ∥UD ∥ ≤ (1 − cθ dB θ )−1 and hence
log sup ∥UD ∥ ≤ 2cθ B θ d. Otherwise take nD = ⌈B(4cθ d)ν ⌉ equal pieces. Their contrac-

    On the ball ∥H∥θ ≤ K choose one uniform equal partition with ratio at most 1/4.

at most two pieces; use hθ1 + hθ2 ≤ 21−θ (h1 + h2 )θ . For larger distances use 2 sup ∥UH ∥/ℓθ .

                   UH−1 (t) − UH−1 (s) = −UH−1 (t)(UH (t) − UH (s))UH−1 (s)

bounds the inverse seminorm. This proves Lemma 30.1.

On any partition the remainder sum is bounded by a constant times B mesh2θ−1 → 0.
Left sums converge to the Young integral, so uniqueness yields UH+X = UH UZ . This
proves the product identity without assuming smooth density in the full Hölder norm.
Apply (30.1) to Z, then left multiply by A and use submultiplicativity. No inverse or
lower bound for A is required. This proves Proposition 30.2.

    Recall the actual supported identity HI = Ht ⊗ Hsp , the compact scalar covariance

               ct = ∥Ct ∥,       εJ = ∥Ct (I − πJ )∥Ht →Lr (I) ,        TR = TR U.

Proposition 31.4 (Uniform completed mean regulation). On HI the operators

                             Pret = πJ ⊗ PK ,         QJ,K = I − Pret

are real orthogonal projections, and εJ → 0. The actual mean is a strongly measurable
Lr (I; K(E)) field and satisfies, uniformly for R ≥ 1,

                   ∥TR ∥ ≤ M0 ct ,       ∥TR QJ,K ∥ ≤ M0 εJ + Mf ct K −β .           (31.6)

These norms have domain HI and target Lr (I; L(E)).

Proof. The tensor projections are selfadjoint, idempotent and contractive on finite ten-
sors and hence on their Hilbert completion. For the scalar tail, failure of convergence
would supply unit vJ with ∥Ct (I − πJ )vJ ∥ bounded below on a subsequence. But
(I − πJ )vJ tends weakly to zero, since its pairing with h is bounded by ∥(I − πJ )h∥.
Compactness supplies a norm-convergent image subsequence; bounded linear scalar ob-
servables show its limit is zero, a contradiction. Lemma 31.3 transfers this scalar tail
norm to the Hilbert-valued time map without a dimension factor.
    For fixed R, TR is bounded into the separable compact-operator space on E. Com-
position with the actual Bochner field U Φ gives the stated mean and its first bound.
The exact decomposition is

                         QJ,K = (I − πJ ) ⊗ I + πJ ⊗ (I − PK ).

The first term is bounded by M0 εJ . For the second term use the typed intertwining

                    U (πJ ⊗ (I − PK )) = (ILr ⊗ (I − PK ))(Ct πJ ⊗ I).

It holds first on finite tensors, then by continuity. Apply (31.4) pointwise, followed by
Lemma 31.3 for Ct πJ , whose norm is at most ct . This proves (31.6). Infinite spatial rank
is allowed throughout; neither tensor compactness nor uniform-radius compactness has
been asserted. This proves Proposition 31.4.

32.1     Uniform outer factors and the exact whole-space envelope
Fix real χ0 ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ), equal to one on B1 and supported in the closed ball of radius two.
Choose real ψ0 ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ), equal to one on a neighbourhood of supp χ0 and supported

both cutoffs are supported strictly inside the cube of side L. The auxiliary torus has

Lemma 32.1 (Uniform rescaled outer maps). The cutoff periodization and recovery

       ∥Pψρ : H r (Rd ) → H r (TL )∥ ≤ Cψ0 ,r ,         ∥Eχρ : H r (TL ) → H r (Rd )∥ ≤ Cχ0 ,r ,

                        Cr ρd/2 Fr (k − ρξ),            Fr (t) = |ϕˆ︁0 (±t)|⟨t⟩|r| .

The nonnegative Fr is integrable and bounded by arbitrarily high inverse powers of ⟨t⟩.
At fixed k, integration in ξ gives a bound Cρ−d/2 . At fixed ξ, summation in k gives
Cρd/2 : translate ρξ into a unit lattice cell and sum a power tail of order greater than
d, as in (18.11). This lattice spacing is one and has no remaining ρ dependence. The
mixed Schur estimate bounds the operator norm by the square root of the product of
those two quantities. Their radius powers cancel. For recovery the row and column roles
are reversed.
    Bounded kernel extension from Schwartz functions and torus polynomials gives the
full Sobolev maps by density. Their distributional meanings are the same cutoff maps
as in Lemma 18.5; no point sampling of a rough Fourier function is used. This includes
negative indices. This proves Lemma 32.1.

power, it follows that
                                          √
        ∥MN (t) − MN (u)∥Lq (L(E)) ≤ CQ,B q Rd/Q |t − u|βt ,   |t − u| ≤ 1.      (32.7)
                      √︁
At α = 1, the factor 1 + log(1/h) is absorbed by the strict gap 1 − a/2 − βt > 0. For
α > 1, the power is (3 − a − α)/2. These power/log constants are independent of R, N, q.
Longer increments are finite sums of increments of length at most one, with constants
depending only on the fixed B.
    Let Dn (F ) be the largest adjacent increment on the grid kB2−n of [0, B]. For fine
levels B2−n ≤ 1, the finite-maximum bound gives
                                               √
                       ∥Dn (MN )∥Lq ≤ 2n/q CQ,B q Rd/Q (B2−n )βt .

No independence of increments is used. The sum after multiplication by 2nηt converges
uniformly for even q ≥ Q, because its geometric ratio is at most 2−(βt −ηt −1/Q) < 1. The
finitely many coarse levels are controlled by unit partitions. The deterministic dyadic
                                                                                 √
chaining argument in Lemma 19.9 therefore bounds the Lq C ηt norms by CQ,B q Rd/Q .
Each finite path is continuous and starts at zero, as required there. The fixed-horizon
embedding C ηt ↪→ C θ gives the same estimate in C θ .
    For each fixed R, Lemma 19.9 identifies MR (W ) as the all-finite-moment C θ limit of
these very MN . Pass the uniform finite-N estimate to this limit. Uniform convergence
in R is unnecessary: the limiting bound has its explicit radius dependence at every R.
For arbitrary v ≥ 1, choose an even q ≥ max(v,  √ Q) with q ≤ v + Q + 2 ≤ (Q + 3)v.
Lyapunov’s inequality gives (32.6), absorbing Q + 3 into the fixed constant. This
proves Proposition 32.3.
Corollary 32.4 (Actual deterministic covariance residuals). Let Y be a centered Gaus-
sian linear noise on the actual coupled space, with Var Y (f ) ≤ C∥f ∥2HW , C ≥ 0. Its
actual transferred driver satisfies
                                               √      √
                           ∥MR (Y )∥Lv (C θ ) ≤ C CQ,B v Rd/Q .

In particular this holds with C = 1 for Ym (f ) = W ((I − Π)Tδ,m f ), for any deterministic
orthogonal projection Π on the slow HW . Its rank may be infinite and it may depend on
δ, R.
Proof. Proposition 20.2 is a linear Gaussian covariance contraction theorem for this same
physical
   √     mask and these same deterministic coefficient maps. It bounds the path norm
by C times the reference isonormal driver norm. The reference has the canonical first-
driver law used in Proposition 32.3; the actual Y is not replaced in this comparison. The
zero case gives zero directly. This proves the first assertion. For the second, the actual
raw isometry (25.2), proved in Lemma 25.1, and ∥I − Π∥ ≤ 1 give Var Ym (f ) ≤ ∥f ∥2HW ,

                                           207
where the right side is the original raw norm in fast coordinates. The joint residuals
retain the given slow W and may be correlated. They need not lie in the span of
an individual fast noise, which is not a hypothesis of Gaussian transfer. No random
noise-dependent projection is used. This proves Corollary 32.4.

32.3     The growing-radius expected-norm anchor
Proposition 32.5 (Actual averaged block mean tends to zero). Fix integer B ≥ 1 and
use the true block partition B, δ, N, ℓm of Section 29, with 0 < δ < min(1, 1/B) and
N = ⌊1/(δB)⌋. For Ym as in Corollary 32.4, put

                                                    m=0

                                                √

In particular, for Rδ = δ −1 + 2,
                         √                                          1−a  d
    2EZδ ≤ 2(2/B)1/p CQ,B p 3d/Q δ eanchor −→ 0,        eanchor =       − > 0.    (32.9)
                                                                     2   Q
Both bounds are uniform in the deterministic orthogonal projection.
Proof. Stopping a zero-start interaction primitive and extending it constantly to B does
not increase its supremum or Hölder seminorm. An empty last interval gives the zero
path. Probability normalization and Lyapunov give
                                           (︄ N               )︄1/p

                       EZδ ≤ (EZδp )1/p = δ        E∥Xm ∥pC θ

This is linearity for a finite sum; no time-block independence or maximum over blocks
is used. Since δB < 1, we have N ≥ 1 and N + 1 ≤ 2/(δB), proving (32.8).
    Now Rδ ≤ 3/δ because δ < 1. The last condition in (32.2) is equivalent to Q(1 −
a) > 2d. Dividing by 2Q > 0 gives eanchor > 0. Substitute this radius into (32.8) to
obtain (32.9). The estimate holds at each fixed B, p, Q and model, uniformly in every
deterministic residual projection. This proves Proposition 32.5.
    These bounds concern the expected first-driver norm on E. They do not give an F
estimate with constant initial-data columns. The radius substitution is numerical; cone
coverage and source-to-point transfer require their separate arguments. No concentra-
tion, infinite-head Gaussian integration or nonlinear moment upper bound follows from
this section alone. The constants need not be uniform as Q, B tend to infinity or as
model parameters approach an endpoint. For zero covariance, the actual drivers and
stochastic bounds vanish directly.

33      Signed rough static potentials and the physical
        wave
Fix the original nonzero covariance and a finite physical mask mR as in Section 29.
For real q ∈ Cc∞ (Rd ) put Vq = mR (Γ ∗ q) and Lq (u, v, c1 , c2 ) = (0, Vq (u + c1 ), 0, 0) on
F = E ⊕ R2 . Let VR be the operator-norm closure of these operators in K(F ). It is a
closed real linear space. No positivity is imposed on q.

Lemma 33.1 (The actual limiting multiplier). Every L ∈ VR determines a unique real
V ∈ H −1/2 supported in supp mR . It has the actual multiplier action L(u, v, c1 , c2 ) =
(0, V u + c1 V, 0, 0), where multiplication extends from tests to H 1/2 → H −1/2 . For k =
∥L∥, its symmetric pairing obeys

                               |vV (u, w)| ≤ k∥u∥H 1/2 ∥w∥H 1/2 .                       (33.1)

Proof. Choose Lqn → L. The constant-displacement vector has norm one, so ∥Vqn −
Vqj ∥H −1/2 ≤ ∥Lqn − Lqj ∥. Thus, Vqn → V in H −1/2 . Testing outside the fixed com-
pact mask support preserves that support. Every zero column of Lqn remains zero. Its
displacement multipliers converge in L(H 1/2 , H −1/2 ) to a map of norm at most k. For
compact smooth u, smooth multiplication is bounded on H −1/2 by Lemma 18.5. Hence,
Vqn u → V u there, identifying the limiting map on tests and then uniquely on H 1/2 by
density. The constant column recovers V and proves uniqueness. On compact smooth
u, w, the pairing is ⟨V, uw⟩, denoted vV (u, w), so it is symmetric. Its bound and sym-
metry extend by density. Complexification preserves the operator norm by splitting
real and imaginary parts in the Hilbert norms. The pairing extends sesquilinearly to a
Hermitian form; its diagonal is the sum of the real and imaginary diagonal values. This
proves Lemma 33.1.

Proposition 33.2 (Closed form and quarter-power phase). Let K ≥ max(1, ∥L∥) and
c = 2 + K 2 . On complexified L2 the form

                       qV (u) = ∥∇u∥22 − vV (u, u),         Q(qV ) = H 1 ,

represents a unique self-adjoint HV . With A = HV + c and LK = 3(1 + K 2 ),

                     A ≥ 1,       D(A1/2 ) = H 1 ,                                      (33.2)
               1
               2
                 ∥u∥2H 1 ≤ qV (u) + c∥u∥22 ≤ LK ∥u∥2H 1 ,                               (33.3)
                                                                         1/4
              D(A1/4 ) = H 1/2 ,      2−1/4 ∥u∥H 1/2 ≤ ∥A1/4 u∥2 ≤ LK ∥u∥H 1/2 .        (33.4)

The negative dual completion is H −1/2 . No H 2 operator domain is asserted.

Proof. Put b = ∥u∥2 , z = ∥u∥H 1 . Fourier Cauchy–Schwarz and (33.1) give

                   ∥u∥2H 1/2 ≤ bz,      |vV (u, u)| ≤ Kbz ≤ 12 z 2 + 21 K 2 b2 .


                                              209
The last scalar inequality is the weighted arithmetic–geometric mean inequality at
weights 1/2, 1/2 and nonnegative bases z 2 , (Kb)2 , including zero norms. Since qV =
z 2 − b2 − vV (u, u), it follows that

     qV + cb2 ≥ 21 z 2 + (1 + K 2 /2)b2 ,    qV + cb2 ≤ 23 z 2 + (1 + 3K 2 /2)b2 ≤ LK z 2 .

The lower bound dominates both z 2 /2 and b2 . Thus, this densely defined Hermitian form
is semibounded and its shifted norm is equivalent to the complete H 1 norm. It is closed
in the definition of [8, p. 80]. From [8, Theorem 2.14, p. 81, (2.63)], it represents a unique
self-adjoint HV . This representation is independent of the admissible shift. From that
book’s Theorem 3.6 and (3.51)–(3.53), p. 109, applied after shifting, we obtain (33.2).
Here A ≥ 1 is initially its form lower bound. A bounded spectral slice below one would
produce a unit D(A) test contradicting that form bound; hence it is also the spectral
lower bound. Lemma 33.3 records the full bottom argument.
    Set B0 = 1 − ∆. On the common form domain, 21 B0 ≤ A ≤ LK B0 . We prove the
needed square-root comparison rather than assuming an order rule for arbitrary operator
powers. For T0 , S0 ≥ 1 with common form domain and qT0 ≤ qS0 , completing the square
in the shifted form norm gives

    ⟨f, (T0 + t)−1 f ⟩ = sup{2 Re⟨f, u⟩ − qT0 (u) − t∥u∥2 } ≥ ⟨f, (S0 + t)−1 f ⟩,    t > 0.

The scalar identity x = π −1 0 t−1/2 x/(x + t) dt follows by t = xv 2 . Positive spectral
                                     1/2     1/2

I − t(T0 + t)−1 ≤ I − t(S0 + t)−1 . Apply this to (B0 , 2A) and (A, LK B0 ). Both op-
erators in each pair are at least one. The resulting inequalities give (33.4), including
equality of its domains. For v ∈ L2 the L2 -duality norm against ∥A1/4 u∥2 is ∥A−1/4 v∥2 :
Cauchy–Schwarz gives the upper bound, and u = A−1/2 v gives equality after normal-

the form, resolvent and spectral calculus makes these constructions commute with real
conjugation. This proves Proposition 33.2.

Proof. For unit u, z = ∥u∥H 1 ≥ 1 gives qV (u) ≥ z 2 /2 − 1 − K 2 /2 ≥ −(1 + K 2 )/2 ≥ −K 2 .
Conversely dilate a unit compact smooth bump and translate its whole support outside
supp mR . Its potential pairing is zero and its kinetic energy tends to zero. Thus, β ≤ 0.
Real and complex form infima agree because a complex diagonal splits into the two real

                                              210
diagonals. The spectral form formula in [8, (3.52)–(3.53)] identifies this form infimum
with the spectral bottom. For clarity, a bounded spectral slice below the form lower
bound would give a unit form test violating that bound. Conversely the form integral
is bounded below by the spectral infimum. The spectrum is nonempty: a unit vector’s
scalar spectral measure has mass one and a support point, and normalized projections
onto arbitrarily small bounded neighborhoods give approximate eigenvectors, excluding
a bounded inverse there. Conversely, off the closed PVM support Σ the Borel function
(h − z)−1 , set to zero off Σ, is bounded for real z ∈   / Σ, as is h/(h − z) on Σ. Its calculus
maps into D(HV ) and is a two-sided resolvent inverse on the appropriate domains. Thus
the spectrum is precisely Σ. Hence, λ ≤ K and every spectral value h has h + c ≥ 2.

Proposition 33.4 (Identification with the actual physical wave). The spectral wave in

Its actual extension on F satisfies

                      ∥PV (t)∥L(F ) ≤ 16(1 + K)4 (1 + t)3 eλ(V )t ,         t ≥ 0.                (33.7)

Both constant response columns are included.
Proof. The imported form representation [8, Theorem 2.14, p. 81, (2.63)], tested against
compact smooth functions, gives HV u = −∆u − V u in distributions for u ∈ D(HV ).
Such u lies in H 1 and V u ∈ H −1/2 . Consequently (1 − ∆)u = HV u + V u + u ∈ H −1/2 ,
so D(HV ) ↪→ H 3/2 . The embedding is graph-continuous: by (33.3), ∥u∥2H 1 ≤ 2⟨Au, u⟩ ≤
∥Au∥22 + ∥u∥22 , and the distributional equation bounds the H 3/2 norm by this graph
norm.
    The generator of the free E group is A0 (u, v) = (v, ∆u) on H 3/2 × H 1/2 . Indeed,
differentiate its Fourier matrix after a frequency cutoff, dominate by that graph norm and

components; they require v ∈ H 1/2 and ∆u ∈ H −1/2 , hence u ∈ H 3/2 . No operator-norm

    Start spectral data in D(A) × D(A1/2 ) = D(HV ) × H 1 . The preceding graph-level
estimates give continuous graph trajectories and C 1 trajectories in H 1 × L2 , with ut = v,
vt = −HV u. This follows first on bounded spectral cutoffs and then uniformly on finite
horizons in the graph norms by spectral dominated convergence and [8, Theorem 3.2,

gives continuity in D(A0 ) and zt = A0 z + LE z in E, where LE = L|E . The strong-group

Proposition 34.3 (Actual source conservation). At each fixed positive ρ, on one
probability-one set for the fixed c, Φ, R, T , the physical source flow satisfies

                      PDρ (t, s)∗ JE PDρ (t, s) = JE ,      0 ≤ s ≤ t ≤ T.

Cb (r) = SE (−r)B(bρ (r))SE (r) is uniformly bounded and strongly continuous on states.
Lemma 34.1 gives ω(Cb (r)z, w) + ω(z, Cb (r)w) = 0 for all r, z, w.

passage is the actual one in Proposition 22.5, with these fixed shifts.
    Young local Lipschitz dependence gives uniform interaction evolution convergence in
probability on every driver ball. The uniform path tails make the complement small. The
global growth bound CT exp{CT (1 + ∥Dρ ∥C θ )1/θ }, with 1/θ < 2, and square-exponential
tails give all finite evolution moments uniformly in ρ. Uniform integrability upgrades
convergence to every finite Lp of the uniform time-triangle operator-difference norm. The
generic Young arguments apply directly on L(E): bounded multiplication has norm
one, exactly as on F . Physical differences are the bounded linear image V (t, s) ↦→
SE (t)V (t, s)SE (−s) of interaction differences. Its separable strongly measurable image
proves measurability of the triangle supremum norm. No operator-norm continuity of
the physical free group is assumed.
    The same actual E/F restriction holds for mean shifts. The original drivers satisfy
AF i = iM and AρF i = iM ρ . Bounded Bochner expectation with Z gives this intertwining
for their covariance means. Applying i to the Young sums and using state uniqueness
gives UF i = iUE , hence PF i = iPE after free conjugation. The inclusion is closed and
isometric; E is not dense in F .
    Choose a countable source sequence and a subsequence with expected squared uni-
form physical difference at most 2−4n . Markov’s inequality makes the probabilities of
a difference exceeding 2−n summable. The union bound for all events after N tends
to zero, so the differences tend to zero uniformly in operator norm over all time pairs
almost surely. Intersect with the countably many source conservation events. Then
                   ∥Pn∗ JE Pn − P ∗ JE P ∥ ≤ ∥Pn − P ∥∥JE ∥(∥Pn ∥ + ∥P ∥)

                                             215
passes the identity to PD simultaneously on the whole triangle. The raw PD is measurable
with respect to the given W . Thus its conservation event, which has probability one on
the retaining extension, also has probability one on the base space. Countable states
and rational times, followed by continuity and the strong free-group bounds, give the
displayed simultaneous event. Only the fixed parameters are concerned; no common path
set over uncountably many Φ, c, R, T is claimed. For c = 0 the means and evolutions
are deterministic, so conservation is deterministic for each Φ.
    Since ∥JE ∥ = 1, the identity implies 1 ≤ ∥PD (t, s)∥2 . Surjectivity does not follow
from this identity alone in infinite dimension. Instead, the actual Young evolution is
invertible: on short contraction pieces ∥U − I∥ ≤ 1/3, the Neumann series gives the
inverse, then finite products and the free group inverse give the full inverse. This also
covers Φ = 0 and the free case. At a fixed 0 < δ < 1, one may take the actual reference
Wfast = W ◦ Tδ,0 , R = δ −1 + 2, T = δ −1 , c = gδ , Φ = 0. This gives the growing-cone
flow’s norm baseline pointwise in δ, with no uniform moment or mean budget. This
proves Proposition 34.4.

closed inclusion and Tm = Tδ,m : Hfast → HW the actual raw isometry; Hfast has the

             Wm (f ) = W (Tm f ),      ϕm = δ −a/2 Tm∗ iI Φ,      TE Φ = i∗ T Φ i,

                    Dϕm (min(u, ℓm )) = Hm,E (Φ)(u),           0 ≤ u ≤ B.            (34.6)

extending by an independent auxiliary source while retaining all base variables. No
slow-source pullback is presumed; the raw covariance direction is independent of the
auxiliary realization.
    The mean T Φ has range in closed iE. Bounded compression passes its strong measur-

nonempty because θ < 1 − a/2 and θ > 1/2; p only instantiates that package here. The

    Here AF,m is the full F interaction driver from Proposition 22.2, and Hm,F is its

The factor here is δ a/2 , not δ. Completion in the earlier covariance identity permits
every Φ ∈ HI , without positivity or smoothness.
    The operator Tm Tm∗ is the orthogonal projection onto the raw isometry’s closed range.
Thus, W ((I − Tm Tm∗ )iI Φ) is orthogonal to the entire fast first chaos. Every continuous
linear coordinate of Mm is an L2 limit of finite Wm -basis sums. Its covariance with this
difference vanishes. Bochner covariance exists by L2 path moments, and bounded linear
functionals separate its values. Hence,

                       E[Mm (u)W (iI Φ)] = E[Mm (u)Wm (Tm∗ iI Φ)].

Multiply by δ −a/2 and use (34.7) to obtain (34.6) on [0, ℓm ], then stop both sides. No
unstopped equality beyond ℓm is asserted.
    Apply Proposition 34.4 with c = 0, reference Wm , horizon B and the deterministic
direction ϕm . Equality of drivers before ℓm and Young uniqueness give the same actual
endpoint, so its norm is at least one. If ℓm = 0, the endpoint is the identity and its norm
is one. The radius may be chosen δ −1 + 2 after fixing δ; all identities are fixed-parameter
instances. This proves Proposition 34.5.
    For zero covariance the drivers and covariance means vanish, and the claims reduce
directly to the free identity in Lemma 34.1. These results give a norm baseline and
its exact completed mean application. They provide no uniform control-radius budget,
stochastic moment upper bound or nonlinear product ratio by themselves.


35     Actual Gaussian control tubes and Rayleigh
       transfer
We first state the Gaussian geometry with its exact covariance scale. Let H be real
separable Hilbert, W its given isonormal process, and X a strongly measurable centered

                                            217
Gaussian variable in a real separable Banach space B, with E∥X∥2 < ∞. Assume every
continuous linear coordinate has the form ℓ(X) = W (kℓ ), kℓ ∈ H. Fix 0 < δ < 1 and
write
                           √
      Ch = E[XW (h)] = δ Mδ h,          Hseen = span{kℓ },    E0 = C(H).      (35.1)

Assume Mδ : H → B is compact. All Hilbert balls below are closed.

35.1     The genuine Wiener realization
Proposition 35.1 (First-chaos realization on the actual support). The variable X lies
in E0 almost surely, with a centered Radon Gaussian law of full support there. Its genuine
Cameron space and norm are

                      HCM = C(Hseen ),       ∥Ch∥CM = ∥Pseen h∥H .                  (35.2)

This support triple is an abstract Wiener realization, including the measurable-norm
condition.

Proof. The zero law has C = 0 and E0 = {0} and is immediate. A countable norming
family exists in B ∗ : choose a dense sequence on the unit sphere and apply Hahn–Banach
at each point. Approximation gives ∥x∥ = supj |ℓj (x)|. Norms of differences from a
countable dense set show that these coordinates generate the Borel sets. Consequently
X is measurable in the completion of σ(W (H)). Fix an orthonormal basis (ej ) of H
and set Gj = σ(W (e1 ), . . . , W (ej )). In finite dimension these sums and projections
stabilize at the dimension. Bochner conditional expectations need no Radon–Nikodym
property here: define them first on finite-valued simple variables using scalar conditional
expectations, then extend by the L2 norm contraction. Simple approximation supplies
their scalar testing identities. Gaussian regression and the countable norming family
give                               ∑︂
               Xj = E[X | Gj ] =        W (el )Cel ,   Xj −→ X in L2 (B).            (35.3)
                                  l≤j

To justify convergence, finite-coordinate scalar events approximate all events in the gen-
erated sigma field: the class of events whose indicators are approximable in L2 is a
monotone class containing the finite-coordinate algebra. Approximate a Bochner vari-
able by simple variables and use the conditional L2 contraction.
                                                                  ⊥
   Bounded testing gives ℓ(Ch) = ⟨kℓ , h⟩. Thus, ker C = Hseen       , and C is injective
on Hseen . The closed first chaos generated by X is exactly W (Hseen ). Its covariance
image therefore has the first-chaos norm in (35.2); unused directions are removed by the
orthogonal projection, without an inverse covariance. This is the genuine Cameron image
under the first-chaos definition. The finite sums in (35.3) lie in E0 , so L2 convergence
and an almost-sure subsequence put X in that closed space almost surely. Functionals
on E0 extend to B by Hahn–Banach, so restriction preserves the scalar laws and first
chaos.


                                           218
    Its law is tight. For each radius 2−j choose a finite union of balls of mass greater
than 1 − ε2−j−1 , using a countable cover of the separable space. The intersection of
the corresponding finite closed unions is closed and totally bounded and has mass at
least 1 − ε. Completeness makes it compact. Metric Borel regularity on these compact
sets, followed by tightness, gives a Radon law. To prove full support, the tail X − Xj
is independent of the finite Gaussian head: every tail coordinate has label (I − Pj )kℓ ,
orthogonal to e1 , . . . , ej . Joint Gaussianity and countable Borel coordinates give Banach
tail independence. Given h and ε > 0, choose j with ∥CPj h − Ch∥ < ε/3 and P(∥X −
Xj ∥ < ε/3) > 0. The finite head has positive probability in every ball about CPj h,
even if its covariance is singular: the preimage under its finite linear map contains an
open ball about the coefficient vector of Pj h. Independence proves positivity of every
ball about Ch. Closure proves that the support is all of E0 .
    We check the measurable norm explicitly. In Hseen put Vj = span{Pseen el : l ≤ j}.
Any finite orthonormal family (hk ) in Vj⊥ is also orthogonal to e1 , . . . , ej . The same
regression and Bochner Jensen argument gives
                                                          ⃦               ⃦2
                                     ∑︂                   ⃦∑︂             ⃦
  E[X − Xj | W (hk ), all k] =          W (hk )Chk ,           W (hk )Chk ⃦ ≤ E∥X − Xj ∥2 .
                                                          ⃦               ⃦
                                                        E⃦
                                                          ⃦               ⃦
                                k                        k

For any ε > 0 choose j so that the right side is less than ε3 . Markov bounds the
probability that this finite sum has norm exceeding ε by ε, uniformly over every such
finite orthonormal family. Since C : Hseen → HCM is isometric, the excluded finite-
dimensional Cameron subspace is C(Vj ). This is the measurable-norm tail condition for
the dense Hilbert image, whose Banach completion is E0 . The characteristic functional
of the law is Eeiℓ(X) = exp(−∥kℓ ∥2H /2). These facts give the stated actual abstract
Wiener realization. The zero space has the direct zero-law interpretation. This proves
Proposition 35.1.

35.2     Closed tubes and exact half-action geometry
We import the set-enlargement theorem [7, Theorem 11.6, p. 190], with footnote 3 on
p. 190; p. 191 supplies the following context. For an abstract Wiener space, a Borel A
of positive mass and its Cameron unit ball K, it states

                 µ((A + tK)c ) ≤ ΦN (ˆ︁
                                     a + t),      a = Φ−1
                                                  ˆ︁   N (µ(A)),     t ≥ 0.

Here ΦN is the standard normal distribution function; the same page gives ΦN (y) ≤
   2
e−y /2 for y ≥ 0. This theorem is imported, not proved here. It concerns Gaussian
input sets, not Gaussianity of a nonlinear wave. We verify Borel measurability of every
enlargement we use.
Lemma 35.2 (Compact images and attained tube radii). Suppose the law of X is
nonzero and put

        bδ = 2E∥X∥,        Aδ = {x ∈ E0 : ∥x∥ ≤ bδ },        Tr = Aδ + Mδ (B H (r)).

                                            219
Every Tr is closed. The extended distance dδ (x) = inf{r ≥ 0 : x ∈ Tr } satisfies
                                                            2
                 {dδ ≤ r} = Tr ,      P(dδ (X) > r) ≤ e−r /(2δ) ,   r ≥ 0.          (35.4)

In particular, dδ is lower semicontinuous and finite almost surely.

Proof. A compact map from a separable Hilbert space sends a closed bounded ball
to a compact set, including closedness of the image. Indeed, diagonal orthonormal
coordinates and Fatou give a weakly convergent subsequence of any bounded sequence,
with limit still in the ball. Compactness gives norm-convergent image subsequences,
and bounded scalar testing identifies every limit with the image of that weak limit.
This proves sequential compactness. Closed Aδ plus a compact image is closed: along a
converging sequence of sums, extract the compact component and pass the residual into
the closed base set.
   Membership in Tr implies dδ ≤ r. Conversely, if dδ (x) ≤ r, choose hj with ∥hj ∥ ≤
r + 1/j and x − Mδ hj ∈ Aδ . A weak subsequence has limit h of norm at most r;
compactness gives Mδ hj → Mδ h. Closedness of the residual proves membership in Tr .
This also covers r = 0 and proves least finite-radius attainment. Closed sublevels give
lower semicontinuity and Borel measurability.
   The nonzero law has bδ > 0, and Markov gives µ(Aδ ) ≥ 1/2. Projection onto Hseen
removes all unused raw directions, so (35.2) gives the exact set identities
                                √                                   √
               K = C(B H (1)) = δ Mδ (B H (1)),       Tr = Aδ + (r/ δ)K.

These are Borel enlargements by the preceding closedness. The imported theorem applies
to the genuine realization in Proposition 35.1. Since ˆ︁ a ≥ 0, it yields (35.4). The
value ˆ︁
       a = +∞ is allowed when µ(Aδ ) = 1. The tail tends to zero, proving almost-
sure finiteness. No Cameron embedding constant changes the half action. This proves
Lemma 35.2.

Rayleigh has no atoms, and at the closed endpoint zero both probability bounds are

In particular the operator-moment bound in (35.11) is finite at fixed parameters.

Proof. The finite scalar cutoff inequality gives, for r ≥ M , Crν − r2 /2 ≤ −M 2 /8 − r2 /8.
Its exact guards are M > 0, r ≥ M , C ≥ 0, 1 < ν < 2 and 4C ≤ M 2−ν . Indeed,
r2−ν ≥ M 2−ν ≥ 4C gives Crν ≤ r2 /4, while r2 ≥ M 2 splits the remaining −r2 /4 into the
                                                         2                  2
two displayed negative terms. Analytically, − dr  d
                                                    [4e−r /(8δ) ] = (r/δ)e−r /(8δ) . Integrating
                              2                                  2
gives the front factor 4e−M /(8δ) ; multiplication by e(A0 −M /8)/δ proves (35.13). Such a
cutoff exists since 2 − ν > 0. On [0, M ] the growth bound gives G(r) ≤ A0 + CM ν , so
the integral is finite there as well. Apply this to A0 = Cenv (1 + bνδ ) and C = Cenv in the
preceding proposition. No infinite-dimensional Gaussian density is used. This proves
Lemma 35.7.

Lemma 35.8 (The raw half-action supremum). For finite J : H → R bounded above
on every raw ball, let gJ (r) = sup∥h∥≤r J(h). In the extended reals,

                        sup{gJ (r) − r2 /2} = sup{J(h) − ∥h∥2H /2}.
                        r≥0                     h∈H

Proof. For the lower inequality take r = ∥h∥. For the upper inequality, every ∥h∥ ≤ r
gives J(h) − r2 /2 ≤ J(h) − ∥h∥2 /2. Take first the ball supremum and then the radius
supremum. Each ball is nonempty with finite supremum, so no infinity-minus-infinity
expression occurs. No compactness, rank or density assumption is needed. This proves
Lemma 35.8.

   The Gaussian transfer retains the half of the single global raw action. The coarse
envelope is not the sharp spectral/product envelope. Its application to the original
canonical point moment, and the sharp envelope and variational comparison, require
their separate proofs.


36     Uniform spectral rates and phase products
Retain every original covariance and amplitude. Set E = H 1/2 × H −1/2 , F = E ⊕ R2 ,
and mR = η(·/R)2 , R ≥ 1, with the same fixed root profile. Choose
                   1
                     < θ < min(1 − a/2, (3 − a − α)/2),   ν = 1/θ,
                   2
                   (1 − θ)−1 < r < 2/a,    ν ≤ p < 2 < r.

Choose auxiliary exponents 0 < γ < min(1/2, (2 − α)/2) and θ < β < min(1 − a/2, (3 −
a − α)/2) for the actual phase construction. The original strict threshold makes these
intervals nonempty. Write iI : HI → HW for the supported closed inclusion. For nonzero
covariance the actual field is

            TR = TR (Ct ⊗ IHsp ),     ∥TR ∥HI →Lr (I;K(E)) ≤ Cmean = M0 ∥Ct ∥,

by Proposition 31.4. For zero covariance set Hsp = HI = {0} and M0 = Cmean = 0.
Means and their rates are then zero, and mean flows are free; no nonzero-noise estimate
is needed for that case. The residual comparisons below still act on the nonzero phase
E.

36.1     The actual E class and its own spectral constants
For real smooth compact q let Vq = mR (Γ ∗ q), Mq : H 1/2 → H −1/2 be its multiplier and
LE
 q (u, v) = (0, Mq u). Let VE,R be their operator-norm closure in K(E).

Lemma 36.1 (Unique potential and fixed-radius lift). Each L ∈ VE,R has the form
L(u, v) = (0, M u), with ∥L∥ = ∥M ∥ =: k, and determines a unique real V ∈ H −1/2
supported in supp mR . The actual symmetric pairing satisfies

                             |vV (u, w)| ≤ k∥u∥H 1/2 ∥w∥H 1/2 .                     (36.1)

It has a lift LF in the exact class of Section 33, with LF i = iL. Lift constants may
depend on R and are used only for identification. Here vV (u, w) = ⟨V u, w⟩H −1/2 ,H 1/2 on
real states, with its Hermitian extension on complex states.

Proof. For zero covariance, L = M = V = LF = 0 gives the claims directly. If LE    n → L,
                                                1/2  −1/2
the zero columns persist and Mn → M in L(H , H            ), with the stated norm equality.
At fixed R choose real compact smooth χR = 1 near supp mR . Since Mn χR = Vn ,
∥Vn − Vj ∥H −1/2 ≤ ∥χR ∥H 1/2 ∥Mn − Mj ∥. Thus, Vn → V , with the same support by testing

                                            225
outside it. Smooth multiplication on H −1/2 identifies M u = V u on compact tests, then
on H 1/2 by density. The identity M χR = V gives uniqueness; symmetry and (36.1) pass
to the limit.
    The original F operators are (u, v, c1 , c2 ) ↦→ (0, Mn u + c1 Vn , 0, 0). Their differences
are bounded by (1 + ∥χR ∥H 1/2 )∥Mn − Mj ∥. They therefore converge in the old F class
and give the lift. This bound is allowed to grow with R; it will enter no uniform estimate.
Dense smooth raw controls and bounded completion of TR also show that all completed
mean fields lie in VE,R almost everywhere. Bochner averages preserve this closed linear
class. Complexification preserves k by the Hilbert real/imaginary splitting. The real
symmetric pairing becomes Hermitian and its diagonal splits into the two real diagonals.
This proves Lemma 36.1.

Proof. We derive the budgets without transferring a quantitative F bound. With b =
∥u∥2 , z = ∥u∥H 1 , Fourier Cauchy–Schwarz gives ∥u∥2H 1/2 ≤ bz. Equation (36.1) and
the half-weight arithmetic–geometric mean inequality give |vV (u, u)| ≤ Kbz ≤ z 2 /2 +
K 2 b2 /2. The weights are 1/2, 1/2, bases z 2 , (Kb)2 ≥ 0; positive powers include zero
norms. For qV = ∥∇u∥22 − vV (u, u) and c = 2 + K 2 ,
     1 2
     2
       z + (1 + K 2 /2)b2 ≤ qV (u) + cb2 ≤ 23 z 2 + (1 + 3K 2 /2)b2 ≤ 3(1 + K 2 )z 2 .   (36.4)

This is a densely defined closed semibounded Hermitian form on full H 1 : its shifted norm
is equivalent to the complete H 1 norm. The lower bound dominates 2b2 , since z ≥ b
and K ≥ 1. From [8, Theorem 2.14, p. 81, (2.63)] and Theorem 3.6, p. 109, (3.51)–
(3.53), it represents a unique self-adjoint HV with A = HV + c ≥ 2 and D(A1/2 ) = H 1 .
Closedness uses the definition on that book’s p. 80. The spectral meaning of A ≥ 2
follows from bounded spectral slices: a nonzero slice below two would give a unit vector
in D(A) contradicting the form lower bound. The bottom argument below uses the same
representation.
    Put B0 = 1 − ∆ and LK = 3(1 + K 2 ). The common forms obey 21 B0 ≤ A ≤ LK B0 .

    For unit L2 data, b = 1 and z ≥ 1. The form budget gives qV ≥ z 2 /2 − 1 − K 2 /2 ≥
−K 2 . A dilated compact unit bump translated wholly off the potential support has
zero potential pairing and kinetic energy tending to zero, so β ≤ 0. Complex form
infima agree with real ones: dividing each complex diagonal by its squared norm is
the convex combination of the two normalized real diagonals, omitting zero parts. The
spectral form formulas identify β with the spectral bottom by bounded spectral slices.
There are support points because scalar spectral measures of unit vectors have mass
one; their arbitrarily small bounded slices give approximate eigenvectors and the reverse
form bound. If Σ denotes the closed PVM support and real z ∈              / Σ, the Borel function
fz (h) = (h − z)−1 on Σ, zero outside, is bounded, as is hfz (h). Its calculus has range
in D(HV ) and supplies both inverse identities for HV − z on their respective domains.
Thus no such z is spectral; the approximate eigenvectors give the other inclusion. Hence
λ ≤ K and h + c ≥ 2 on the spectrum.      √          √ √

matrix norm is at most twice that entry bound, and the condition cost gives (36.2).
Spectral dominated convergence and scalar addition give the group.
     Denote the spectral matrix evolution by PVE (t). It is the actual physical E wave. The
fixed-R lift in Lemma 36.1 belongs to the precise class in Proposition 33.4. Its form is
this same qV and has the same self-adjoint representative by uniqueness, regardless of the
lift’s larger shift. On dense bounded spectral cutoffs the two waves have the same Cos, Sin
functions of HV . Boundedness extends equality to all E. The old graph-core Duhamel
identification therefore applies; no lift norm or χR constant entered the budgets above.
Conjugation commutes with the real structures, so real restrictions are the actual phases.
For compact L, finite-rank approximation and strong continuity of the free group and

                                              227
its adjoint make v ↦→ SE (−v)LSE (v) norm continuous on finite intervals. Its primitive is
Lipschitz. Its Young equation is therefore the ordinary bounded-interaction equation by
Proposition 22.4. This identifies the averaged static baseline without transferring any
lift norm into the uniform budgets.
     Finally, take a unit ε-minimizer for W , 0 < ε ≤ 1. Since β(W ) ≤ 0, it has qW ≤ 1.
Thus z 2 −1−Kz ≤ 1, which forces z ≤ K +2. The multiplier difference changes its form
by at most ∥LV − LW ∥∥u∥2H 1/2 ≤ (K + 2)∥LV − LW ∥. Take infima, let ε ↓ 0 and reverse
V, W . The square-root inequality on nonnegative −β gives (36.3). For zero covariance
the class is {0}, HV = −∆ and β = λ = 0; the free wave gives the claims directly. This
proves Proposition 36.2.

36.2       Scalar-time averaging and actual mean budgets
Proposition 36.3 (Uniform completed-field averaging). Use the true slow partition Im
with mesh at most δB. Let Aδ average over each positive-length cell; an empty final
cell is ignored. Put ωδ = ∥(Aδ − I)Ct ∥Ht →Lr (I) . At fixed B, ωδ → 0. For M ≥ 0, if
∥Φ∥HI ≤ M , WR = TR Φ, W R = Aδ WR and Z = Cmean M , then

             ∥WR ∥Lr , ∥W R ∥Lr ≤ Z,  ∥W R − WR ∥Lr ≤ M0 M ωδ =: Ωδ (M ),           (36.5)
