       I

These errors are uniform in R ≥ 1. The rate is finite, nonnegative and continuous on
HI .

Proof. Bochner averaging is a contraction on Lr (I; Z ) for any Banach Z , by Jensen.
For continuous fields on [0, 1], its error is bounded by the modulus of continuity at
the mesh. Simple-field approximation, followed by continuous approximation of scalar
indicators in Lr , proves density of those fields. The contraction gives strong convergence
for every Bochner field. In the scalar case it is uniform on the compact closure of Ct ’s
unit-ball image, by a finite net and ∥Aδ − I∥ ≤ 2. This proves ωδ → 0.
    On finite tensors, integration commutes with the fixed bounded TR :
                            K(E)
                          Aδ       TR (Ct ⊗ I)Φ = TR ((Aδ Ct ) ⊗ I)Φ.

The dimension-free Hilbert tensor bound in Lemma 31.3, and ∥TR ∥ ≤ M0 , extend this
typed identity to the completed HI . Applying it to the difference gives (36.5). Averages
remain in the same closed E operator class. Only scalar-time compactness was used; the
tensor map is not claimed compact. Nor does this assert noncompactness of a composite
spatial family: each fixed-radius completed mean is compact.
   For any two such fields, apply (36.3) with the pointwise bound

                             K(s) = max(1, ∥W1 (s)∥, ∥W2 (s)∥).

proves (36.7). It also proves rate continuity, while λ(L) ≤ max(1, ∥L∥) gives RateR (Φ) ≤
1+Cmean ∥Φ∥. For zero covariance the fields and rate errors are zero directly. This proves

Lemma 36.4 (Explicit physical primitive and growth budgets). Write GB =
sup|v|≤B ∥SE (v)∥ and CB = G2B [B 1−1/r + B 1−1/r−θ ]. Then 1 ≤ GB ≤ 3B,

                  log ∥SE (ℓ)UD (ℓ, 0)∥ ≤ Cgr B(1 + ∥D∥νθ ),    0 ≤ ℓ ≤ B.            (36.9)

Here D is any zero-start C θ ([0, B]; L(E)) path; no Gaussian or Hamiltonian hypothesis
is imposed on this growth bound. The actual mean endpoints have ∥Pmmean ∥ ≥ 1.
Proof. The balanced free matrix has upper entry ⟨k⟩√sin(vk)/k and lower −k sin(vk)/⟨k⟩.

have
  3 + 2B 2 ≤ 3B; SE (0) = I gives GB ≥ 1. The stated CB inequalities follow, including
CB B 1/r = G2B (B + B 1−θ ) ≥ B. Hölder in time with 1 − 1/r > θ, the affine time change,
compact conjugation and constant stopping prove (36.8); the true partition sums the
rth powers, with zero on an empty cell.
    For the explicit Young budget put d = ∥D∥θ and cθ = 2 max(1, CYoung ), as in the
local estimate proving Lemma 30.1. If cθ dB θ ≤ 1/4, whole-interval contraction gives
log sup ∥UD ∥ ≤ log(4/3) < 1/2. Otherwise at most 2B(4cθ d)ν contraction pieces have
norm at most 4/3. Combine their logarithms with log GB ≤ log(4B). For example
Cgr = 4 + log 4 + 2(4cθ )ν gives (36.9), since B ≥ 1. This is the generic algebra argument
on L(E) with multiplication norm one, not an unspecified radius-dependent bound.

mean, with the same one-mask physical model. Its fast direction is δ −a/2 Tδ,m ∗

at fixed δ. The completed covariance identity identifies the primitive up to ℓm , then

                                             229
stops it. Thus, pure-mean symplectic conservation gives the endpoint norm at least
one, deterministically for each Φ. No fast-direction cost estimate is used. An empty
endpoint is I, and the zero-covariance case is the free identity in Lemma 34.1. This
proves Lemma 36.4.

Proof. Choose s > r(3 + κball )/(r − ν) and KB = B s . At each fixed B, the inner δ limit

bad terms become

                    Cgr 32r Cmean

limit gives the claim. No diagonal choice Bδ , Mδ is asserted. At fixed parameters all

dependence and positive endpoint norms make the displayed logarithms continuous;
the rate is continuous by Proposition 36.3. Growth bounds make each control-ball
supremum finite. A countable dense subset of each closed Hilbert ball has the same
supremum. Supremum over R is an ordinary deterministic real supremum, not a random
measurability or compact-family claim. This proves Corollary 36.6.

proves (36.12). The endpoint functional is continuous on all tuples by generic Young
local dependence, including nonphysical residuals. Nothing was multiplied as scalar
displacement moments. This proves Proposition 36.7.
   These are uniform deterministic E-phase product estimates. Their Gaussian
Rayleigh application, original point transfer and signed-positive score comparison are
proved separately in Sections 35–40.

Retain all original covariances and amplitudes. Write a = α0 , κ = (4−α−a)/(3−α) > 1.

Use the same real even root η, set ψ = η(·/R), m = ψ 2 , z = (ψ, 0) ∈ E, D = B1 (0) and

Lemma 37.1 (Actual restriction and fixed-parameter finiteness). For nonzero covari-
ance, the actual E flow and its source flows are the closed isometric restrictions of their
actual F extensions. Their operator norms and differences are measurable, and
                                   1 ≤ E∥Pδ ∥2L(E) < ∞.                                (37.1)
Proof. Let AF = gAF,R (Wfast ) be the scaled full driver. Linearity and Proposition 22.2
give AF i = iDE , with DE = gMR (Wfast ), and SF (t)i = iSE (t). The extension uses a
cutoff ψ˜︁ = 1 near its larger localization, not the datum ψ. Applying i to the state Young
sums and using uniqueness gives UF (t, s)i = iUE (t, s), then PF (t, s)i = iPE (t, s). The
same argument applies to sources. Closedness and isometry imply ∥PE ∥ ≤ ∥PF ∥ and the
analogous difference bound; density of E in F is neither true nor used. The continuous
Young map on its separable path span, followed by bounded physical conjugation and
restriction, gives measurable endpoint norms and uniform time-triangle difference norms.
No operator-norm continuity of the free group is assumed. Young Neumann pieces and
the free inverse also give invertibility.
    We check a direct finiteness input, without a fixed-mask asymptotic rate. Take
B = 1 in Propositions 35.4 and 35.6 and Lemma 35.7. Every 0 < δ < 1 satisfies their
clock condition. The global endpoint in (35.10) is exactly this Pδ : the same given Wfast ,
amplitude g, root η, one mask m, massless E phase, and horizon δ −1 . We use the same
admissible θ and actual driver version. Literal row identities, rational times and Young
uniqueness identify versions if a smaller common admissible exponent is used. Thus,
the finite radial bound applies to this actual endpoint, not merely an equal-law copy.
Proposition 34.4 gives its norm at least one almost surely, proving (37.1). This use
requires no F -norm rate along growing radii. Source convergence and source uniform
integrability are checked next, separately from this raw endpoint finiteness. This proves
Lemma 37.1.

These identities use neither compactness of a Hilbert ball nor an inverse covariance
matrix. This proves Lemma 39.1.

                                    S (C) = max dC

Proof. For arbitrary Φ, let A(Φ) = {i : ⟨Φ, ki ⟩ > bi }. It is contained in J . All other

      2

    Conversely, FC (0) = 0 = dC ∅ . For nonempty A, take its √︁    dual minimizer. Equa-
tion (39.5) makes every label in A strictly active at ΦA , with ⟨ΦA , ki ⟩ − bi = ℓi /wiA .
Both inequalities in the preceding display are then equalities for the sum restricted to
A, since z = ΦA . This restricted score equals dC  A . All omitted labels contribute non-
negative terms to the full score, hence FC (ΦA ) ≥ dC   A , even if this value is negative.
Maximizing over A, including the empty set, proves (39.7) and the asserted attainment.
For a maximizing nonempty subset the upper and lower bounds force FC (ΦA ) = dC      A ; no
outside inactivity was assumed. All-zero labels give S (C) = 0 directly. This proves
Proposition 39.3.

Lemma 39.4 (Real Gram realization). Every finite real symmetric positive semidefinite
matrix is the Gram matrix of vectors in a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space. When
its entries are nonnegative, the value S (C) is independent of its realization.

Proof. Induct on the size, with the empty matrix realized in {0}. Diagonal entries are
nonnegative. If all vanish, testing on ei + tej gives 2tCij ≥ 0 for every real t, hence
C = 0, realized by zero vectors. Otherwise permute a positive diagonal c into the first

                                            242
                           [︁   ]︁
position and write C = vc vAT . The Schur complement B = A − vv T /c is positive
                                               on (−v T y/c, y). Realize
semidefinite: test the original quadratic form √                    √ B by vectors ui
using the induction hypothesis. Then k1 = ( c, 0) and ki = (vi / c, ui ) for the other
indices realize C. Undo the permutation. Entries of B need not be nonnegative; this
construction requires neither nonnegative coordinates nor complete positivity.
   In any realization, Cii = 0 means ki = 0, so its entire row vanishes. Formula (39.7)
depends only on C, ℓ, b and these eligible indices. It therefore proves realization inde-
pendence whenever the entries are nonnegative, which is the scope in which S (C) is
used. This proves Lemma 39.4.

Proposition 39.5 (Entrywise Gram comparison). Let C, D be real symmetric positive
semidefinite matrices on the same finite index set, with 0 ≤ Cij ≤ Dij . With the same
ℓi > 0, bi ≥ 0,
                                   S (C) ≤ S (D).
No positive-semidefinite order on D − C is required.

Proof. Lemma 39.4 defines both values. Their eligible sets satisfy JC ⊂ JD . For
nonempty A ⊂ JC and positive weights,

                      D        C          1 ∑︂

Taking infima gives dC         D
                        A ≤ dA . The empty values are both zero. Taking maxima and
using the inclusion of eligible families proves the claim by Proposition 39.3. This includes
the appearance of previously zero labels. This proves Proposition 39.5.

Remark 39.6 (Exact checks and scope). The empty value is essential: for one label
with C = 1, ℓ = 1/10, b = 4, the nonempty dual has D(4) = −3199/400 < 0, whereas
the primal
       √︁ score at zero is zero. Global concavity is unavailable: for k = ℓ = b = 1 the
score 2 (ϕ − 1)+ − ϕ2 /2 decreases on [0, 1] and has infinite positive right slope at one.
For k = ℓ = 1, b = 0, differentiation on ϕ > 0 gives the maximum 3/2 at ϕ = 1; on ϕ ≤ 0
the score is nonpositive. Doubling the cost changes the [︂ positive
                                                                ]︂    critical point to 2−2/3
                                                          1 1/2
and the maximum to 3 2−4/3 . Finally C = I2 , D = 1/2        1     are nonnegative positive
semidefinite Grams but D − C has eigenvalues 1/2, −1/2. These are finite analytic
checks, not a covariance-order theorem for physical waves.
    The scalar, finite Gram, conditional active upper and half-quadratic entrywise de-
ductions have existing local Lean certificates at their exact hypotheses. Dual coercivity,
calculus, attainment, realization and the infimum/maximum argument above remain
analytic proofs. No new kernel fact or formalization of the physical application follows
merely from these certificates.

bounds and full calibration and whole-argument verification remain separate steps.

A fixed compact smooth unit profile, constant in time, belongs to O and has positive A

    The map (Uℓ g)s (x) = ℓd/2 gs (ℓx) is an H 1 isomorphism preserving unit mass and

                        A(Uℓ g) = ℓα A(g),         T (Uℓ g) = ℓ2 T (g).
Indeed the two densities contribute ℓ2d , dx contributes ℓ−d , and the pushforward of Γ

over g, ℓ leaves the class unchanged. For fixed A, T > 0, the derivative of Aℓα − ζT ℓ2
has the sign of αA − 2ζT ℓ2−α . Its global maximum occurs at ℓ = (αA/(2ζT ))1/(2−α)
and equals Dζ [A/T α/2 ]2/(2−α) . For A = 0 the supremum is zero as ℓ ↓ 0. Bounding
every ratio by Cocc gives one inequality in (41.3). Fixed ratios approaching its positive
supremum give the other by continuity of the positive power. Algebra gives (41.4) and
the penalty scaling. No optimizer or minimax interchange is assumed. This proves
Proposition 41.2.

At ℓ = c > 0 the profile attains c2 /6. These are full H 1 energy profiles; compact

Put x = 1 + a/2, y = (3 − a)/2, so x, y > 0 and x + y = 5/2. In Euler’s Gamma integral
the L2 (0, ∞) functions t(x−1)/2 e−t/2 and t(y−1)/2 e−t/2 have squared norms ΓE (x), ΓE (y)
and inner product ΓE (5/4). Cauchy–Schwarz, positivity, inversion and the increasing
square root prove the bound. At a = 1/2, x = y = 5/4, giving equality. Replacing a by
1 − a exchanges these two arguments, so only this ratio of bound formulas is symmetric,
not the moment constant or exponent. This proves Corollary 42.4.

                                             255
   This example retains the all-model theorem rather than replacing its scope. The
formal static exponent is 3/2 as a ↓ 0; no interchange of long-time and temporal-endpoint
limits is asserted. Jensen holds for the same canonical solution, while S1pos remains
auxiliary. The read-only Skorohod comparison at strict spatial Riesz exponent below
one does not cover this spatial-white endpoint. A separate white-time problem cannot
be substituted for 0 < a < 1. No new Skorohod result, source correction or certified
decimal enclosure is claimed.


43     Targets and verification status
Goal 43.1 (Upper bound at the correct scale). Under Hypothesis 1.1, prove
lim supt→∞ t−κ log Eu2 (t, x) < ∞, ideally with an explicit constant.

   Theorem 13.8 proves this upper bound for every admissible dimension and homoge-
neous covariance, independently of the later variational identification. Earlier explicit
bounds for 0 < α ≤ 1 remain available.

Goal 43.2 (Sharp second-moment constant). Under Hypothesis 1.1, derive a finite con-
stant C2 (Γ, α0 ) and prove, for every fixed x ∈ Rd ,

                            lim t−κ log Eu2 (t, x) = C2 (Γ, α0 ).
                            t→∞

A variational characterization must specify its full domain, covariance amplitude, ki-
netic coefficient and Gaussian action. Derive further evaluations and bounds from this
formula. For zero covariance, u(t, x) = 1 and C2 (0, α0 ) = 0.

    Theorem 40.6 proves this limit and specifies its complete positive-control formula.
Theorem 41.4, Corollary 41.5 and Theorem 42.3 derive bounds from that value. The
original proposed bounds with endpoints 2(4−α)/(3−α) L∗ and 2(4−α)/(3−α) L∗ remain com-
parisons with Conjecture 2.3. Matching those endpoints or closing the source’s varia-
tional gap is not required to identify the intrinsic value. No correction of the source’s
normalization or equality of its energy domains is adopted. The component integrations
have passed their scoped independent proof and application reviews. Whole-argument
and final independent cold reviews of the consolidated candidate, followed by the re-
maining project delivery checks, are still required.


44     Established steps and open questions
1. The mixed expansion and the first obstruction. Section 3 proves the complete
   nonnegative mixed expansion and an independent convergent solution series. It cor-
   rects the time-uniformity issue in the source’s intermediate estimate. The common-
   per-pairing majorant has exponent ρ > κ and cannot give the desired scale. This is a
   restriction on that majorant, not on the model or all uses of the source’s Theorem 3.1.


                                            256
2. Calibrations. Section 4 gives the finite white and Riesz coefficients. As α0 ↓ 0,
   the target exponent becomes (4 − α)/(3 − α), the static exponent. Proposition 2.4
   is unconditional; its numerical 2L∗ specialization requires the stated first-moment
   premise. Comparisons with the Skorohod model identify time exponents only.

3. The full upper scale. Section 5 gives explicit bounds when α ≤ 1. Section 13
   proves the full homogeneous-covariance upper scale by independent interval fields,
   local Gaussian-degree estimates and dyadic covariance domination. The latter proof
   does not assume an endpoint representation of the spatial covariance.

4. The intrinsic constant. Sections 19–40 construct the actual phase/source frame-
   work and prove matching original point-moment bounds. The upper score uses the
   original raw variance-half action. Finite Gram optimization and positive smoothing
   identify it with the full positive-control lower value. The auxiliary label-one score is
   not identified with an actual first-moment limit or Chen’s printed constants.

5. Computed bounds and the white example. Section 41 bounds the same con-
   stant using the full occupation class and a common spatial energy. Section 42 evalu-
   ates that energy for Γ = cδ0 and gives explicit moment bounds and the relative width
   of their formulas. It does not evaluate the intrinsic supremum numerically or assert
   equality at either bound.

6. Verification still to complete. Scoped reviews do not replace the fresh whole-
   argument skeptic and final independent cold read. The source first-moment normal-
   ization remains a qualified comparison question. Historical alternatives and failed
   routes above retain their original scope; they are not new premises of the intrinsic-
   limit proof.
